## A20. Executing Entity information

* **Deutsches Institut für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH:** Besides being the Accredited Entity (AE) for the programme, will serve as an Executing Entity (EE). In addition to the financial element (venia), GIZ will receive a grant from GIZ to implement the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes in both regions.

* **The Instituto Tecnológico de Estudios Superiores de Monterrey:** A secular and co-educational private university based in Mexico with campuses across Latin America. It is one of two Executing Entities that will receive a grant from GIZ to implement the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes in both regions.

* **One of three Executing Entities for the West Africa region is Impact Hub Abidjan (iHUB ABIDJAN):** A commercial pre-accelerator and accelerator (limited liability company) based in Abidjan, Côte d'Ivoire. It will receive a grant from GIZ to implement the technical assistance elements of the pre-acceleration programme as well as being the conceptual partner for the overall pre-acceleration programme in West Africa.

* **The second Executing Entity for the West Africa region is Investisseurs & Partenaires (IPED):** A commercial pre-accelerator and accelerator with offices throughout the region. IPED is an entity providing technical assistance to entrepreneurs, investors, and governments (TP). IPED will receive a grant from GIZ to implement: i) the financial element (venia) for the pre-acceleration programme in West Africa; ii) the technical assistance and financial element (repayable grants of the acceleration programme in West Africa.

* **The Stichting Climate Knowledge and Innovation Community International Foundation (Stichting Climate KIC International Foundation or, for brevity, Clima-KIC):** It will serve as the key Executing Entity for the CATALIST Initiative's venture development activities. The financial element (venia) of the pre-acceleration programme will finance climate impact assessment and gender climate entrepreneurship support to the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes in both regions.

# A.2 Executive Summary

## Climate Change Problem

1. Climate start-ups and other micro and small enterprises, collectively known as climate ventures, are well placed to make a difference in delivering climate action. They can, at limited risk to the economy, as a result of their innovative nature, be more flexible than large corporations in terms of scale, speed, impact pace and ambition into private-sector mitigation efforts. Climate ventures can provide solutions that are not yet available on the market but which could be developed with sufficient support such as improved products and services that are already available (incremental technologies).

2. In Latin America and West Africa, there is a significant opportunity to leap-frog emissions-intensive development pathways by supporting climate ventures in their early stages of development. The key is the provision of appropriate policies and regulatory frameworks. The context provided by post COVID-19 national recovery plans offers an opportunity for governments to provide the necessary policy and venture capital (VC) flows into both regions are rapidly rising, albeit from a low base (particularly in West Africa). In Latin America, there has been a significant increase in the availability of venture capital and, and there is a strong appetite among regionally active VC firms to scale-up funding. Young climate ventures can, if properly, play an important role in leap-frogging into more sustainable industries.

3. Climate ventures are currently being pushed out by the reasons of the decision sector on their limited technical expertise, limited technical support services and role models, and the lack of industry standard (widely accepted), best practice tools and frameworks – and the mis-match between the needs of climate ventures and the available financial instruments to scale-up and finance.

## Proposed Interventions

### The CATALYT!5T (Concerted Action To Accelerate Local I:5T technologies) Initiative will establish and implement a regional technical assistance and investment grant platforms that build a portfolio of early-stage climate ventures in Latin America and West Africa. The objective of each regional CATALYT!5T will be to support the development of a pipeline of innovative, low-carbon, high-impact, high-potential climate impact and business growth potential. Each regional CATALYT!5T will provide support for (i) cli-mate venture identification, assessment, selection, incubation and support organization (ESOs), and (ii) VC firms and other venture investors.

### At the core of each regional CATALYT!5T will be an acceleration programme. Each acceleration pro-gramme will aim to help climate ventures to develop or scale-up their most viable products or services (MVPs) and ensure investability by VCAs. Specifically, the acceleration programmes will enable climate ventures to: (i) identify and validate MVPs; (ii) conduct market surveys, product testing or product enhancement; (iii) enhance the success of their products and services through intellectual property protection, marketing, branding, sales and distribution channels etc.; and (iv) maximise the climate impact of their products and services through robust, internationally-recognised methodologies and standards. The acceleration programme will also provide support for climate adaptation co-benefits. This support will take the form of technical assistance and financial assistance to enable climate ventures to develop or scale-up their MVPs.

### Less mature, pre-commercial climate ventures will receive support to develop minimum viable products or services through two regional pre-acceleration programmes, encompassing tech-nical assistance and financial assistance in the form of a grant to each climate venture up to EUR 15,000 to cover pre-accelerated costs.

### Accompanying this support to climate ventures will be (i) community-building and identity activities that provide public recognition for climate ventures; (ii) capacity building and training for regional groups in entrepreneurship; and (iii) technical assistance for the broader venture ecosystem in both regions. The CATALYT!5T initiative will also provide a platform for dialogue between climate ventures in order to build and sustain climate businesses. With CATALYT!5T initiative support, regional ESOs and venture investors will be enabled to assess and enhance ventures’ climate potential (emission reduction

**8. By contributing to the successful development and growth of climate ventures, the CATALIST Initiative will unlock their climate mitigation benefits, resulting in quantifiable GHG emissions reductions that are aligned with the GCF’s mission.**

This will be achieved through the sale of low-emission products/services by the 60 climate ventures supported by the CATALIST Initiative's two regional acceleration programmes. The CATALIST Initiative will provide these ventures with the technical, financial, and networking support needed to build and shape them into robust, high-potential investment opportunities that can be sold on the market. These sales are expected to occur in the private sector, outside of GCF programme boundary – and, in the majority of cases, is expected to occur after the end of the 20-year life span of the CATALIST Initiative. This will ensure (i) a focus on market-driven climate mitigation while (ii) establishing a clear exit strategy for the GCF and (iii) making minimal demands on GCF resources.

**10. The CATALIST Initiative is expected to reduce 58 MCO2e of greenhouse gas emissions during its 20-year lifespan, of which MCO2e will be directly attributable to the GCF. The GCF mitigation cost will be Euro 7.1/MCO2e (S$9.4 /MCO2e).**

# B.1 Climate context

## B.1.1 Strategic context

### Climate Innovation and Climate Ventures

1. Limiting global warming to 1.5°C with no or limited overshoot, addressing already locked-in climate change impacts, and adapting to the unavoidable effects of climate change will require significant behavioral change by individuals and societies. Additionally, social, business and technology innovations will be necessary in order to reduce urban emissions (including transport and buildings) and industrial system terms at unprecedented scale.
2. Transformative climate technology innovations are technologies and related business practices that enable a large-scale reduction or elimination of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. The concept of "climate innovation" is purposely broad in order to encompass the wealth of relevant technology innovations, including those that reduce GHG emissions, increase energy efficiency, and/or mitigate associated with energy generation and consumption, the built environment, mobility, light and heavy industry, and food production and consumption. Climate innovation also includes technologies for carbon reduction and improving reporting, monitoring, and verification (MRV).
3. With few exceptions, only industrialized countries can finance expensive basic research and development of climate innovations. However, developing countries can produce future GHG emission-reducing technologies that can promote the development of transformative home-grown solutions which might require less cost and time to implement than those developed in other countries.
4. Climate ventures are micro or small enterprises that apply climate innovation. For the purposes of the CATALYST Initiative, the definition of climate venture encompasses start-ups and growth companies that:
    - Satisfy the IFC Definition of a micro or small enterprise (MSE) according to the table below; and
    - Offer innovative solutions in one or more of the following areas: energy efficiency, renewable energy, and/or carbon reduction (i.e. the reduction or avoidance of greenhouse gas emissions, or the sequestration of carbon).
5. Within the climate ventures category:
    * Start-ups are early-stage companies that are developing or already have a minimum viable product (MVP), but do not have significant revenues and/or profits yet.
    * Growth companies are established enterprises with revenue and/or profit potential and possibly profits, but are unable to grow to their full potential for instance, by making large investments due to lack of technical capacity, access to finance, or other constraints.

6. Climate ventures can target business models and/or technological solutions that would be beneficial to consumers and the global environment but which have not been introduced due to market failure, such

### We need faster, bolder innovation in digital tech, and start-up innovation can help deliver this. The start-up venture ecosystem is geared up to deliver fast-growing, highly scalable companies with a strong focus on the market. These are often based on new technologies, but they may also be the combination of human capital with novel technologies, often wrapped in business models which are more innovative than those of established players. Start-ups are often more agile and better at tackling the climate crisis, even if activity today is comparatively modest. They often present the embodiment of a new technology or business model that can help to solve the problem. They have flex- ibility plus ability with a talent demand signal in the market. The incentives for founders and the venture ecosystem are aligned to deliver fast-growing companies with a strong focus on the market, potentially large markets and high returns. The capital fuels the talent to create new products and services that can help solve climate change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE B

The purpose of the CATALYST initiative is to use the term "pre-accelerator" to refer to ideational and pre-seed stage support to smaller ventures that have not yet developed a prototype service or product, while the term "accelerator" refers to a more structured program for ventures with a working prototype or service that is ready to be commercialized or scaled up beyond the pilot market. They are estimated to be crucial in bridging the gap between early-stage and later-stage venture funding. However, fewer than 20 focus on climate ventures and just 25 are located in developing countries.

## Venture Financing

### 21. The crucial role that pre-accelerators / accelerators can play in supporting ventures is widely recognized, including by the World Bank (2018) and the Global Innovation Fund (GIF). These programs help climate inventors into technologies that meet social needs. They act as local intermediary institutions, strength-ening the ecosystem for innovation and entrepreneurship. Pre-accelerators / accelerators can also facilitate linkages between entrepreneurs, other innovators action and potential markets of suppliers and buyers. The role of pre-accelerators / accelerators is to provide a structured environment that enables entrepreneurs to connect with sources of finance, providing them with the means to innovate. Mission Innovation (2018) highlights the importance of these programs in helping climate inventors become the most important stakeholders for a solution agenda when more than incremental improvements in existing systems are possible and needed."

### 22. In mature venture ecosystems in Europe and North America, climate innovators and entrepreneurs meet their funding needs in the pre-seed and seed stages with: (a) their own personal assets; (b) investments from family or friends; (c) angel investors; (d) venture capital firms; and (e) public sector funds. If they have little or no valuable collateral in their early stages, they usually do not qualify for loan instruments. The vast majority of climate innovators and entrepreneurs are unable to access these sources of funding.

### 23. VC funding, generally in the form of equity, is typically invested into a venture at the end of this stage, as it requires a minimum level of traction or proof of concept. This is often referred to as Series A funding. Series A funding normally materializes when the potential of the product or service is recognized in the market and requires investment for further development. The most common sources of Series A funding are angel investors, seed funds, and advertising space on media. The Series B funding round builds on the success of a Stage A model, with the aim to scale up and reach more customers. This stage also aims at raising capital to allow the venture to scale up and reach maturity. This investment may also aim to broaden geographical reach, with the prod-uct or service being sold in new markets. Series C funding is typically provided by institutional investors for sales campaigns. This is the phase where initial traction are fostered. Series C funding is typically provided by institutional investors for sales campaigns. This is the phase where initial traction are fostered.

### 24. In mature venture ecosystems, angel investors and venture capital firms provide the most common sources of funding to climate innovators and entrepreneurs in their early stages. However, these sources of funding diversify into new providers.

### B.2 Baseline Scenario
**Global Context**

- Annual VC investment has increased more than five-fold in the past 10 years, rising to US$ 284 billion in 2020.
- VC investment is focused primarily on the technology sector with investments in cloud computing, mobile applications, and software development.
- The most significant sectors for venture capital are biotechnology, clean tech, and cleantech financing. Other significant investment sectors include digital banking ("fintech"), healthcare, media and consumer products, and renewable energy.
- In 2013, the United States accounted for just 6% of total VC investment, but it has grown over 375% in absolute terms since 2013, equating to $84 billion.

### Climate-related Investment
Climate-related investment is widely considered to be in its second phase of development, dubbed "Clean-tech 2.0." Clean-tech 1.0 constituted a boom in investment in the "Classic tech" sector between 2006 and 2013.
- The financial crash of 2008 unexpectedly cheapened natural gas prices and the ascendency of China's solar PV manufacturing, which led to a significant drop in venture capital funding for cleantech. In response, many venture capitalists, they lost roughly half of it, rendering clean energy investing unattractive for the early

part of the US$25 billion in Qtrunch 2 represents a resurgence in climate-related investment, as well as a reaction to the lessons learned from the first wave – notably, through the adoption of broader investment strategies that are more inclusive and less focused on the most advanced markets and sectors. There is no definitive list; there are now estimated to be at least 28 cleantech unicorns**, spanning across all regions and stages of development.

It is, however, notable that of the (approximately) 910 unicorn ventures worldwide, fewer than 3% are focused on climate. Furthermore, 95% of the US$60 billion invested in cleantech in the period 2013-2018 was directed towards early-stage deals, with only a small proportion of this investment directed to later-stage ventures.

This is despite the fact that Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) has more than its fair share of climate unicorns (in particular) in many developing countries struggle to access finance. Among emerging markets, LAC is home to 10% of the world’s population and 8% of the world’s GHG emissions – attracted just US$2 million of climate VC funding between 2013-2018. In contrast, Europe and Central Asia are home to over 660 million people and an 8% share of the world’s GHG emissions – attracted just USD $500 million in climate VC investment during this period. Furthermore, most climate investment is directed towards large deals of over US$ 100 million, not at small, early-stage ventures.

## Latin America

Latin America has the potential to meet a major share of global power demand with renewable energy by 2050. However, Mexico and many Central American states still rely heavily on fossil fuels, and others such as Brazil are at risk of seeing renewable shares slip as climate change makes hydroelectric generation less reliable.

### VC Investment

30. VC investment in Latin America reached a record of US $15 billion across more than 650 deals in 2020. The region also saw its largest ever VC deal, the US$4.8 billion acquisition of VivaAerobus by LATAM Airlines Group in April 2020. This figure is skewed by some very large deals, such as a US $13.6 billion investment into MercadoLibre and a US $5.7 billion investment into Grupo FEMSA. Nonetheless impressive. However, while the trend is generally positive, VC investment in Latin America has been unevenly distributed.

* **Country:** VC investment has almost exclusively targeted Brazil, Mexico, Colombia, Chile, and Argentina.
* **Sector:** The largest share of VC investment in the region flows to sectors with close links to climate change mitigation. In 2019, fintech attracted 35% of all VC flowing into the region, followed by e-commerce (24%), renewable energy (18%), and digital health (17%). Fintech has focused on sectors that offer rapid growth on the back of digital trends, such as the widespread use of mobile banking. Renewable energy received only a 3% share of all VC in Latin America, while digital health received only 3% of capital (US$15 billion) in 2019.

### Stage Investment

Stage investment in Latin America occurs at the early and late stages, while intermediate, seed stage investments play a very minor role and only in the renewable energy sector. In 2019, 87% of all VC deals in Latin America were at later stages including advanced valuations; VC firms tend to invest post-seed; nonetheless, it emphasizes the challenge that seed stage ventures face in attracting financing.

## West Africa

31. A full baseline description is provided in the West African feasibility study (Annex 2).

### GHG Emissions from West Africa

32. GHG emissions from West Africa represent 2% of global emissions. *Between 1990 and 2014, regional GHG emissions grew by 17%, slower than the world average growth of 45%. Land-use change and forestry accounted for 68% of total emissions in 2014 (up from 38% in 1990), with agriculture contributing 22% (and waste at 11%). Approximately 70% of West Africa's primary energy supply comes from biofuels, wood and charcoal. The region is also a major producer of oil and gas, which account for 45% of the continent, with an estimated 22% of its working age population starting businesses, and over 10 million

## West Africa suffers from a clear lack of VC capital in absolute and relative terms – even compared with other regions in Sub-Saharan Africa. The region is also lagging behind in terms of the number of start-ups that are at the growth stage, which is what many consider as the Valley of Death, a period where many firms fail to secure funding. This is partly due to the lack of venture capital and private equity financing, specifically venture funding, that will help them take their businesses to scale.* A comprehensive review by the World Bank (2019) found that only 3% of all VC investments in Africa are directed at early-stage venture startups across the whole continent but, at the same time, indicates a clear underweighting of the Foundation’s portfolio countries.


### Country: The picture for the continent as a whole is encouraging. 347 African ventures raised equity capital in 2020 ($159 million funding rounds), up (44% in deal count) from 234 ventures and $202 millions ($86 million in deal count) in 2019. The total amount of equity capital raised was approximately US$ 1.4 billion in 2020, down from US$ 1.7 billion in 2019. In terms of the number of deals, the total fell by 35% to 682 deals in 2020; the amount raised increased in 2019 ($US 277 million). The drop in average deal size in 2020 vs. 2019 likely reflects the fact that most of these rounds were small seed rounds, which are more common than larger rounds. VC funding in 2020 went to just 4 countries: Nigeria, Kenya, Egypt and South Africa. Ghana followed with 3 deals, while Uganda had two. The top five recipients of VC investment in 2020 were Nigeria (US$ 158 million), Kenya ($97 million), Egypt ($65 million), South Africa ($55 million) and Ghana ($45 million). Francophone West Africa, Senegal and Côte d’Ivoire were the biggest recipients of VC investment, but their share of total equity funding in 2020 was only 13% (US$ 196 million), compared to 28.7% behind (US$ 3.6 billion) and 24.1%, respectively, in 2020; and Burkina Faso, Niger, Guinea and Mali received no VC funding at all.


### Sector: sectors with significant potential to address climate change represented less than 25% of VC investment in Africa in 2020. These sectors are agritech, which attracted US$179 million of VC funding (13% of total equity funding), and cleantech, which received US$ 148 million (10%) of the total.* While the presence of agritech and off-grid power in the top five recipients of VC investment is not surprising, it is interesting to note that none of the investments in these two sectors occurred in Francophone West Africa. The vast majority of funding for agritech went into Nigeria ($97 million), followed by Kenya ($35 million). Cleantech investments were roughly split between Nigeria and Kenya, with a very small portion going to South Africa (US$ 10 million) and Egypt (US$ 2 million). Francophone West Africa received no VC funding in 2020; only 14% of total VC equity funding in Africa went to female-funded ventures.


### Stage of venture development: While Francophone West Africa-specific data are not available, one can assume that the vast majority of investments were at early stages. The term “seed” (used in Partenix’s terminology) and Series A deals, 228 late seed stage deals were recorded in 2020, up from just 53 in 2019. This represents a 436% increase in the number of late-stage deals in 2020, roughly four times the 2015 figure. In 2020, the average amount of equity raised was US$ 8 million per deal (up from $US 2.7 million in 2019). The average deal size has increased by more than a year average but at a drop from 2019 levels (likely due to risk aversion caused by the pandemic). The presence of late-stage deals is encouraging, as it shows that the region’s venture ecosystem is maturing and that investments are being made in later stages. However, these investments need to be rigorously selected and adequately prepared; early-stage VC firms that already have experience elsewhere in Africa may be drawn to investing in West Africa.


* Tony Blair Institute for Global Change (2022), Closing the Funding Gap for Africa Tech Startups: https://www.toniablair.org/policy/press-releases/closing-the-funding-gap-for-africa-tech-startups
* World Bank (9 May 2019), Francophone West Africa Has the Most Promising Tech Start-ups and They Are Looking for Investors: https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/feature/2019/05/09/francophone-west-africa-has-the-most-promising-tech-startups-and-they-are-looking-for-investors
* Partenix (2021), 2020 Africa Tech Venture Capital Report: https://www.startupgrind.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/2020-africa-tech-venture-capital-report-final.pdf
* Partenix (2021), 2020 Africa Tech Venture Capital Report: https://www.startupgrind.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/2020-africa-tech-venture-capital-report-French.pdf

B.1.3 Baseline Policies and Strategies

Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), UNFCCC Assessments and Climate Policies

34. All countries in Latin America and West Africa have ratified the Paris Agreement. An overview of Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) targets and UNFCCC reports and strategies, such as National Communication (NC) and Biennial Update Report (BUR), can be found in Annexes 2a and 2b. In addition to national climate policies, plans and strategies can be found in Annexes 2b and 2c. All countries in both regions have developed or are developing NDCs. However, the level of ambition is not always clear, and their ability to ensure the effective implementation of those instruments is, in many cases, limited.[^1]

Policies and Strategies: Latin America

35. A detailed review of the Latin American policy environment is provided in Annex 2a. In brief, venture promotion policies have been introduced by several countries including Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Uruguay and Venezuela. Some countries such as Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Mexico, Panama, Peru and Uruguay have also introduced regulations to support the development of SMEs in their respective countries.

- Strengthening institutional frameworks for supporting ventures – notably in Mexico, which created venture capital funds (FONAVIT) in 2013;[**^2**] Chile, which created a division within CORFO[^3^] to develop ventures. The region has also seen the creation of several public-private partnerships such as the Fondo de Inversión en Colombia, Nacional Financiera S.C. (INFRAFIN), Infórica and Corporación Financiera De Desarrollo.

- Investing in transforming mind-sets and promoting a culture of entrepreneurship. Transforming mind-sets is a critical challenge for Latin America where most people are employed by the state or society and relatively few see entrepreneurs as key contributors to national development. In Mexico, this has been addressed through the creation of the National Commission on Rising awareness of the crucial role that entrepreneurship plays in the country's development.

- Modernizing institutions and infrastructure. This includes improving access to finance, infrastructure, finance and services with access to contacts and networks – such as the role acted-out by CORFO as a national pre-accelerator and accelerator.[**^2**]

Policies and Strategies: West Africa

36. A detailed review of the West African policy environment is provided in Annex 2c. Africa, as a whole has the highest rate of entrepreneurship in the world, with ADB reporting that 22% of Africa's working-age population are self-employed.[**^4**] However, there have been few efforts to promote and support entrepreneurship and, in some cases, technological innovation. However, many public agencies have been established or are being established across West Africa.

- Burkina Faso, for example, ranks fifth in its income group in the Global Innovation Index 2021 report.[**^5**] The country has also introduced a number of policies to support entrepreneurship and SMEs including the Faso 151[^6^], with relatively high scores for starting a business, protecting minority investors, trading across borders and access to finance. In addition, the government has established the Agency for Financing and Promotion of SMEs (AFPM). Under the Ministry of Economy and Finance, AFPM is a national fund and a technical assistance provider for ventures/ SMEs.[**^7**]

B.2.1 Problem Statement

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 17

## B

40. The opportunity: There is a significant opportunity in both regions to help firms increasingly embrace emissions-intensive development pathways and, instead, transition to low-emissions technologies and practices. Government policies and regulatory frameworks. The content provided by post-COVID recovery plans represents a timely policy opportunity for governments to support the scaling up of climate innovation.

41. The problem: Governments are struggling with large deficits, partly driven by large public expenditures incurred in response to COVID-19. As such, they have limited capacity to provide financial incentives and the private sector needs to play a prominent role, but is currently prevented from doing so by the nascent state of the climate innovation ecosystem in both regions. The lack of a well-developed market for high-quality climate ventures and the lack of "industry standard" (widely accepted) best practice tools and frameworks – and the mismatch between the current capacity of climate investors and those required to successfully access VC finance.

The solution: The overarching objective of the CATALYT initiative is to build a pipeline of 60 high-potential technology-based climate ventures across Latin America and West Africa, characterized by strong market demand (30 in Latin America and 30 in West Africa) and 120 high-quality pre-seed climate ventures (80 in Latin America and 60 in West Africa), characterized by strong market demand. The CATALYT initiative will help these ventures to cross the "Valley of Death", navigate the stringent VC due diligence process and successfully attract VC funding, while also providing them with the necessary support to scale up their operations and compete with the opportunity to thrive in their respective markets. By contributing to the successful development of climate innovation ecosystems in both regions, CATALYT will help to achieve GCF's 2030 target for cost-attainable GHG emissions reductions that are aligned with GCF mitigation results areas and Fund remit items.

## B.2.2 Barrier Analysis

41. There is considerable evidence of emerging VC interest and nascent climate innovation in Latin American and West African markets, accompanied by improving ( albeit sparse and inconsistent) government policies and regulatory frameworks. However, the development of these ecosystems has been hampered by a range of barriers that serve to constrain or prevent VC investment in climate innovation in both regions – in turn, limiting their ability to scale up and compete with other ventures.

3. The relative importance of these barriers, and their implications, varies between the regions and be- tween the sectors. These barriers are described below (Annex 1) for Latin America and (Annex 2) for West Africa and the West Africa flexibility study (Annex 2). These barriers specifically apply to investment in climate innovation ventures in both regions; however, other barriers related to market access and foreign exchange risks – are not detailed here but are reflected in the CATALYT initiative design.

| Barrier cluster | Barrier | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Complex sourcing of high-quality climate ventures | 1.1 Growing interest of entrepreneurs and investors in climate change, effects, and climate impact, but limited understanding of entrepreneurs on climate-related government objectives and limited awareness of VC opportunities | Limited understanding of entrepreneurs on climate change causes, effects, and climate impact, but limited awareness of VC opportunities |
|  |  | Limited knowledge of entrepreneurs on climate-related government objectives and limited awareness of VC opportunities |
|  | 1.2 Perceived complexity among entrepreneurs and investors, of climate so- lutions and related business opportunities | Perceived complexity among entrepreneurs and investors, of climate solutions and related business opportunities |
|  |  | Even among investment investors (VCs) and other investors with an explicit interest in climate innovation, there is a lack of technical capacity to appraise and support climate ventures. |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

## Barrier Cluster 1: Complex sources of high-quality climate ventures in Latin America and West Africa (Barrier 1.1)

### Barrier 1.1: Growing interest of entrepreneurs and investors in achieving climate impact, but limited understanding of cascades, trade-offs and related business opportunities

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 19

## B

### Key Takeaways:
- Entrepreneurs are missing opportunities to attract VC funding and effectively monetize climate change solutions.
- Many entrepreneurs struggle more than they should to source ventures offering climate benefits (since these benefits are not being recognized by investors).
- The mismatch between demand (availability of VC finance for climate ventures) and supply (the number and quality of climate venture ideas) is significant, with the latter being considerably greater than it likely reflects.
- Artificially supported market activity.

### Further Insights:
- There's widespread perception shared by both entrepreneurs and investors in both regions that climate change solutions are not scalable or profitable due to a lack of skilled labor to implement this.
- The perception of complexity stems largely from additional technical requirements associated with climate change solutions, which can be challenging for entrepreneurs to grapple with—such as design and marketing, manufacturing, finance, etc., as well as the mismatch between their business models and market needs (e.g. low-cost, high-volume products).
- Entrepreneurs may not be familiar with—and thus underutilize—some of these sources of support.
- The diverse range of sectors and technologies that offer potential climate mitigation benefits is often overlooked by entrepreneurs who are focused on a narrow set of solutions.
- Expertise in various areas is vital for ventures developing prototypes, demonstrating proof of concept and finesse business models. This expertise is often lacking across all (or at least) most sectors and deep technical expertise in a small handful where it is critical to the solution in an under-served sector may struggle to attract the support they need.
- The perception of high upfront capital requirements stems from a widespread misunderstanding of climate change solutions, which are often low-cost or no-cost interventions (e.g. wind farms or cutting-edge manufacturing plants).
- The examples of Tesla and turtles as aligned with their mental models of high-growth investment opportunities compared to other sectors.

### Conclusion:
- It is certainly true that some climate technologies have relatively high upfront costs (e.g. recycling infrastructure)—even if they ultimately deliver higher value profiles, richer cash flow over time.
- These costs may be prohibitive for entrepreneurs in developing countries who lack access to capital or are not yet able to build an attractive brand.
- An early stage climate venture is unlikely to be able to raise sufficient funds from investors to pay back upfront costs and still have enough left over to develop the technology, pay for production, logistics, or the costs of skilled labor.
- Another example is Sanergy in Kenya, a venture that provides affordable, no-cost sanitation services to low-income communities. The company uses biodegradable bags and sells them at a profit, using the proceeds to fund its operations.
- Entrepreneurs in developing countries may need to consider how they can adapt their business models to fit into existing value chains or markets, rather than trying to build entirely new ones.

### Barrier 1.2: Limited venture-building support infrastructure for entrepreneurs preparing climate ventures for VC funding.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 20

B

## 49. The widespread market informality makes it difficult to access support and finance from the formal sector.

If an enterprise is not officially established, it typically lacks the appropriate documentation to receive VC funding or other forms of financing. This is particularly true for small-scale enterprises that are often informal or micro-finance. It also means that many promising ventures under the radar of VC firms, at least until they have reached a certain level of maturity and visibility, may be overlooked by VCs as potential venture sourcing is, at best, patchy with most mitigating outcomes as a result, and that VCs have to rely on their own networks or informal sources for information. This can make the process too slow and hence too rigid, to accommodate changes that improve their attractiveness to VC firms.

## 50. Pre-accelerators and accelerators provide a vital element of the venture support ecosystem, delivering opportunities for training, networking, collaboration, co-founding and mentoring. Despite a growing number of pre-accelerator and accelerator programs in developing countries, they are still largely homogeneous. In Mexico, for example, only 22% of enterprises have received support from a pre-accelerator or accelerator program. The lack of diversity is also reflected by the fact that most of these programs are run by foreign actors. Although AfricInvest and similar initiatives do not offer any climate-focused capacity building, and the majority of them are focused on early-stage ventures, they still provide an important form of "traditional" business support to address the specific needs of climate ventures is very constrained.

## 51. In addition, there are large differences in the maturity of the markets within both regions. In Latin America, Brazil has a more mature market than Mexico and Colombia. The latter two countries have been drawn between more mature coastal economies and those of the Sierra Nevada. Combined with the limited access to finance, this makes it difficult for entrepreneurs to scale their businesses. A one-size-fits-all approach to venture support is unlikely to be effective – and, indeed, counterproductive. One-to-one, tailored support that takes into account the needs of a greater extent than both regions pre-accelerators and accelerators are accustomed to providing.

### 1.3.1. Lack of pre-seed / seed funding available to climate entrepreneurs

## 52. Accessing finance is an obstacle for ventures generally and climate ventures specifically. A majority of stakeholders in the ecosystem, including the Prime Minister of Côte d'Ivoire acknowledge that this is a major barrier. There are gaps in the ecosystem, notably a lack of technical and financial support to help entrepreneurs scale their businesses. In addition, while most climate enterprises receive limited financial support; grants typically extend up to approximately US$10,000-20,000. But the main financing gap is at the pre-seed/seed stage where there are virtually no options for funding. This creates frustration among entrepreneurs, particularly in West Africa where financing opportunities are even more limited than elsewhere. In many cases, climate enterprises are forced to rely on their own savings or those by the entrepreneur themselves or by their friends and families, with angel investors playing an additional role as a source of finance. However, this is not always possible given that the cost of living in Africa costs are ramping up due to revenue remains limited. VC finance for climate ventures is certainly increasing at high rates but it is still very early days. In addition, many climate enterprises are small and their potential ventures are simply unable to advance to the (gold) seed stage where Series A VC financing becomes available.

## 53. Alternative funding opportunities are very limited. Banks and other traditional lenders, even those with a focus on microfinance, are not always willing or able to provide finance for climate enterprises. Climate ventures risk, even further other barriers to accessing bank finance – large ticket sizes, collateral requirements, lack of credit history, and the fact that many climate entrepreneurs are women who face compounded challenges in securing finance. Women, who are, in some countries / cultures, traditionally not official owners of land or assets, face additional hurdles when it comes to accessing finance from banks. This is further compounded by high interest rates and short durations (typically 6-12 months). As a result, the specific resource and support needs of ventures as they enter the seed stage are too large to benefit from easy pre-seed grants and too small for the minimal thresholds of VCs, including most impact investors. The general lack of

### Barrier 2:1 Lack of methods and data

**Issue:** An issue faced by most venture initiatives is access to widely accepted methodologies to measure climate impact.

- **Reasons:**
    - It makes it difficult to evaluate climate impact ex ante and to compare the potential climate impacts of different ventures.
    - This, in turn, means that investment may flow to the wrong ventures—those on the periphery of climate change.
    - It is inefficient. Ventures, accelerators, or other entrepreneur support organisations (ESOs) are unlikely to be able to conduct a full and rigorous assessment of the venture’s climate impact. These tools may not be robust or credible; they are unlikely to be consistent with each other.

**Impact:** The lack of methods and data limits the ability of investors to make informed decisions, especially international development organisations or the financial (pension funds, foundations, etc.) that typically invest in climate change. This is because it is difficult to assess the climate impact of ventures, cumbersome and more time-consuming than it needs to be.

**Barriers:** The lack of methods and data limits entrepreneurs to claim climate impact and, to the extent that this climate impact leads to poor VC funding decisions, limits the ability of investors to make informed decisions.

5. Investors in climate-related ventures require verifiable information on the climate impact of their investments prior to allocating scarce funds to them. While methodologies to measure the climate mitigation potential of a venture are available (e.g., MRV frameworks), these methods are often overly opaque (built on untested or unvalidated assumptions); they tend to focus on Scope 1 and 2 emissions, which are easier to quantify but not necessarily the most impactful.

6. The problem of impact measurement, including robust MRV frameworks, is compounded when the other uncertainty associated with the success or failure of ventures is considered. Managers of the baseline projects (e.g., in energy efficiency) have a much better understanding of their business and more optimistic assumptions about survival rates (and hence the realisation of GHG reduction outcomes). In contrast, managers of climate change-focused ventures are less certain about the climate impact as they gain market share: for climate ventures, the time to reach Series A funding is typically longer than that seen in the seed stage.

**Barrier 2:2 Limited capacity**

7. Ventures require entrepreneurs to be multi-skilled—having an understanding of technology, business processes, sales and marketing, HR and other functions—in order to grow their business successfully. Climate change-focused ventures are often more complex because they need to integrate climate science, and the methodologies and restrictions of climate finance as a sub-set of green finance. This adds complexity that is not always present in traditional sectors such as agriculture and especially in Africa, where technical skills and knowledge networks are weaker.

8. Many of the entrepreneurs surveyed during preparation of the CATALYST Initiative were engaged in relatively mature industries (e.g., energy efficiency). These ventures presented strong climate awareness, considerable body of successful investments worldwide), but also because donor agencies are more likely to invest in these sectors. However, it is important to note that climate change is not associated with them in both regions. However, even in these relatively well-addressed sectors, climate-specific knowledge and skills are often lacking. For example, “the number of entrepreneurs with a climate domain expertise such as electrical engineering” was found to be extremely limited. Climate

## B.2.3 Theory of Change

The CATALIST Initiative theory of change is presented in Figure 5. The logic can be stated as follows:

- If inclusive and diverse local communities of climate entrepreneurs in Latin America and West Africa generate commercially viable climate solutions, and if the scale and impact of these ideas are enhanced, then a paradigm shift will be achieved that creates a clear pathway for scaling up climate solutions from Latin America and West Africa. Because local climate technologies and solutions successfully commercialize and/or climate mitigation. Ecosystem actors with the tools to accurately assess the potential of climate solutions can be enabled to use these tools and enabling them to disseminate appropriate tool-use across the entire ecosystem.

The theory of change illustrates the causal progression from underlying drivers, knowledge, awareness, financial and tool availability – that prevail in the baseline and which currently hinder such communities from generating commercially viable climate solutions. The CATALIST Initiative will support organizations (ESCs) - pre-accelerators, accelerators, government venture support agencies, etc., and provide them with technical assistance to enable them to generate innovative climate business ideas and if these can be commercialized and achieve rapid diffusion.

To ensure this is the case, targeted activities are required to overcome the identified barriers: knowledge, awareness, financial and tool availability – that prevail in the baseline and which currently hinder such communities from generating commercially viable climate solutions. The CATALIST Initiative will support organizations (ESCs) - pre-accelerators, accelerators, government venture support agencies, etc., and provide them with technical assistance to enable them to generate innovative climate business ideas and if these can be commercialized and achieve rapid diffusion.

Technical assistance in the context of the CATALIST Initiative refers to training, capacity building, coaching, mentoring, and other forms of support provided by ESCs. The CATALIST Initiative is funded and financed by the CATALIST Initiative. Financial assistance in the context of the CATALIST Initiative will be provided to eligible entrepreneurs in Latin America and West Africa (and repeatable grants averaging Euro 100,000 per grantee) for the development of their climate solutions, so that they can attract venture capital and other financing that allows them to achieve market success.

The use of these grants and repayable grants is subject to terms outlined in individual grant agreements signed by each venture and the relevant Executive Entity.

## B.3. Project/programme description

B.3.1 CATALIST™ Initiative Description

Overview
The CATALIST (Consortium Action to Accelerate Local IS™ Technologies) initiative will establish and improve regional climate venture capital platforms that build a portfolio of early-stage climate ventures in Latin America and West Africa. The objective of each regional CATALIST™ is to engage with investors, entrepreneurs and other stakeholders to create an enabling environment for climate mitigation and business growth potential. Each regional CATALIST™ will provide support for (i) climate venture accelerators and other entrepreneur support organisations (ESOs); and (ii) VC firms and other investors.

65. The design of the CATALIST™ Initiative directly addresses the barriers identified in Section B.2.2.


| Barrier | Barrier Removal by the CATALIST™ Initiative |
| --- | --- |
| 1.1 Growing interest of entrepreneurs and investors in achieving climate mitigation and business growth potential, but a lack of clarity on how to scale up their climate-based businesses | Nurturing nascent climate innovation ecosystems in Latin America (Sub-Activity 1.2.1) and West Africa to ensure its mutually supportive roles: (i) the technical underpinning of climate innovations; (ii) the creation of an enabling environment for climate venture investors and climate ventures, thereby enabling entrepreneurs and investors to scale up their climate-based businesses; and (iii) the development of a robust ecosystem of VC firms and other investors. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 26

Capacity building of seed-stage ventures to enable them to scale-up and commercialize their minimum viable climate products/services and ensure investability by ESG investors (in Latin America, West Africa).

A comprehensive and practical guidebook for Executing Entities, ESQs, venture investors and climate ventures on how to integrate gender equity into their operations, be it through the development of gender-sensitive business models, or through awareness-raising, training and ad hoc support on ESG issues for climate ventures.

The CATALIST® Initiative is organized across 5 components:

Component 1 encompasses CATALIST® in Latin America.
Component 2 encompasses the CATALIST® in West Africa.
As a result of the CATALIST® initiative, there will be a portfolio of special advisory and capacity-building services to venturers. Executing Entities, ESQs and investors involved in Components 1 and 2. Component 3 is focused on the development of regional pre-acceleration programmes.
Component 4 focuses on the development of regional pre-acceleration programmes.
Component 5 encompasses CATALIST® Initiative monitoring, reporting and evaluation activities.

## Activity 6.1.1 - Latin America and Activity 2.3.1 - West Africa

Each regional acceleration programme focuses on providing support to seed-stage climate ventures in order to enable them to scale-up and commercialize their minimum viable climate products/services through a regional pre-acceleration programme.
The acceleration programmes will enable climate ventures to:
(i) further develop their business models (e.g., by incorporating gender equity, social inclusion or other forms of impact enhancement);
(ii) enhance the success of their products and services through market demonstration, development and/or validation;
(iii) increase their awareness about ESG issues and how they can be integrated into their products and services through robust, internationally-recognized climate change mitigation assessment tools (e.g., VCA, CCI, etc.); and
(iv) take the form of Technical Assistance and Financial Assistance in the form of a repayable grant to each climate venture of up to EUR 15,000 to cover costs associated with the acceleration programme.

## Activity 6.2 - Latin America

To promote the regional pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes, inspire entrepreneurs' interest in climate entrepreneurship and increase their awareness about ESG issues, some of them specifically targeting women and other under-represented groups in entrepreneurship.
In addition, trans-regional advisory, capacity and knowledge support (TRACKS) Activity 1.1.1 - Latin America and Activity 2.3.1 - West Africa will provide technical assistance to climate ventures with expertise in climate impact assessment, transformational climate mitigation solutions; women's empowerment and/or social inclusion. The TRACKS initiative will also provide training for climate entrepreneurs who augment their current business and finance skill sets and enable them, for example, to assess venture funding needs and identify appropriate investors. This will help climate ventures move from seed-stage ventures towards more diverse and transformational climate solutions, to promote women's participation in climate entrepreneurship, and to understand and apply emerging ESG frameworks in the VC industry.

| 73. The CATALYS T initiative will be sector- and technology-agnostic. It will follow a market led approach to scaling up the most promising opportunities for GHG mitigation. Subject to some limitations on eligibility (described in Section D.1.2), it is expected that the initiative will support projects and ventures from the pre-acceleration programmes in Latin America and West Africa, and Sub-Activities 1.4.1.1 & 2.4.1 relating to energy generation and storage, respectively. The CATALYS T initiative will focus on the types of climate mitigation opportunities outlined in Table B that demonstrate the potential to reduce / sequester GHG emissions at scale in any of the sectors listed above. These include: carbon dioxide (CO₂), methane (CH₄), nitrous oxide (N₂O), hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs) and perfluorocarbons (PFCs). The types of climate mitigation opportunities outlined in Table 5 provides a summary of the types of ventures that can, in principle, be selected for inclusion, subject to additional screening and competitive selection criteria. |

| Energy generation & power generation ventures | Energy storage | Energy storage technologies for consumers and businesses, particularly those that increase the usability of renewable energy solutions (e.g. batteries). |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 28

## Renewable energy systems
Scaling up the deployment and rehabilitation of generation technologies such as wind, solar and run-of-river hydropower.

## Small-scale solar
Every building should have access to rooftop solar panels and premises such as clinics and offices.

## Smart grids
Research and development of associated software and tools capable of managing intermittent renewable energy supply and demand.

### Low-emission transport vehicles

#### E-mobility
Electric vehicles (cars, bikes, etc.) and associated business models, such as car-sharing and ride-hailing services.

##### Shared mobility
Business cases around shared mobility solutions (car-pooling, last-mile delivery).

##### Smart mobility
Route planning apps for minimizing journey times and energy consumption.

## Energy efficiency
Platforms, technologies and equipment that require less energy than their conventional counterparts in buildings, industry, household appliances and commercial applications. 

## Alternative materials
Materials that are more sustainable or have a lower environmental impact (e.g., bioplastics, multi-use plastics, recycled paper and cardboard).

## Smart city solutions
Analytics for mobility in cities and regions; development of smart traffic management systems.

## Smart manufacturing
Manufacturing processes and equipment that use less raw material, energy and water than conventional counterparts. 

## Sustainable building materials
Materials such as cement, wood and insulation foam - that have lower embodied carbon emissions (and can be more easily disposed) than baseline alternatives: materials may also be zero-carbon or biodegradable.

## Smart buildings
Systems and equipment that integrate discrete energy saving or emission reduction technologies (e.g., solar panels, heat recovery cooling systems) or which incorporate low-emission features in their design. 

## Urban planning
Solutions that address settlement-related emissions from a holistic perspective: e.g., urban planning solutions for the provision of cycle routes, tree planting schemes, etc.

## Sustainable consumption
Technologies and products – such as energy-efficient goods and services, biodegradable packaging, reduced-weight alternatives, materials with lower embodied carbon or water footprint. 

## Clean water/water availability
Technologies and processes - such as PV on PV water pumping and solar thermal systems for heating and cooling of water.

## Clean air
Solutions that reduce pollution – where such solutions also reduce GHG emissions.

## Sanitation
Transformation of processes that reduce GHG emissions associated with sewage management - e.g., variable speed pumps, energy-efficient sludge drying and incineration technologies. 

## Waste management
Technologies and processes for the reduction or elimination of waste generation; solutions to reduce the GHG emissions from stored waste (e.g., landfills).

## Forestry & land use ventures

### AgroTech (land)
Solutions to increase efficiency and sustainability of land management: e.g., precision agriculture, agroforestry, alternative crops, crop rotation, alternative wetting and drying (in paddy fields), etc.

### AppTech (soil/pesticides)
Reduced use of nitrogen fertilizers; alternative feedstock low-carbon intensity for the production of pesticides. 

### Alternative proteins
Alternatives to industrial meat production – e.g., plant-based and lab-cultivated meat, aquaculture, etc.


For example, this would not include vehicle catalytic converters - as they tend to increase carbon dioxide and nitrous oxide emissions (even when using low-carbon fuels such as hydrogen or ammonia blends). However, improved conditions that use less fossilised and produce less CO2 will be eligible, for example.

## Food security
- Agricultural solutions that reduce inputs (fertilizers, pesticides, etc.) and/or increase outputs (more efficient farming practices), in the production of staple food crops.
- Solutions to reduce spoilage/wastage: e.g. more efficient transport, processing, storage, packaging, marketing, distribution, and use of devices, etc.

## Food waste
- Technical solutions and analysis for livestock feed and soil nutrient management – e.g. satellite data services, drones, mobile apps, GIS, etc.

## Monitoring land use
- Business cases and solutions for reversing ecosystem loss through improved land use practices, including the development of ecosystem services, data provenance and analytics, etc.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 30

## Implementation arrangements while the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes in West Africa are different from a regional perspective, the GCF will provide funding for local pre-accelerators / accelerators and seed funds in the IPED network in participating countries. The GCF will also provide technical assistance to the IPED network in West Africa, Impact Hub, Climate-KIC and (IPED), delivery of the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes is provided to and from Montevideo (Mexico) by the Executing Entity, Tec de Monterrey.

### Structure and duration of acceleration programmes: The acceleration programme in Latin America/CAT5 will be implemented through a regional approach. The programme will be carried out in cohorts of 10 ventures and each cohort will benefit from programme support for 6 months. The first cohort is expected to start in the second half of Year 2, with subsequent cohorts starting over the first 3 years of the CATALYST Initiative. In West Africa, in contrast, the regional acceleration programme will be implemented on a country-by-country basis (on an annual basis). Venture screening and selection will occur continuously, on a rolling basis, in the first year of the project. The second year will see the start-up of 10 ventures per country through the acceleration programme for three years, receiving intense technical assistance support in Year 2 of the project. In Year 3, the GCF will provide funding for follow-on meetings and mentoring meetings IPED and light touch' technical assistance in Years 2 and 3 if needed.

### Standardisation versus customisation: more generally, the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes are expected to be implemented on a standardised basis. The only exception is that triggering repayment of the repayable grant is the same for all (fully funded ventures) while the programme support provided by the GCF will be tailored in a more customised manner, tailored to the specific profile and needs of each supported venture. This different approach reflects the greater complexity of the start-up sector in West Africa and the generally more challenging business environment.

### Despite these differences, the two programmes share some common elements: For the sake of brevity, only the regional acceleration programme is described below. However, it should be noted that this is directly applicable to Component 1. The reader is referred to the regional feasibility studies (Mexico and West Africa) for detailed descriptions of all Outputs, Activities and Sub-Activities in both regions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 52

## General requirements for additional ESSOs and venture investors – they must:

- Be an existing participant in the programme planning to be active, in one of the following regions: Latin America and West Africa.
- Have a clear business plan and investment proposition and entrepreneurial experience.
- In the case of VC firms, this will extend to investments or planned investments in climate change projects.
- Be private sector, public sector or non-profit entities.
- Cover all relevant costs (in the case of in-person workshops or events) themselves.

### Activity 2.2.1: Community building and ideation actions in West Africa

To support and encourage entrepreneurs and potential entrepreneurs to develop early ideas for climate business opportunities, a series of community-building and ideation actions will be conducted as part of the pre-acceleration programme (Sub-Activity 2.2.1). The local implementation partners of the pre-acceleration programme will run workshops (Sub-Activity 2.2.1.1) in each country to promote early ideas for climate business opportunities, which will then be promoted within each country through the local implementation partner’s existing stakeholder network (local government, NGOs, etc.). In addition, a series of ideation actions will also be conducted (Sub-Activity 2.2.1.2), with those being promoted in women’s networks and among entrepreneurs backgrounds usually underrepresented in climate entrepreneurship.

All community-building and ideation activities will be conducted in a gender-sensitive way (see GCF).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 33

## A climathon is a place-based programme that harnesses the power and imagination of stakeholders to co-create ideas to tackle local climate mitigation challenges.

### Background

A climathon is an innovative, participatory approach for engaging diverse groups of stakeholders in the design and implementation of solutions to local climate change challenges. It brings together representatives from government, civil society, business, professionals, youth representatives, civil society organisations (CSOs), academicians, students and software developers. The climathon model has been piloted by GIZ in West Africa and is now being scaled up.

### Objectives

The objectives of the climathon are to:

- **Strengthen local climate action**: By bringing together a diverse group of stakeholders from different sectors, the climathon aims to generate innovative ideas that can be implemented at the local level.
- **Build collective knowledge**: The climathon provides an opportunity for participants to share their experiences and expertise, leading to the creation of new knowledge and insights.
- **Strengthening networks through climathon participation**: By participating in a climathon, individuals and organisations are able to build relationships with others who have similar interests and goals.

### Implementation

Climathons will be executed as follows:

1. **First, Climatic Kicks off**:
   - Facilitate local delivery partners in all countries participating in the regional CATALIST™.
   - In close coordination with GIZ, Impact Hub Abidjan and PED (all Existing Entities for the pre-acceleration programme).
   - Provide the local climathon delivery partners with templates and materials with which to reach out to stakeholders.

2. **In select cases**, work with local climathon delivery partners to hold warm-up activities that help build interest in the climathon, such as:

   - Short workshops at universities/schools.
   - Presenting at related conferences/ events.
   - Connecting participants to others in the global Climathon Network via existing climate networks.

3. **Connect climathon participants to others** in the global Climathon Network via existing climate networks.

### Next

The local delivery partners will:

1. **Recruit participants and other relevant partners to participate in annual climathons**.
2. **Manage the overall planning of the annual event locally**, including:
   - Recruitment of all participants from relevant organisations (universities, municipal- ities, etc.).
   - Outreach, communications and PR.
3. **Monitor progress on ideas generated contributing to the overall learning agenda of the regional CATALIST™**.

### Organise climathons with participants prior intervention:

1. **Plan for follow-up activities**, with further sources of support and other regions (e.g., via engagement with the CATALIST pre-acceleration programme or other GCF-funded climate partners or via the global Climathon Network).

Where possible, climathons will be held in October/November each year, in order to coincide with Climate Week. This will serve to bring together a diverse group of 30+ like-minded organisations from around the world and a global repository of past ideas/solutions.

A strong professional network is key for female entrepreneurs to succeed. Before and after participation in the climaths and the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes, (potential) en-hance-ment of their networks will be supported by a digital community platform for climate entrepreneurship in West Africa. The digital community will be for female entrepreneurs to become part of a vibrant network of like-minded women entrepreneurs and other stakeholders benefiting from the support of like-minded female founders. It will serve as an online gateway to useful information (materials for the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes). The digital community will:

- Open up opportunities – calls to application, etc.

### Output 2.3: Selected ventures in West Africa have launched their climate products in local markets

#### Activity 2.3.1: West Africa climate venture are acceleration programmes

86. Over 3 years (approximately 36 months), the West Africa pre-acceleration programme will support 60 climate ventures to develop and commercialize high-impact, medium-viable climate products or services and be ready to enter local / regional markets. The pre-acceleration programme will be implemented on a country-by-country basis in each of the five countries (Benin, Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Togo). The programme will be implemented in two phases:

- Phase 1: Selected climate ventures with technical assistance over a period of approximately 6 months.
- Phase 2: Climate ventures from phase 1 with the highest climate impact will be selected for approximately 4 months of additional one-on-one coaching, as well as each receiving a up to EUR 10,000 grant and other pre-agreed expertise.

## Venture Selection Process

To achieve diversity among applicants, special events will actively promote the programme amongst women and entrepreneurs with backgrounds usually under-represented in pre-commercial programmes. The following are examples of such events:

- **Impact Hub Abidjan**: In conjunction with Impact Hub Abidjan, a global network of innovation hubs that support early-stage climate-related businesses, we invite all applicants involved in climate-related businesses to participate in local events to present their early-stage concepts and receive feedback from the audience. We will also provide an opportunity for these entrepreneurs to share their experiences on our website/webinars to share their experiences and outline how the programme could have supported them.

- **Impact Hub Abidjan**: In conjunction with Impact Hub Abidjan, a global network of innovation hubs that support early-stage climate-related businesses, we invite all applicants involved in climate-related businesses to participate in local events to present their early-stage concepts and receive feedback from the audience. We will also provide an opportunity for these entrepreneurs to share their experiences on our website/webinars to share their experiences and outline how the programme could have supported them.

### Request for referrals

We request referrals from regional pre-accelerators and accelerators: Impact Hub Abidjan in conjunction with other hubs in West Africa, as well as other hubs in Europe and North America. These referrals will be used to identify potential applicants who may not otherwise apply through the online application process or social media channels.

## a) E&S screening for pre-acceleration (see ESFM, Annex 6a). This screening will serve to:

- Exclude ventures that:
    - Identify potential E&S "red flags": that is, negative risks and impacts that would not be managed adequately by the venture.
    - Assess a venture's needs in the context of environmental and social permits, licences and similar requirements.
    - Identify topics where the ventures could require dedicated support if they enter the pre-acceleration programme.

- Apply a media check for potential infringements of human rights or discrimination.

The ESFM provides the relevant E&S screening checklist. The list of excluded ventures, and the list of ventures requiring further assessment, will be updated on agreement with the European Commission (EC) resettlement (ESR), negative impacts on biodiversity (ESB) and negative impacts on indigenous people (ESE). These lists are available in the Environmental and Social Assessment Framework (ESAF) and the Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA). The list can be updated upon agreement of the European Commission.

## b) Gender screening (see GAP Annex Bii):

- The venture's product or service has obvious negative implications for women: e.g., exacer-bating existing gender inequalities, creating new ones without extra compensation.
- The venture has none of its founders and employees identifying as female and the venture has no plans to recruit any female staff in the next 12 months.

For acceptance into Phase I of the pre-acceleration programme, applications can be submitted by individuals. Formal business registration of the venture will only be required for Phase 2 application. The gap analysis will be used to determine whether a venture requires further gender screening. Ventures that pass the eligibility screening will then proceed to the venture selection process.

The Pre-Acceleration Screening Team will consist of:
- Two members from Impact Hub Addis Ababa, one from ESD, and three IPED (one of whom is regional E&S specialist).
- The second is a gender specialist.
- The role of the climate expertise expert will primarily be to assess the plausibility of (i) the optimisation of the venture's business model in line with the Climate Impact Hypothesis (CIH), and (ii) self-assessment in the venture selection process (below). The decision to clear a venture to proceed to the pre-acceleration programme will be made by the screening team, which may include IPED. As Executing Entities for the pre-acceleration programme, will exercise ultimate decision-making authority on whether or not to invite ventures to apply and to initially agree on clearing a venture; the venture shall not proceed to the selection process.

### Venture Selection

The selection process will consist of 3 elements:
- Climate impact assessment: Each of the ~40 ventures will be asked to complete a climate impact self-assessment, using the Climate Impact Hypothesis (CIH) tool. This will provide an initial indication as to whether or not the venture is likely to have significant mitigation impact – essentially, one that provides: (i) a basis for subsequent structured discussion; and/or (ii) a basis for further assessment of the venture’s potential (e.g., market size, sales projections, etc.); and (iii) a tentative means of comparing the climate impacts of the different ventures.
- Selection interviews: The ventures will be invited to online interviews. During the interviews, the members of the Selection Panel will ask questions about the venture's business model presentation; the members of the Phase I Selection Panel will have the opportunity to ask questions and provide feedback on the venture’s business model. The interviewers will also assess their motivations to join the pre-acceleration programme. A scoring sheet will be used for this purpose, which will be discussed in more detail during the training session and the Q&A session.

* <https://impactforecast.com/impact-hypothesis>


---
**Page 41**
---

Selection criteria will include:

- Climate mitigation impact potential: using a combination of the results of the Climate Impact Hypothesis tool and expert judgment of panel members. Climate mitigation impact potential will be assessed on the basis of:
    - The anticipated abatement effectiveness of the product/service (50% of total score)
    - The climate mitigation impact potential will represent the single most important selection criterion (50% or more of total score).
- Understanding the problem and relevance of the solution: Does the venture have a good understanding of the problem and why it is relevant? Does the venture understand why it has the appropriate solution? (10% of total score)
- Business model: Does the business model provide consistency between the different elements of the business model? Is the value proposition of the venture at least as attractive as that of its competitors? Does the business model have been validated by a management team validated the business model? (20% of total score).
- Market fit: Does the venture have a good understanding of the size and attractiveness of the market potential for its product/service? Does the product/service target an attractive market segment? Is there sufficient demand in the market to support the proposed market expansion? Is the target market large enough for the business to be sustainable? (10% of total score).
- Competitive analysis: Does the venture have a good understanding of its competitors and their products/services? Does it understand how it is different from or better than its main competitors? Are there barriers to entry significant? (10% of total score).
- Shift potential: In what way does the proposed product/service have the potential to shift consumer behavior in favor of climate mitigation? For example, a product (for instance, an electrical appliance that is just 20% more energy efficient than a baseline competitor) may not be considered to have significant shift potential. However, if the market penetration compensates for the relatively low per-unit GHG abatement potential thereof, then it can still be considered as having significant shift potential. Shift potential will be favored, subject to considerations of execution risk, consumer receptiveness, etc., (10% of total score).
- Climate resilience co-benefit: Does the product/service offer potential climate adaptation co-benefits, either to the venture itself or (preferred) to its customers? How extensive and deep are these co-benefits in terms of their contribution to the overall climate mitigation impact. An additional climate adaptation impact is not necessary for inclusion in the above criteria.
- Team: Does the entrepreneur have the qualities and abilities to grow the business (enthusiasm, background, technical and management skills, devotion to the venture)? Does the venture have a strong team? Does the team have a clear understanding of the value proposition, including an explicit interest in addressing climate change? (10% of total score).
- Gender: Is the venture female-led, defined as the founder being a woman? Where there is more than one founder, are the majority of founders (50%) or women? Will the venture offer employment opportunities for women and/or will it help to address gender inequality, diggity, increase women's wages, provide women with unpaid work burden, unlock the potential of women in society etc. (e.g., important social co-benefits)? Does the venture have a strong female leadership team? Does the venture have a strong female technical or related technological innovation? (10% of total score).
- Quality of the presentation/pitch: Is the presentation clear? Is the tone and rhythm of the voice engaging? Is the presentation dynamic? (10% of total score).

Other criteria such as the quality of the business plan, its completeness, clarity, differentiation among members will help calibrate the scores and enable the final selection of participants to be made for Phase 1 of the pre-acceleration programme (20 ventures per cohort).
