## Sub-Activity 2.3.1.2: Pre-acceleration programme – Phase 1

### Executive Unit: Impact Hub Abidjan, Climate KIC, GIZ
Unit of responsibility: Tchoukoua, Fofana

Phase 1 of the pre-acceleration programme will provide technical assistance to 80 pre-climate ventures — 20 ventures per cohort, for a period of 6 months per cohort. Phase 1 will consist of a blend of online training and in-person workshop workshops.

### Online training: The online training will cover the following topics:
- **Business management and planning**
    - Market analysis to gain an understanding of market opportunities, customer needs and unique product benefits.
    - Competitive analysis, including identifying the strengths and weaknesses of the offer, how the venture distinguishes itself from its competitors, and finding its true competitive advantages.
    - Stakeholder mapping, determining the different partners the venture needs to launch and achieve its goals, as well as the stakeholders that may be affected by the venture coming back.
- **Climate adaptation and resilience**
    - Building ventures’ understanding of their potential climate impact (mitigation, adaptation, or both) and how this can be addressed in their business model (e.g., costs and cost-effectiveness).
    - Understanding of how the venture’s product/service climate impact can be improved.
    - Giving ventures on how they can introduce systems thinking and systems innovation concepts into their business model design, with a view to promoting more transdisciplinary approaches to addressing climate change.
- **For those ventures that offer a potential climate resilience co-benefit, providing the tools for them to identify and quantify this co-benefit. This will be done through a series of workshops and one-to-one mentoring sessions, as well as by introducing more transdisciplinary approaches to maximizing this co-benefit in the context of currently viable business models.**
- **Environment and social (ES) topics and gender** (also see Activities 3.1.2 and 3.1.3)
    - Curating ES content relevant for each cohort, including health and safety.
    - Other ES topics as relevant, depending on the characteristics of each cohort: e.g., advice on effluents and waste management, ES frameworks used by UN funds, etc.
- **Business cases for gender in climate innovation and using a gender lens in venture selection**
- **Information on risks of sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH), and gender-based violence (GBV)**
- **Gender training, including on gender-based violence and reducing unconscious gender bias**

### Mentoring: Following the online training, each venture will benefit from one-to-one mentoring, covering a range of topics such as business management, climate adaptation and resilience, and climate leadership. The mentor will be based in (or affiliated with) the local implementation partner and will regularly follow up on the venture’s progress. The local mentor will also provide guidance and support

### All Phase 1 participants will be encouraged to submit online applications for Phase 2 support. Applications will be reviewed against the following selection criteria:
- **Accountability**: If a venture is in the process of being formally registered as a business*, the venture has its own bank account (distinct from any personal accounts) and has received at least one payment.
- **Quality of the venture’s idea, model, growth plan, impact: likely climate mitigation impact, market potential, scalability, level of innovation, quality of envisaged partnerships.**
- **Quality of the venture’s organisational structure, management team, quality of the management / implementation team, quality of the business plan, budget and finance: relevance of the budget vs. activities and desired outputs, financial planning.**
- **Participation in Phase 1: the venture registered good attendance and participation in Phase 1.**
- **Quality of the presentation / pitch: the presentation is clear and dynamic. The tone is engaging. The presenter is able to answer questions about the business model in a fluid manner.**

The Phase 2 Selection Panel will consist of panel members with the following responsibilities:
- **Adlanet**: Assess whether the business model is strong and marketable.
- **Dakar**: Assess whether the business structure and projections are coherent, that the uses proposed for the grant are legitimate (e.g., are not likely to involve prohibited activities), such as

### Payment of entertainment costs or the purchase of products and services from suppliers closely affiliated with venture employees) and are catalytic for the business; and that the grant will be used to support the development of a new product or service, where at least 25% of the total budget must come from the venture's own resources (not in-kind) contribution.

The CATALIST Initiative’s regional E&S specialist will confirm the E&S screening (already performed by Impact Hub Abidjan) and define specific criteria for further coaching according. CAF experts will then consider impacts of the invested businesses are strong and well-articulated.

The Phase 2 Selection Panel will issue two types of decisions:

- Positive opinion: The venture meets all the requirements of specified conditions (eg. obtaining a lease, recruiting an executive, etc.)
- Negative opinion: Due to specific reasons. In this case, Impact Hub Abidjan will:
    - notify the venture of the refusal,
    - provide reasons for the refusal; and
    - provide constructive feedback on how the venture can improve its offering to other potential sources of support / finance.

The decision to accept a venture into one of 2 of the pre-acceleration programmes will be made by majority vote. The decision is based on the following criteria (in no particular order):
- On climate relevance (or lack thereof) of the venture and compliance with the E&S safeguards and GMS.
- In the case of a positive opinion from the Phase 2 Selection Committee, a draft grant contract will be prepared by Impact Hub Abidjan, which will forward it, together with appendices, to PIED. One appendix will include a detailed description of the venture’s business plan, another will outline the venture about obligations pertaining to gender and E&S safeguards (including SEAH), that will be included in the grant contract. Once the contract has been approved by PIED, it will be signed by both the venture and PIED.

### Sub-Activity 2.3.1.4 Pre-acceleration programme – Phase 2

Executing Entities: Impact Hub Abidjan, Climate KIC, IPEI, GIZ
Use of funds: €50k (€25k per selected venture) (granted to climate ventures)
Support provided to the climate ventures (15% in cohort) in Phase 2 of the pre-acceleration programme will take the form of:
- an in-depth climate mitigation impact assessment (if); one-to-one coaching; and
- a monthly budget for travel expenses to cover all agreed expenses. These will be provided in parallel over a period of 4 months.

In-depth climate mitigation assessment: Each venture will have opportunity to advance the climate mitigation impact assessment, which is an important step towards achieving their goals (using the Climate Impact Hypothesis tool under Sub-Activity 2.3.1.1). This advanced assessment will be conducted by a third-party consultant and will be guided by an independent, third party certified CDP trainer. This exercise will provide an opportunity to review the venture’s business plan (including its assumptions about market size, technology or market context), calibrate parameters (e.g. emissions factors, projected sales, etc.), and identify opportunities for further mitigation. The results of this assessment can be shared with VC funds and other potential investors (for more information, see Sub-Activity 2.3.1.1).

One-to-one coaching: The selected ventures will receive one-on-one coaching (online or face-to-face) in the areas of climate strategy and the Green Growth Model (GGM). During Phase 1, Entrepreneurs will have access to coaching sessions (at least 4 hours per month) with their coaches. These sessions can be held either online or in-person at Impact Hub Abidjan as a partner in their country. The coaching will be growth-oriented and will address themes such as:
- Production

# Sales and marketing

* Business management
    * Financial planning
    * E&S management for specific subjects identified during the EES screening
    * Proposal development plan (investment readiness for grant payment)

Sessions will be held at specified intervals to share feedback, review progress toward goal attainment, and provide advice for the next steps. These sessions will also allow the coaches to identify potential partners and other resources that may be needed by the venture. The coaches will also connect with the Executing Entities' regional networks to seek advice and/or make useful connections with other experts in their field of expertise. At the end of Phase 2, we will provide training to 3 Impact Hub Abidjan coaches on the investment readiness module.

## Grant element

Upon satisfactory completion of the grant contract between IMPD and the climate venture, the grant will be disbursed in accordance with the terms set out in the grant agreement. IMPD will disburse the grant in installments. The first installment will be paid upon signature of the grant agreement by both parties. Subsequent payments will be made after the completion of a range of activities relevant to pre-acceleration of the venture – including R&D, business development and market research. In addition, if the climate venture is not able to demonstrate that it has developed a viable commercial product or service, the climate venture may have to order to develop technological, commercial and consumer viability.

The grant contract will include clauses that aim to minimise the possibility of grant misuse, including:

* Eligibility for grant funding: The grant will be paid only after the Phase 2 Selection Committee's initial approval.
* Eligible costs: The grant can be used to cover R&D, equipment, software, data / information services and professional services (e.g. legal, accounting) critical to the R&D, product development or market research activities of the venture. The grant will not be used for any general nature of the expenses and generally show that it is managing its business in a lean manner.
* In-kind financing: (an entrepreneur's own work time) and net income from the 25% own-contribution to the project, if applicable, are eligible costs. The venture must also be able to borrow, given the very early stage of ventures in the pre-acceleration programme. This grant will not be used for any general nature of expenses.
* Exclusion lists: and spending cap (for instance, travel and entertainment costs, or remuneration of staff members who are not directly involved with the project). The grant can only be used to cover both grant funded and entrepreneur-funded expenses.
* Confidentiality: The grant will be used for preventing the purchase of products and services from suppliers closely affiliated with climate venture and pre-accelerator staff mem bers.
* Adherence to E&S requirements on excluded activities.
* Prohibition against child labour, forced employment, protection of the workforce, provision of a safe and healthy work environment.
* Non-discrimination: The grant will not be used for any general nature of expenses.
* Zero tolerance of gender-based violence (GBV), or of sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment.

## Phase 2 monitoring

The focal point within the local implementation partner will monitor progress and support the ventures in drafting and submitting monthly reports (progress on activities and financial status) to IMPD. The monthly reports will be used by IMPD to collect information on the market and the venture. The monthly reports will include mention of significant E&S issues that may arise during Phase 2.

Cohort workshop: At the end of Phase 2, participants will come together for a third and final time for an in-person group workshop (3 days in Abidjan, Dakar or Bamako) to review the content of

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 42

## Output 2.4: Selected climate ventures in West Africa with MVPs in local markets are empowered to rapidly scale their business and business impact

### Activity 2.4.1: West Africa climate venture acceleration programme

87. The West Africa acceleration programme aims to achieve the commercial investment readiness of 30 West African climate ventures by the end of the CATALIST initiative, with a focus on providing them with acceleration support. The acceleration programme will provide comprehensive technical assistance and regulatory guidance to help these ventures reach their market potential.

88. Activity 2.4.1 consists of 3 Sub-Activities:

* **Sub-Activity 2.4.1.1: Venture screening and selection**

    Executing Entities: IPEF, Climate-KIC, GIZ

    Use of Funds: Up to €500k for the venture assistance

This will consist of 5 principal steps:
1. Prospect identification and first assessment
2. Due diligence
3. Selection committee
4. Value Creation Plan
5. Co-Development of the Value Creation Plan (IPEF and the venture)

For each venture, step 1 to 5 will, together, typically take 5–6 months:

* Identification and first assessment of the venture: approximately 1 month.
* Mandate D check and due diligence: approximately 3 months.
* Selection committee meeting: approximately 1 month.
* Co-Development of the Value Creation Plan (IPEF and the venture): approximately 1 month.

**Prospect identification and first assessment:** Unlike the Latin America CATALIST ventures, will not be selected for the acceleration programme in West Africa through a regular call for applications. Instead, IPEF will scope out participants by identifying potential seed companies that are already being supported by IPEF. This continuous, rolling approach is driven by two considerations: (i) it will provide entrepreneurs with more consistent access to IPEF’s support and (ii) it will allow IPEF to focus on ventures around their needs rather than around the CATALIST initiative's preferences; and (iii) financing 10 companies in parallel will be more efficient. The selection process will be based on a seed company’s readiness with basic business plans, financial statements, and market research. A seed team will not be available to conduct D2D due diligence missions at the same time and will need to balance its workload.

IPEF will scope:
* Up to 10 participants of the West Africa CATALIST pre-acceleration programme (see Activity 2.3) as promising climate ventures.
* Up to 5 promising ventures discovered through market surveillance. To achieve market diversity among applicants, IPEF will also run D2D missions with women-led needs-based enterprises with basic business plans and financial statements in addition to the seed companies (see D2D Annex B). All scoping activities will be conducted in a gender-sensitive manner (see CAP). Information materials will be in French, outlining the specific criteria and the selection process.

## Initial eligibility check

The most promising ventures will be invited to submit further information that will allow for a more detailed assessment of their eligibility.

### The venture satisfies the IFC definition of a micro or small enterprise (MSE).

- **The venture is located in one of the following countries:** East Asia and Pacific, Latin America and Caribbean, South Asia, Sub-Saharan Africa, West Asia.
- **The venture has not received a No Objection Letter (NOL).**
- **The venture does not have any outstanding debt or personal bank account of the entrepreneur / founder.**

### The venture meets all the requirements of the venture credit indicators that support projects provided by this programme and will primarily benefit beneficiaries from developing (Non Annex 1) country.

- **The venture is located in a country with a per capita income below $2,500 and does not exceed $4,976.**
- **The venture has no more than 30 employees.**

### ESG screening

The same ESG screening for the pre-acceleration programme is applied to the acceleration programme.

- **Gender diversity:** The venture must include at least one of:
    - The venture's product or service has obvious negative implications for women, e.g., exacerbates wage disparities or restricts access to education.
    - At least 50% of the founders and employees identifying as female and the venture has no more than three founders.
- **The venture’s founder(s) is/are devoted to this one venture only. Alternatively, the venture is the founder's main business.**
- **The venture has a minimum viable product (MVP) in one or more of the categories listed in Table 1 below and/or has demonstrated significant progress towards achieving its goals in terms of GHG emissions or carbon sequestration in one or more of the SCP’s Mitigation Result Areas. To provide evidence, please include a description of how the venture is expected to reduce GHG emissions, and by roughly how much, the venture will be required to complete an initial climate impact assessment (CIA) as part of the pre-acceleration programme. For ventures that have graduated from the pre-acceleration programme or wish to apply for funding beyond $50,000 and $100,000 respectively, they must submit their CIA report.**
- **The venture produces, or plans to produce, only one or more climate-friendly (low-emission products or services) or low-carbon solutions. The venture does not produce, nor plan to produce high-emission products or services. This ensures that support provided by the GCF is used for its intended purpose and does not contribute to GHG emissions.**
- **The venture needs funding; the venture requires seed funding between EUR 50,000 and EUR 100,000.**

### Mandate 18: Clarity of risk

Those ventures that pass the initial eligibility check will be invited to a meeting with the IFC team to interview them where the venture is assessed against the following screening criteria:

- **Climate impact mitigation potential:** The score (or, where relevant, the climate mitigation assessment) and the expert judgement of the IPEF screening team. Climate impact mitigation potential will be assessed on the basis of all of the anticipated activities.

## Business model and market traction (20% of total score)

- The start-up has a minimum viable product and some market experience – potentially actual product sales but, alternatively, early-adaptor testing of an initial version of the product/service.
- The start-up’s product/service has reached at least Technology Readiness Level (TRL) 4 (i.e. it is ready for commercialization).
- The start-up has a high-potential business model, backed up by evidence of market adoption and/or potential for growth and sustainability.

## Venture management team (10% of total score)

- The start-up has evidence of team competence/capability for growing the start-up (as defined in the Selection Criteria) and managing the risks that sets expose it to (e.g. event markets).
- The venture management team demonstrate an understanding of the main impacts and risks of their product/service (e.g. energy and materials sourcing, waste and efficiency, etc.) and how these can be managed.

## Paradigm shift potential (10% of total score): In what way does the proposed product/service have the potential to be innovative and disruptive in the target market? Incremental change is not sufficient; the venture must demonstrate a clear pathway for significant market penetration compensates for the relatively low per-unit GHG abatement potential. However, ventures with high GHG abatement potential but low market penetration will be favoured, subject to considerations of execution risk, consumer receptiveness, etc.

## Climate resilience co-benefit (10% of total score): Does the product/service offer potential climate resilience co-benefits? How extensive and deep are these adaptation benefits likely to be? All start-ups must offer a solution that is climate-resilient. However, ventures with high climate resilience will be necessary for inclusion in the acceleration programme, but it will be positively assessed.

## Gender (10% of total score): Is the start-up female-led, defined as the founder being a woman? Will the start-up offer products or services that address a gender-related socio-economic challenge and/or provide opportunities for women to participate in and benefit from unpaid work burden, unlock the potential of women-dominant sectors (e.g. improved agricultural land management, increased access to finance, etc.) and/or contribute to the growth of female-dominated sectors (e.g. high-yielding cash crops, climate-related technological innovations)?

Because applications to the acceleration programme will be assessed on a rolling, continuous basis – and not as part of a group of applicants for entry into a cohort – the scoring system will play a different role in each round. In the first round of the Acceleration Programme, the scoring will enable ranking – and hence selection – of the top 20 ventures to enter the programme (see below). The Selection Committee will then assess these 20 ventures against the criteria set out above for the purpose of making its final selection.

(i) For those ventures that score 35% or more, the scoring will serve as a means of assessing each venture’s potential and will be used to determine whether it is suitable for inclusion on the agenda of the Selection Committee. (For details on the composition of the Selection Committee, see below.)

If the IFED screening team judges that a venture is suitable for potential entry into the acceleration programme, it will sign a Non-Disclosure Agreement (NDA) with the venture and advance to the next stage: due diligence.

## B

### Undertake a know your customer (KYC) check.
- Establish the business growth potential, including detailed analysis of market size and competition.

Ventures that have graduated from the pre-acceleration programme can use their assessment report as a basis for this exercise, unless it is more than 12 months old (e.g., 2023.1.4), provided there has been no material change in the venture's product / business service.

## Selection Committee

The venture due diligence results will be presented to the Selection Committee for review. The Selection Committee will consist of the following members, with the following responsibilities:
- The IPED Seed Funding Director will organise and chair the Committee, and will prepare the minutes of the meeting.
- A member of the IPED screening team will assess the business aspects of the venture (business model, market size, competitive landscape, financial projections, etc.).
- A legal/technical investor or local private sector specialist will provide his/her knowledge of the local venture ecosystem.
- A GIZ representative will assess the EAS risks, impacts and management capacity, will define specific needs for further training and support and will ensure that the selected ventures are in compliance with the GIZ policies.

The decision to accept a venture into the acceleration programme will be taken by majority vote, with objections from any of the members being considered valid. The decision will take into account relevance (or lack thereof) of the venture and compliance with the EAS standards and gender policies.

### Value Creation Plan
Each venture that is accepted into the acceleration programme will develop a 3-year Value Creation Plan in conjunction with IPED at the very start of the programmes. The Value Creation Plan will be developed as part of Sub-Activity 2.4.1.2, which is described below (see Activity 2.4.1.2). The Value Creation Plan will focus primarily on the tasks that need to be accomplished in order for the venture to achieve its business goals and objectives within a three-year period: climate impact, recruitment of key personnel, financial reporting, the implementation of good practices, and EAS aspects. The Value Creation Plan will also include an assessment of the EAS aspects in the Value Creation Plan focuses the EAS due diligence (EASD) and is described in Section 4.5.3 of this document. IPED will provide technical assistance to the venture during the technical assistance missions, IPED will closely monitor the implementation of the Value Creation Plan of each venture throughout the three-year period. The IPED team will also provide targeted, bespoke needs-based coaching and mentoring support during the 3-year technical assistance mission.

### Sub-Activity 2.4.1.3 Acceleration programme – technical assistance

**Executing Entity:** IPED, GIZ Climate KIC  
**Use of GCF proceeds:** Technical assistance  

This will be structured as follows:

* Phase 1: Pre-acceleration phase (up to and including the end of the venture’s participation in the acceleration programme), which will benefit from an intensive programme of technical assistance that includes business coaching, climate change advisory support, and labour market and skills development support.
* Phase 2: Acceleration phase (from the start of the venture’s participation in the acceleration programme, light-touch mentoring will be provided, as well as an update on depth climate impact assessment.

### Phase 1 Technical assistance

Technical assistance will be provided that is highly tailored to each venture. The precise mix of support activities will vary according to need. This high level of TA individualisation serves as a key differentiator from the standardised approach used in the pre-acceleration programme (especially Phase 1 of the pre-acceleration programme, which offers a standard training curriculum). The technical assistance provided during this phase is complementary to the acceleration programme and subsequently enter the acceleration programme do not duplicate the support provided.

* **Business coaching:** IPED staff or external experts can cover themes such as (non-exhaustive): investment readiness (inclusive governance, legal formalisation, trustworthy accounting systems), market access (e.g. customer acquisition, sales and marketing), etc.
* **Labour formation support and labour quality enhancement:** awareness-raising and ad hoc support to improve labour formalisation and, while on the path to formalisation, improvement of labour conditions and skills development (e.g., through training in basic literacy, numeracy, etc.). IPED has built a track-record and acknowledged expertise on such formalisation training in West Africa.
* **Climate advisory support:** will be available on an as-needed basis, including (non-exhaustive):
    * Building ventures’ understanding of their potential climate mitigation impact: introduction to concepts (baselines, emission factors, leakage, etc.), tools and case-study applications
    * Guiding ventures on how they can introduce systems thinking and systems innovation approaches into their business models (e.g., through the use of scenario planning)
    * Facilitating access to relevant tools and methodologies to assess the scope and magnitude of this co-benefit in the context of commercially viable business models.

## Activity 3.1.1: Climate impact and co-benefit assessment

### Sub-Activity 3.1.1.1: Climate mitigation impact assessment

Executing Entities: Climate-KIC, GIZ
Use of Funds: Up to €250k

Climate-KIC will collect and review sub-dataset tables (economy, emissions, etc.) from the relevant geographies / countries in order to update the Climate Impact Forecast (CIF) tool with locally calibrated baseline data. The CIF tool will be used as a decision support system for climate venture selection and venture emission scenarios. The software, and accompanying user manuals and training materials, will be developed by Climate-KIC. The CIF tool will be improved by expanding its functionality to include the ability to assess the impact of different mitigation measures (which builds on the CIF tool to predict cohort-level climate impact). This expanded CIF tool will be used in conjunction with the MORSE model.

The proposed MORSE model will serve as a free online resource for any stakeholder (worldwide) that manages or invests in climate ventures. The model will allow users to assess the net and ex post climate mitigation impact of their portfolio, including in the context of their own carbon footprint.

Test run workshops (accompanied by training) will be undertaken with the regional Executing Entities: Tico de Monterrey in Latin America and Impact Hub Abidjan and IPED in West Africa. A workshop series will be held to train a small group of trainers from each region on how to use the CIF tool and MORSE model, as well as how to run workshops for different types of climate venture programmes (e.g., 4 workshops in total) to enable trainers to work with a small group of ventures at different stages. The workshops will focus on using the CIF tool and MORSE model to assess the impact of these elements from the outset of the CATALYT’5 Initiative in order to select ventures for inclusion in the portfolio. The workshops will also include training on how to run more in-depth climate mitigation assessments of the ventures at various points in their development.

2. **MORSE Model for Regional Climate Start-up Ecosystem Impacts**: See Section D.1.1 for further details.

## B

### Introduction: The evolving frequency/severity of climate hazards and the difficulty in establishing effective revenue-generation models. These challenges can be overcome but most organizations lack the necessary skills, knowledge, and resources to do so. This proposal outlines the support needed by entrepreneurs and ventures working to create these solutions. Sub-Activity 3.1.2 will provide a regional acceleration initiative (CATALYT) that will help entrepreneurs and venture teams in the region understand ESGs to help them understand what constitutes an effective adaptation and resilience co-benefit. The CATALYT initiative will also provide support for entrepreneurs and venture teams who need to provide to the entrepreneurs developing mitigation solutions in order to maximize adaptability and flexibility. This initiative will be implemented by Climate KIC, a European Union funded community of climate solution providers that are able to offer mitigation benefits and substantive adaptation co-benefits. The CATALYT initiative will also provide support for entrepreneurs and investors. In addition, the CATALYT initiative will introduce tools that help ventures to assess their solutions’ ability to deliver both mitigation and adaptation co-benefits.

Climate KIC will draw upon its existing, extensive experience of building adaptation and resilience innovation training and capabilities to develop a cohesive body of literature, case studies and tools for CAT. The support framework will be developed in close collaboration with the regional capacities of the regional Executive Units and the climate venture. Climate KIC will determine what constitutes an effective adaptation and resilience co-benefit (e.g., through workshops, toolkits, guidance materials, sectoral priorities, etc.) and will provide a range of tools to entrepreneurs and investors. In addition, the CATALYT initiative will introduce tools that help ventures to assess their solutions’ ability to deliver both mitigation and adaptation co-benefits.

Organizations will hold a four-hour workshop for each region to present the preliminary results to local part- ners and gather collective feedback to inform the design of a support framework. Based on this stakeholder input, Climate KIC will then develop a regionally-specific support frame- work, which will be piloted in the regions. The support framework will be based on the assumption that the primary objective and climate adaptation co-benefits are subsidiary to the mitigation ob- jective. The support framework will be developed for each region (e.g., North America, Europe, West Africa, where possible), and it) translated into Spanish and French.

Dedicated training in the form of a one-day train-the-trainers workshop in each region will be pro- vided by Climate KIC to build capacity within the regional Executive Units and other interested regional ESOs on key adaptation topics and issues. The support framework will consist of a series of workshops, toolkits, guidance materials, sectoral priorities, etc., that will be based on the Adaptation Solutions Taxonomy - ASAT for assessing adaptation and resilience ideas and the climate venture’s ability to deliver both mitigation and adaptation co-benefits. In addition, it will be enabled to:

- Continue or inclusion in the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes on the basis of the likely climate resilience co-benefit.
- Advice on how to integrate climate resilience into acceleration programmes on how to en- hance the climate resilience co-benefits of their goods/services and, crucially, how to lever- age these co-benefits in their business models.
- Undertake in-depth climate resilience assessments of selected ventures using third-party assessment tools and methodologies.

In the regional pre-acceleration programmes, and for relevant ventures only (those with adaptation co- benefits), the CATALYT initiative will provide:

- Ongoing workshops on climate resilience, adaptation and resilience best-practices initially provided by Climate KIC and then led by the regional ESOs.
- The support framework will be based on the basis of the workshops that will be in practi- cal terms. What is the co-benefit? Does my venture have a co-benefit? How can I measure this benefit? To whom? How can these resilience benefits be measured, priced and marketed?
- Peer-to-peer learning sessions with participants in the cohorts and more advanced peers that have developed similar solutions.

In the regional acceleration programmes, and for relevant ventures only (those with adaptation co- benefits), the CATALYT initiative will provide:

### Sub-Activity 3.1.3: Paradigm shift and systems transformation potential

**Executing Entities**: Climate-KIC, GIZ  
Use of GCF proceeds: Technical assistance  

The Executing Entities, local implementation partners and other interested regional EECs will support ventures to make a paradigm shift from a narrow traditional business excellence focus (e.g. profitability) or even an incremental climate impact focus (lower emis-

## Essential Impact Factor Identified in the EAS Assessment (Annex B) is the Opportunity to Advance Labour Formalisation among Small Enterprises in Latin America and West Africa.

### Sub-Activity 3.1.1: Environmental, social and governance (ESG) frameworks

#### Use of ESG criteria for Technical assistance
Climate impact is at the core of the CATALIST Initiative. However, to support truly 'good' and resilient climate businesses and to ensure long-term sustainability of programme interventions, the CATALIST Initiative will also consider other ESG factors in its selection process. These include the processes of pre-accelerated / accelerated ventures and VC firms. Young ventures are innovative but may be at risk of failure due to a lack of resources and experience. They can benefit from pre-accelerators and accelerators and / or funding from VCs. This expectation is also extended to the venture capital community, which will be encouraged to invest in young ventures supported by the CATALIST Initiative. It is, therefore, crucial to (i) support the regional Executive Entities in their efforts to develop a robust ESG framework for pre-accelerated and accelerated ventures; (ii) assist the ventures in becoming ESG-ready; and (iii) support the two regional VC communities to develop an ESG framework that will help them identify opportunities for investment, and address use increasingly expected of them by venture- and other key ecosystem actors.

#### Sub-Activity 3.1.1.5: Focus on adapting existing ESG tools that are not currently fit for purpose for the CATALIST Initiative
The CATALIST Initiative will adopt a number of existing ESG frameworks, including the Principles for Responsible Investing (PRI) and the Sustainability Accounting Standards Board (SASB). The CATALIST Initiative will also develop its own framework to support the use of the pay-out funds. The specificity of VC early stage companies (e.g., fast-changing new markets and business models, lack of historical data, etc.) requires a tailored approach for these entities. Tools will be defined in accordance with investors' needs in Latin America and West Africa. IPEF already has a number of tools that can be adapted to the CATALIST Initiative's needs. Specific gaps in coverage – e.g., impacts on biodiversity, Impact Hub Abidjan and Tec de Monterrey have identified these as key areas for further work – will need to be addressed. The approach is prescriptive. On the basis of discussion held with stakeholders during CATALIST’s Intrapre- sential workshops, the following ESG tools will be used:

* **An ESG due diligence framework (questionnaire) (DDQ):** This is an ESG-aligned framework of questions that can be used to assess a venture's alignment with the UN Sustainable Development Goals and commercial / market / legal due diligence. DDQs come in a short form 'check list' question- naires, which are easy for entrepreneurs to complete. They will be used by pre-accelerators/accelerators, VC firms and other ESGs in the two regions.
* **An ESG reporting framework:** This is an ESG-aligned framework focused to be used by the pre-accelerator / accelerator teams (and, subsequently, other ESGs) to work with the ventures on their ESG performance. It will include a set of questions that can be used for training and improving ESG practices.
* **A Venture Fund Management Framework:** To help manage ESG in pre-accelerators/accelerators, VC firms and other ESGs. This framework will be based on the CATALIST Initiative Report 2017 results and the queries they require adaptation. The Principles for Responsible Investing's (PRI) Reporting Framework (adapted mostly for buy-out/PE funds) will serve as a starting point.

* * *
**Note:** While little academic work has been done on the topic, and development of ESG in venture capital, recent articles in both the Harvard Business Review and the Stanford Social Innovation Review substantiate the claim that VC is slowly starting to adopt ESG. See, for exam- ple: (https://hbr.org/2018/03/the-case-for-esg-in-private-equity) and (https://ssir.org/articles/entry/private-equity-and-esg).

### SASB-like materiality map: The SASB "materiality map" is popular among investors in other asset classes to identify which ESG areas are relevant for a specific (portfolio / investor) company or sector. It can be used as an input into the pre-accelerator, accelerator and venture (star-up) sector. Adopting the external tool for Latin American - West African pre- accelerators, accelerators and VC firms will help them to zoom in on (financially) relevant ESG issues.

These tools will be adapted to the specific contexts of the target pre-accelerators, accelerators and investors. The tools will cover a broad range of topics such as climate change, biodiversity, seed, etc.) and investment focus will be considered. The tools will be provided in Spanish and English. Revised versions will be refined with selected CATALYT initiative partners and revised versions will then be produced.

### Explaining the rationale behind different tools (and ESG more broadly): as part of a broader education effort around ESG, it is important to explain how these tools are used. This document sets out to outline the use of the tools and, ultimately, enable a re-thinking of how pre-acceleration, acceleration and VC firms can better integrate ESG into their investment processes. The document will be parsed by prior input from key CATALYT initiative stakeholders in a dedicated co-development workshop, with feedback on the target ESG-related ESG, local implementation partners and VC funds in individual sessions.

### Current ESG frameworks are not always detailed and prescriptive regarding compliance with typical ESG issues such as board composition, executive compensation, workforce diversity, etc. The ESG framework that is currently used by CATALYT initiative stakeholders is the ESG model required. But they do develop a useful focus on the realisation of positive results along many dimensions, including climate change and biodiversity. However, there are still some ESG issues such as board composition, executive compensation, workforce diversity, data security, privacy, etc., that are not covered by this framework. These are the ESG issues that will govern the behaviour of the climate ventures, the regional pre-accelerators and accelerators, and VC funds. The CATALYT initiative is currently working on harmonising, to the extent possible, the CATALYT initiative's ESMF and the ESG tools developed. This document sets out a rationale for how these two frameworks can be used in tandem: disruption after the GCF investment ends (i.e., to smooth the transition from ESMF to ESG).

Accordingly, Subsection 13.3.1 will identify governance-related gaps in the ESMF and will seek to address them by developing a new framework that is more aligned with the potential for disruption for CATALYT initiative participants (hence the reliance upon the ESG tools that will be used as a basis). The ESG framework will also be useful in addressing some of the number of governance issues, potentially data safety and cybersecurity (to be confirmed following consultation with stakeholders) and other related issues. The rationale behind the ESMF models developed will be made available to external stakeholders free of charge (available online) so that they can use them as a basis for their own ESG frameworks or similar.

## Management Unit recommendations to strengthen CATALIST Initiative execution and achievement of far greater results.
### Ensure CATALIST Initiative momentum and coherence with the evolution of the international context.

#### Description:
Start units: The 3 management Units (MUs) will implement activities under Component 1, Component 2, and Component 3 respectively. The MUs will comprise management-level representatives of the corresponding implementing partners. Each MU will be responsible for the overall coordination of the respective component, including project administration, execution and technical coordination. The Latin America MU will be located in Mexico City and will be led by GIZ. The Africa MU will be based in Dakar and will be led by CAF. The Asia-Pacific MU will be located in Eschborn, Germany. The MUs will ensure CATALIST Initiative execution proceeds according to the agreed work plans and that all activities are aligned with the overall project strategy.

The CATALIST Initiative's Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) and Gender Action Plan (GAP) will provide guidance on how to address these issues. This includes:

- Take management-related and technical decisions.
- Develop, monitor and coordinate annual work plans (including procurement).
- Ensure that the activities are carried out in a safe manner with due regard for health and safety.
- Identify and resolve bottlenecks and implementation challenges and bring these to the attention of the MUs.

At least twice a year, all 3 MUs will meet to undertake strategic thinking and planning, thereby connecting strategy with execution. At the same time, these meetings will serve to exchange on challenges, solutions and lessons learned from the field. The MUs will also meet at least once every quarter to monitor and promote the best possible synchronization of implementation between the Executing Entities. All MUs will also meet on an additional ad hoc basis whenever necessary.

B.3 Alternative Operating Models

103. The design of the CATALIST Initiative reflects best practice. The GCF’s own assessment of climate ventures and entrepreneurs’ needs is pertinent in this regard.

Developing countries face a number of barriers to scaling up their deployment of pivotal technologies to decarbonize high-emitting sectors. Unless the climate innovation potential of developing countries can be unlocked, it will be difficult for them to achieve their mitigation action. However, there are many barriers to the development and widespread adoption of inclusive and sustainable climate innovations in developing countries. These include sector-specific and country-specific barriers that can broadly cluster into four categories: (i) limited awareness of climate innovation opportunities; (ii) lack of access to finance and other enabling factors to ideate or tailor novel approaches; (iii) weak climate innovation and entrepreneurial ecosystems; and (iv) a lack of capacity for scaling up.

A public-private innovation ecosystem is required to drive climate innovation. In mature start-up ecosystems, a host of public and private start-up incubation and acceleration schemes makes available the resources needed by entrepreneurs to develop their ideas into viable businesses, technologies, or digital platforms. The so-called “valley of death” (the gap between initial seed funding and more substantial investment) is often a significant barrier for climate innovations that are nascent and ends later – becoming a forbidding obstacle. This call for new financing models to support climate innovation is also reflected in the GCF’s own experience.

104. GIZ recently produced a book, Strengthening Entrepreneurial Ecosystems™, that summarizes the key lessons learned from GIZ entrepreneurship projects and programmes, and which have informed the design of the CATALIST Initiative. These lessons include:

• A healthy entrepreneurial ecosystem, supported by accelerators and incubators, can provide entrepreneurs with the resources they need to develop their ideas into viable businesses or products with an uncertain future. This dynamic ecosystem produces a self-reinforcing system of innovation that is more resilient – which, in turn, leads to more creative entrepreneurship and attracts even more entrants.

• What makes for a healthy entrepreneurial ecosystem include:

o Capacity building for entrepreneur support organisations (ESOs) – improving their services by better understanding the needs of entrepreneurs

o Connecting ecosystem actors – outreach to entrepreneurs, recruiting mentors and coaches, mobilising investors

o Facilitating international connections e.g. with international ESOs and international investors.

o Providing access to finance – grants, loans, equity investment

o Capacity building for financial organisations – bringing in experts, training, peer learning, de- veloping new products

o Establishing grant schemes - direct grants to entrepreneurs, matching capital to investments, providing guarantees, etc.

o Organising events and competitions – supporting the convening of events, competitions, technology-enhanced platforms and introductions.

#### GIZ as Accredited Entity

105. GIZ, with its head office located in Germany, is the Accredited Entity (AE) for the CATALYT Initiative. GIZ will operate as an Executing Entity in relation to implementation of the CATALYT/3 Initiative.

For the avoidance of doubt: GIZ as the Accredited Entity and GIZ as the Executing Entity are a single legal entity. The responsibilities, authorities and accountability of GIZ will be strictly separated.

106. GIZ is one of the largest international providers of capacity development and technical assistance on climate change issues. It has been involved in climate change projects since 2005 with an accumulated funding of over US$ 1.9 billion. GIZ has a strong track record in the area of green private sector and enterprise development, including support to small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs). In addition to its role as AE for CATALYT/3 Initiative projects in Latin America, GIZ is implementing the CATALYT/3 Initiative projects in Africa. GIZ has a strong presence in Latin America with 140 staff in Bolivia (148 staff), Colombia (222 staff), Costa Rica (62 staff), Ecuador (174 staff), El Salvador (58 staff), Guatemala (92 staff), Honduras (32 staff), Mexico (102 staff), Nicaragua (102 staff), Panama (102 staff) and Peru (192 staff); and in West Africa: Benin (428 staff), Burkina Faso (362 staff), Côte d'Ivoire (295 staff), Ghana (72 staff), Liberia (102 staff), Mali (102 staff), Niger (102 staff), Nigeria (102 staff) and Togo (262 staff). In the CATALYT/3 Initiative countries in Latin America, GIZ is implementing a portfolio of 45 projects. In Africa, GIZ is implementing a portfolio of 78 projects, including 9 climate change projects (see Annex 2b); in the CATALYT/3 Initiative countries in West Africa, GIZ is implementing a portfolio of 10 projects, including 6 SMEs and entrepreneurship and green growth projects, and 14 relevant climate change projects (see Annex 2c).

#### GIZ as Executing Entity

As the AE, GIZ will have oversight responsibility for the CATALYT/3 Initiative, as defined in the Accredited Entity Agreement. The GCF will provide guidance on the implementation of the CATALYT/3 Initiative and oversee the programme during its implementation.

107. Oversight of the programme during implementation:
- Monitoring and evaluation: Ensuring regular communication with the GCF.
- Establishing internal control routines.
- Ensuring independent third-party assessment.
- Financial management, in particular receiving GCF proceeds as well as disbursing, administering and reporting on such funds.
- Ensuring the proper use of GCF proceeds.
- Assessing the performance of the CATALYT/3 Initiative.
- Setting up the subsidiary agreements.
- Monitoring the implementation and the performance of Executing Entities.
- Managing the relationship with the GCF and the performance of Executing Entities.
- Ensuring that Executing Entities' procurement activities comply with GIZ's policies and rules.
- Evaluating the results of projects, including interim and final reviews.

108. Oversight and quality assurance are supported by specific departments in GIZ head office:
- Finance Department: Responsible for strategic and operational financial control, maintaining standards of financial management, and accounting and elaboration of annual statements of accounts, according to GIZ's internal rules.
- Procurement Department: Responsible for procurement, contracting, setting up the financing agreement, and ensuring compliance with the rules of tender procedures through the procurement plan, among other responsibilities.
- Compliance and Legal Department: Responsible for ensuring compliance with GIZ and government rules and regulations.

109. GIZ (head office in Germany and regional offices) will act as an Executing Entity (EE). GIZ will establish a Task Team for Management Unit (MU) for the implementation of Component 3 of the CATALYT/3 Initiative, which will also be responsible for providing coordination support to the 2 Regional Management Units. ## B.4.4 Legal and contractual arrangements

116. The German Federal Ministry for Cooperation and Development (BMZ) will commission GIZ with the implementation of the overall programme (commissioning agreement). The GCF will transfer funds based on a defined CATALYST Initiative monitoring, reporting and evaluation framework. GIZ will receive an internal task assignment from the AE for the implementation of defined CATALYST Initiative activities in the context of the overall programme. In addition to the commissioning agreement, GIZ may sign Hub Abidjan, IPED and Climate-KIC will sign subsidiary agreements with GIZ, based on GIZ’s internal task assignments. The subsidiary agreements will define the scope of work and the legal basis on which GIZ makes the GCF proceeds available to the Executing Entities for the measures in the West Africa pre-acceleration programme (Activity 2.1) and the Latin America pre-accelerator programme (Activity 3.1).

## B.4.5 Financial Flows

117. GIZ will transfer GCF funds to 4 Executing Entities, as well as retaining a portion off the GCF funds to implement activities itself as an Executing Entity. GIZ (Executing Entity) and Climate-KIC will use the grant for the implementation of the pre-accelerator programme in Latin America. IPED will use the pre-accelerators, accelerators and other entrepreneur support organisations (ESOs), as well as climate viability analysis tools and capacity building activities to support the development of business plans and financial assistance for climate ventures. In the West African pre-acceleration programmes (Activity 2.1) and the Latin America pre-accelerator programme (Activity 3.1), GIZ will use the GCF funds to local implementation partners that will provide technical assistance in other West African countries, and IPED will use the GCF funds for financial assistance overruns grants.

B

## Ventures eligible for the acceleration programme will be short-listed in the case of Latin America and start-up selection will be based on a combination of criteria including: (a) the venture's business model; (b) companies with a minimum viable product (MVP) and proof of traction in the market but no significant revenue yet; (c) the venture's ability to scale up its MVP and enter into a phase of growth-oriented marketing test, as well as for the purchase of equipment/technology. Growth companies' business models may be more complex than those of start-ups, but they should have a clear strategy in one or two well-maintained sectors; they may be semi-formal or not very structured, but they have already started their development and are ready to scale up. They must also demonstrate that they can generate as much cash as possible and need funding for working capital, capex or capacity building, or to pivot from an existing business model.

## Candidates will be screened and selected based on transparent criteria relating to the sector and climate mitigation potential, as well as E&S and gender compliance (see Sub-Activity 1.4.1.1 for Latin America and Sub-Activity 2.4.1.3 (West Africa) for more details). The selection process will be carried out by an independent technical assistance described in Sub-Activity 1.4.1.3 (Latin America) and Sub-Activity 2.4.1.3 (West Africa). The total amount of the grant to be disbursed is €UR 50,000,000 for Latin America and €EUR 50,000,000 in West Africa (depending on the venture's specific funding needs, with the maximum amount per venture being €EUR 1,000,000). The grant will be disbursed to the Executing Entities. The Executing Entities will not receive an equity stake in the ventures supported, in recognition of the additional risk they are taking by providing grants for climate mitigation projects and in recognition that targeting the sector, which justify additional concessionality for climate ventures in Latin America and West Africa.

## The useable repayable grants will be dispensed by a grant agreement signed by each climate venture and the respective Executing Entity. In Latin America, one standard agreement will apply to all beneficiaries. The grant will be disbursed as a single payment of €EUR 100,000 per beneficiary. The value creation plan developed by the venture and agreed with IPEF. Repayable grants will be eligible for a boost in the form of an additional grant of up to 50% of the repayable amount (€EUR 50,000) if the repayable grant is disbursed in two instalments. In West Africa, it will be disbursed in one or more instalments as agreed between the Executing Entity and the beneficiary. The grant agreement signed by each party will include clauses that aim to minimise the possibility of grant misuse, including exclusion lists, restrictions on the use of funds, conditions for grant use, conflict of interest and anti-fraud clauses, and adherence to E&S and gender requirements.

## The repayable feature of the grants is deemed integral to the acceleration programme's effectiveness. It will be used as a tool to ensure that beneficiaries are committed to achieving their business goals and obtaining a grant with no strings attached is at risk-free proposition for the recipient. It is also meant to incentivise beneficiaries to use the funds in an efficient manner, thereby increasing the likelihood of capital providers – be it VC funds or commercial banks (the latter being more attractive to growth companies) – providing additional funding to the venture after the grant has been repaid. The terms and conditions of IPEF financing through the duration of the CATALIST Initiative (i.e., during Years 1-4) and the terms and conditions for repayment will be agreed between the beneficiary, the Executing Entity and IPEF. Grants will be admitted into the acceleration programme in Year 1-3.

## The grant will be repaid to the Executing Entity in two instalments: (i) in Latin America and West Africa; In Latin America, the repayment will be triggered in two circumstances: either (i) when a venture achieves annual incremental revenue growth of at least 50% over its baseline period or (ii) if it is admitted into the acceleration programme prior to admission to the acceleration programmes. If any, the grant will be subsequently repaid in quarterly instalments until the outstanding amount has been repaid in full; and (ii) in West Africa, the repayment will be triggered when a venture achieves annual incremental revenue growth of more than US$ 5 million. In which case the grant or any amount yet to be repaid under the previous point (i)) will be repaid in a bulk payment for the outstanding amount.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 66

## 128. While in politics, repayment will be triggered by milestones tailored to each venture and detailed in the grant agreement. During the due diligence phase, IPEF will engage in an in-depth dialogue with the venture on its business plan. The process of vetting the business plan is a critical component of IPEF at guiding the venture towards business and financial goals that are ambitious but achievable. The pro- cess of vetting the business plan is comprehensive and involves a detailed review of the business plan for every aspect of it. Once this intensive vetting of the plan is done, IPEF will be in a position to determine and agree on repayment milestones with the venture. Repayment milestones are designed to reflect the inherent volatility of any business(venture) prospect of repayment. Examples of possible repayment milestones include: (a) 50% of the amount of the fund raised planned in the venture’s business plan; (b) 75% and/or 100% of the amount of the fund-raising planned in the company’s value creation plan; achievement of a business performance indicator such as revenue, EBITDA or EBITDA forecast in the company’s business plan or EBITDA forecast in the company’s value creation plan over a given period (subject to the venture having received an extension of the grant agreement); and/or achievement of an operational performance indicator(s) (e.g. obtaining a significant contract sales).

### 127. In both regional contexts, only the principal amount of the grant will be repaid, and the repayment schedule will be based on the following:

#### 128. While the repayable grant instrument provides limited effective leverage to demand repayment in the event of a dispute, a risk mitigation process will be established, including: initial evaluation of the entre- preneurial venture’s business plan; ongoing monitoring of the entrepreneur and the venture; regular meetings with the entrepreneur and the venture; and periodic reporting by the entrepreneur and the venture through meetings and calls; mandatory meetings in case of late payment; and positive incen- tives for early repayment. The venture ecosystems in both regions are relatively small: In the case of bad faith by a venture, the venture ecosystem will be able to exert pressure on the venture to repay its grant or to cease operations in future. The venture ecosystems in both regions are relatively small: In the case of bad faith by a venture, the venture ecosystem will be able to exert pressure on the venture to repay its grant or to cease operations in future.

#### 129. Repayments will be made by the ventures to Tec de Monterrey and IPEF. IPEF will systematically monitor and process anticipated repayments in Years 3-6 in the framework of the 5-year West Africa ecosystem development program. In the case of late payments, the venture will be required to continue to be accepted according to the agreement repayment schedule for ventures. Reflecting the above, the grant instrument provides that IPEF will systematically monitor and process anticipated repayments in Years 3-6 by its financial resources. Financial modeling (Year 1) indicates that repayments of up to EUR 200 million in Years 3–5 in West Africa, repayments of up to EUR 300 million in Years 3–7 after program start; in East Africa, repayments of EUR 1.1 million in Years 4-6.

### 130. In Latin America, repayments will be used to implement additional community building and idea- tion activities, for the purpose of further strengthening the local climate innovation ecosystem and broadening the pool of entrepreneurs with access to IPEF’s grant funding. The repayment funds for community building and ideation activities will encourage outreach and capacity-building measures for entrepreneurs in the CLIMATE INNOVATION LABS (CILs) and other incubators, accelerators, and business schools that are implemented by Tec de Monterrey itself or by a procured party, at the discretion of Tec de Monterrey. No repayments will be used to implement any capacity-building measures for entrepreneurs in the CILs; no repayments will be used to implement any capacity-building measures for individual entrepreneurs. GIZ will not need to rein-in objection vis-à-vis regarding the broadening of the pool of entrepreneurs with access to IPEF’s grant funding.

### 131. Use of repayments in West Africa will follow the same logic as in Latin America, with one difference: repayments up to EUR 300 million during Years 3–5 after program start; in East Africa, repayments up to EUR 200 million during Years 4-6 after program start. The IPEF Entity budget of IPEF incorporates this amount. Repayments above EUR 300 million will be used to implement the aforementioned community building and ideation activities for entrepreneurs in the CILs, as well as for other incubators, accelerators, and business schools that are implemented by Tec de Monterrey itself or a procured party.

### 132. In both regions, the amounts raised by the Executing Entities through the repayment of grants under the regional programs will be used to implement additional community building and ideation activities. These amounts will be spent by the Executing Entities themselves in dedication and community building activities.

B.S. Justification for GCF funding request

### B.5.1 Additionality

33. Meeting the objectives of the Paris Agreement will require a shift from business as usual, driven in large part by a high level of entrepreneurial activity and innovation. Climate ventures in Latin America and other regions are often constrained by their limited access to capital (substantial climate mitigation potential). GCF resources will be used to address identified barriers to climate venture growth, including the lack of access to finance. These barriers may not necessarily require GCF intervention to overcome them. The additionality of GCF intervention is thus clear without such an assessment. However, it should also be noted that the impact of GCF funding on associated GHG impact mitigation – will not materialize or will materialize only over a much longer time period than the project duration.

134. In order to reach the key objectives of bringing high potential climate ventures to the market, the design of the CATALISTT initiative includes: (i) competitive entry of ventures into the pre-acceleration and co-investment programme; (ii) technical assistance to ventures in the pre-acceleration phase to identify their highest-potential, most promising ventures are admitted; (iii) intensive technical assistance to climate ven- tures during the acceleration phase, including: (a) provision of business development support (technical, financial, marketing, staffing, etc.); (b) climate impact assessment (mitigation and, where rele- vant, adaptation); (c) access to market information; (d) access to technical assistance (e.g., legal support, sonnel, consultants, market surveys, etc.); and (iv) capacity building of CATALISTT initiative stakehold- ers, including the provision of training for entrepreneurs and business development support personnel to ensure high-quality delivery of technical assistance to ventures, engagement with market develop- ment stakeholders, and access to a robust network of climate venture investors.

135. Successfully navigating climate ventures through the Series A funding milestone will serve to open up financ- ing for their future growth. But it will also serve to enhance investor confidence in the regional climate sector, which is critical given the limited number of climate-focused investors and the lack of a support network in the regions, all of which will serve as a positive feedback to further market growth.

### B.5.2 Concessionality

136. A quantitative estimate of the level of concessionality of the pre-acceleration and acceleration grants is intended to be provided by the GCF through an analysis of the financial risk profile for ventures in both the pre-accelerated and accelerated. Such analysis would entail computing the financial BRRR (for a prospec- tive exit) for each venture, based on the expected revenue growth resulting from with and without the pre-acceleration and/or acceleration grant, based on assumptions such as:

• For the scenario of GCF grant:
    * The amount of equity injected by investors over one or more funding rounds.
    * The expected revenue growth resulting from the venture’s participation in the pre-acceleration and/or acceleration programme, which would entail estimating how much of the revenue, profit, and cash flow generated by the venture will be reinvested into the business.
    * The expected proceeds from an IPO (sale) of the company, which, in turn, would depend on the growth prospects and profitability of the business at the point of exit.
    * A minimum return to investors.

• For the scenario with no GCF grant:
    * The amount of equity injected by investors over one or more funding rounds.
    * The expected revenue growth resulting from the venture’s participation in the pre-acceleration and/or acceleration programme, which would entail estimating how much of the revenue, profit, and cash flow generated by the venture will be reinvested into the business.


---
**Page 72**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 68

128. The analysis would need to be undertaken for each venture in a model portfolio of – in the case of the acceleration programme – 60 accelerated ventures across the two regions and based on a realistic mix of both equity and debt financing. Any significant deviation from this would undermine the robustness of the analysis or, worse, make it malleable to a desired outcome.

128. Instead, the justification of the use of grants relies on: (i) the analysis of the financing gap faced by companies in each region; (ii) the need for additional capital to scale up and grow in emerging environments in Latin America and West Africa; (iii) the feedback received from the regional Exe- cutive Directors of the Green Climate Fund regarding the need for grants in these two regions; (iv) feedback received from VC funds investing in the two regions; and (v) in the case of the acceleration programme, the need to provide a financial incentive to VCs.

• In West Africa, start-up and growth companies have access to limited sources of long-term capital. VC activity has seen a significant increase in sub-Saharan Africa over the past 5 years, but largely avoided the Francophone West Africa region. Only a few FDI (foreign direct investment) deals have been made by VCs in this region. However, this VC capital has largely avoided the Francophone West Africa region. Only a few FDI (foreign direct investment) deals have been made by VCs in this region.

• In Latin America, climate venture also have access to limited sources of long-term capital. VC activity has seen a significant increase in Latin America. VC capital, however, is predominantly focused on the larger cities and tends to be concentrated in the largest companies. This capital is largely avoided by VCs operating in the region. Also, VC capital is highly concentrated in the largest venture funds, which are typically based in the United States or Europe.

• In Argentina, impact funds are active in Latin America and they represent a pool of capital which enters the market through private equity (PE) vehicles. These PE funds have been active in the region, their target companies are more mature than those targeted by VCs and they focus on climate change. While a number of PE funds have been active in the region, their target companies are more mature than those targeted by VCs.

• Bank financing is another source of capital for climate ventures that graduate from the GCF acceleration programme. Banks are unlikely sources of capital for climate ventures that graduate from the GCF acceleration programme because they typically do not provide equity or debt financing with a high cashflow profile and considering the reluctance of Latin American banks to lend to MSMEs.

• Similarly, IPOs are an realistic source of funding for companies at such an early stage of development.

• The start-ups targeted by commercial accelerators such as Y Combinator in Silicon Valley operate primarily on a subscription basis. They provide a platform for entrepreneurs to pitch their ideas (in- ventors), fintech offering the potential to scale up user bases and revenues very quickly – a prop- erty that makes them attractive to VCs. However, these companies are typically not suitable as operators in sectors that often require significant capital investment, have features specific to the country or region where they operate, and/or have limited access to markets. In addition, they do not meet the needs of vulnerable and/or low-income communities (e.g., smaller farmers). These repre- sent significant barriers for VCs investing in these companies.

• In recognition of the additional risks and barriers involved in investing in climate innovation and the need to provide a financial incentive to VCs to invest in this sector, it is important that funds that will operate the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes will not receive equity stakes as part of their funding.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 | Page 69

## B. Exit Strategy

### B.5.1 Exit Strategy

The CATALIST® Initiative seeks to catalyze and create in private sector investment. The Initiative's leveraged value (excluding the value of goods and services sold due to the climate variant) is approximately 10x. Expressed another way, the vast majority of the impact of the CATALIST® Initiative is at scale. This means that the initiative will not only have a significant impact on the private sector but also in terms of the number of jobs created.

### B.5.2 Sustainability

Sustainability of CATALIST® Initiative impacts must be assured in a number of ways:

1. Creation of a set of high-potential, highly climate-capable ventures.
   - The CATALIST® Initiative will support 60 pre-seed (pre-acceleration) and 20 seed stage (acceleration) ventures in each region. These climate venture initiatives will be supported through various means such as: access to capital, mentorship, business skills training, business services, exposure to financiers, etc.
   - To move into the pre-seed and seed stages, these ventures must demonstrate a strong likelihood of success (e.g., having a viable business plan). This is expected to lead to better traction rates compared to the counterfactual. Specifically:
     * More climate ventures will reach Series A financing than would be the case in the no-CATALIST® Initiative baseline.
     * Climate ventures arriving at Series A decisions will be stronger due to the support they received from the CATALIST® Initiative, and as a result, will have higher chances of success. This is expected to lead to better traction rates compared to the counterfactual.
     * The number of climate ventures that fail after Series A funding will be lower than would be the case in the no-CATALIST® Initiative baseline.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 70

Using conservative, stakeholder-validated assumptions about the ventures' growth and survival prospects (e.g., assuming that just 6 ventures in Latin America achieve Series A financing and 3 receive Series B funding), the CATALYTIC FUND estimates that the climate ventures accelerated by the CATALYTIC FUND initiative in Latin America and West Africa will raise a total of approximately EUR 413 million of private finance over the programme's 20-year lifespan (see Annex A).

## Network, ecosystem and first-mover effects

The CATALYTIC FUND initiative’s barrier removal measures, net-of-costs, are expected to have significant network, ecosystem and first-mover effects. These will create a virtuous cycle of climate venture creation and growth, with the positive impacts creating successful ventures that act as inspirations and role models for subsequent climate en- terprises. The CATALYTIC FUND initiative’s barrier removal measures will also help to build the capacity of TNCs, IPEF and Impact Hub Africa (Executive Entities). All three institutions are already established, respected business pre-accelerators / accelerators in Latin America and West Africa respectively.

## Strengthened capacity of TNC de Monterrey, IPEF and Impact Hub Africa

The CATALYTIC FUND initiative’s barrier removal measures will also help to build the capacity of TNCs, IPEF and Impact Hub Africa (Executive Entities). All three institutions are already established, respected business pre-accelerators / accelerators in Latin America and West Africa respectively. The CATALYTIC FUND initiative’s barrier removal measures will also help to build the capacity of TNCs, IPEF and Impact Hub Africa (Executive Entities).

## Strong emphasis on women-led ventures

The inclusion of women-led ventures as an integral element of CATALYTIC FUND initiatives is essential to ensure that the programme maximizes its impact. Multiple studies concur that women-led companies in developing countries tend to be disadvantaged in terms of access to finance, markets and other resources compared with their male counterparts, especially in sectors such as hospitality and retail.124 But, when these constraints are statistically controlled for, women-led com- panies perform better than men-led companies on a number of key performance indicators (KPIs). Women are more successful at reaching market segments, such as households and services, where women are the primary decision-makers.125 The CATALYTIC FUND initiative will therefore focus on identifying KPIs and metrics to maximize the potential for men and women to participate on an equal footing means there will be a strong emphasis on women-led ventures in the regions targeted by the CATALYTIC FUND initiative, through the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes but also through the changing mindset and practices of TNCs, IPEF and Impact Hub Africa. The CATALYTIC FUND initiative is testing two regions where female entrepreneurs are frequently overlooked.

## Use of reputable grants

The use of reputable grants for seed-stage climate ventures in the regional accelerators will help to build trust with potential investors and partners in publicly funded venture acceleration programmes and underlines the market- investment oriented mindset of the CATALYTIC FUND initiative. The CATALYTIC FUND initiative will also support the development of additional sourcing activities, thereby extending the size and reach of the climate venture ecosystem in both regions.
