## D.1 Impact potential

### D.1.1 Emission Reductions Estimation Methodology

150. When GIZ embarked on developing the CATALYST! Initiative in mid-2019, there was no methodology available to estimate emission reductions from a portfolio of projects and programmes that would be implemented over time. To provide the GCF with an informed, robust and transparent climate impact estimate, GIZ partnered with Climate-KIC, a leading European innovation hub for climate change solutions, to develop a methodology called MORS-E: Model for Regional Start-up Ecosystem Impact. Full details are provided in Annexes 22b and Annexes 23.

151. The Avoided Emissions Potential (AEP) – defined as the difference in lifecycle GHG emissions between an on-boarding baseline solution (e.g., an internal combustion engine vehicle) and a climate venture’s product (e.g., an electric vehicle) – was used to estimate emission reductions from each of the 33 climate ventures. The entrepreneurial initiatives in the European Union for which climate impact (i.e., emission reductions) was an eligibility criterion were selected as climate ventures. These initiatives were assessed against the MORS-E model built for scaling a climate venture database. Additionally, this allowed access to detailed information on a large sample of current ventures, which would otherwise be hard to achieve in a coherent manner, as noted by other studies.[18]

152. Twenty-four ventures were randomly selected from a pool of 304 EU ventures and were complemented by an additional nine ventures that were not included in the random selection but were considered particularly relevant for this report. For these 33 climate ventures were reviewed by Climate-KIC and validated by an expert third party.

153. The selected climate ventures were each assigned to represent a venture archetype.[20] An archetype refers to a group of projects or programmes that share common characteristics, such as the type of technology (e.g., energy generation or + mobility with low-emission transport) or alternative systems within forestry and land use. Each archetype was developed in close collaboration between GIZ, Climate-KIC, the GCF’s project development indicators, Archetype categorization was participatory[21], Climate-KIC, Executing Entities and VC funds. Table 2 provides an overview of each archetype and its associated climate ventures. The archetype types used: Annexes 24d provides an overview of the types of climate ventures in each GCF Result Area and in each archetype.

| GCF Result Area | Archetypes |
| --- | --- |
| Energy access and power generation | Alternative energy sources, energy storage, renewable energy systems, small-scale solar, mini-grids |
| Life emission transport | Electric vehicles, electric buses, electric scooters |
| Buildings, cities, industries and appliances | Alternative materials, energy efficiency, smart city solutions, smart manufacturing, sustainable construction |
| Forestry and land use | Agroforestry, agroecology, climate-smart agriculture, conservation of natural resources, consumption, clean water/eater availability, clear air, sanitation, waste management, wetlands |
| Water | Water supply and sanitation, water resource management |

154. Economic data (GDP and local market data) were used to identify the type and size of the economic sector associated with each venture archetype in each region. The total GHG emissions covered by the relevant ventures

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 75

### MARKET-DRIVEN EMISSIONS REDUCTION

The market-driven emissions reduction is determined using two key variables: the sector and sub-sector emissions (QSE) based on C4T GHG emissions data, and the percentage of addressable sub-sector emissions that reflect the *maximum* amount of emissions reductions that can be achieved in a given year. This percentage is used to represent the upper bound on emission savings: the maximum Avoided Emissions Potential or AEP.

#### 155. In practice, the total emissions in the sub-sector typically greatly exceed those in a venture's AEP. For example, for a typical early-stage venture in the bioplastic sector, it would be possible to achieve emission reductions below 0.03% of the maximum attainable in theory—and at best, 0.02%.

#### 156. The Year 1 Avoided Emissions Potential (AEP) of a climate venture—or, at a higher level of aggregation, an entire sub-sector—represents the emissions reductions that can be achieved by a venture or sector in its first year per functional unit multiplied by the number of functional units sold in Year 1. In a typical group of early-stage ventures, this AEP ranges from less than one tonne to several tonnes of CO2e and with the occasional high-performing outlier (impact venture).

#### 157. Because the AEP represents the entire lifecycle emissions reductions of a product or service (i.e., the total emissions reductions achieved across all years), it is not possible to determine the year in which an individual venture's AEP is acting to produce emission reductions achieved equally across the expected lifetime of that product or service. The Year 1 AEP, therefore, represents the maximum amount of emissions reductions that can be achieved by a venture in Year 3 of the CATALYST Initiative, for example, is distributed equally over the 10-year product lifetime of the archetype-specific product/service life cycle. This means that the emissions reductions associated with the first year's sales of an archetype-specific bioplastic or recycled cardboard are distributed over just one year. The archetype-specific product/service life cycle represents a reasonable upper bound on the expected lifetime of the archetype-specific product or service, and this annualisation process has no impact on the overall mitigation gains; it does enable emission reductions to be calculated for each year in which the venture is active during both the CATALYST Initiative implementation period and the CATALYST Initiative.

#### 158. The lifespan AEP of the CATALYST Initiative is determined by a number of variables:
- The expected average lifetime of a company (i.e., the CATALYST Initiative's "reference period"). A lifespan of 20 years has been chosen because this is approximately the average expected lifetime of a listed company in the UK. This represents an upper bound on the emissions reductions that can be achieved by a venture supported by the CATALYST Initiative. It should be noted, however, that the archetype-specific product/service life cycle will only rarely exceed this time; but a small minority (15%) may exceed 20-year lifetimes.
- If no expected lifetime is available for a company or its products/services, then it is assumed that they will disappear entirely during this time. Its goods/services may continue to be sold by an acquiring firm or its founders may go on to found another venture in the CATALYST Initiative. The archetype-specific product/service life cycle programme influence period, of whatever duration, is itself considered to be a conservative assumption.

#### 159. The effect of the 20-year lifespan on the estimation of emission reductions is three-fold:
- (i) CATALYST Initiative ventures will have a greater opportunity to reduce emissions in the first year of operation than would otherwise be possible;
- (ii) climate venturers supported in Year 1 of the CATALYST Initiative have a marginally greater opportunity to contribute to the overall reduction target for the CATALYST Initiative; and
- (iii) the emission reductions associated with goods/services sold by CATALYST Initiative-supported ventures will continue beyond their first year of operation. For example, if an archetype-specific appliance is sold by a venture in Year 1 that has a 10-year expected product lifetime it would only contribute emissions savings to the CATALYST Initiative for 6 years up to and including Year 20 rather than for the full 10-year product lifetime.

#### 160. For example, software for power saving can be run in every power station; the equipment supplying the software was—downstream—is able to achieve the maximum possible market share (i.e., all power stations); it could save 100% of the electricity sector emissions. In contrast, a typical lighting or HVAC system is only able to achieve a small fraction of its potential emissions savings because it is not installed in every building; and even if it were, it would be subject to market share limitations.

The number of climate ventures included in the analysis. The CATALYST Initiative will provide technical and financial support to all 96 pre-seed climate ventures in each region (i.e. 120 ventures in total) through its regional acceleration programmes. These are expected to generate a further 350–400 new climate ventures (in total) within their regional acceleration programmes. Estimating the future climate impact of pre-seed climate ventures is challenging, as they have little actual market exposure – and will inevitably necessitate considerable speculation and guesswork. The number of climate ventures that graduate from the regional acceleration programmes are included in the mitigation impact analysis.148


The types of climate ventures supported by the CATALYST Initiative. In Latin America, the regional acceleration programme is expected to support 30–50 pre-seed climate ventures (i.e. 60–100 new climate ventures). These ventures are either in the early stages of development or already have a minimum viable product (MVP), and no significant revenues and/or profits. In West Africa, the regional acceleration programme is expected to support 35–45 pre-seed climate ventures (i.e. 70–90 new climate ventures). These are either in the early stages of development or already have a minimum viable product (MVP), but are unable to grow to their full potential for reasons that already generate revenues, and potentially profits, but are unable to grow to their full potential for reasons such as lack of market demand, limited access to finance, or other constraints. The anticipated proportion of growth ventures in the acceleration programme will be approximately 50%. Both types of venture (i.e. pre-seed and growth) have the potential to generate climate benefits through products/services that offer climate mitigation benefits. However, the growth companies differ from the start-ups in having already generated revenues and/or profits.


The failure schedule of each CATALYST Initiative-supported venture: i.e. the number of years after graduation from the regional acceleration programme at which each venture ceases to operate. Until the first year of operation (i.e. until Year 1), the venture is considered to generate zero emissions reductions. Ongoing use of products/services sold before Year 1 will not be counted as emissions reductions, even if they are climate mitigation technologies. The failure schedule for ventures in Latin America and West Africa is shown below. Ventures that fail in Year 10 or later cease to generate any emissions reductions from the product lifetime up to and including Year 20, even though the venture is no longer selling new products.


The success schedule of each CATALYST Initiative-supported venture: i.e. the percentage of such ventures that are still operating at a given point in time (i.e. until Year 1, until Year 5, etc.). The difference between the failure and success schedules is the proportion of ventures that continue to operate beyond their point of failure (e.g. for long-lived ventures), up until Year 20. The success and failure schedules are presented below. Ventures in Latin America and West Africa have different success and failure schedules because: i) there are fewer pre-seed climate ventures in Latin America than in West Africa, ii) the funding sources differ between regions (e.g. Latin America has more debt financing), iii) there is a greater proportion of growth ventures in Latin America compared to West Africa, iv) there is less access to finance for pre-seed climate ventures in Latin America and West Africa.


The growth rate of each CATALYST Initiative-supported venture: i.e. the evolution of its annual sales performance (market share) over time. The growth rates are calculated as a percentage change from one year to the next, with the first year being the point of venture failure. The annual growth rates that populate the model have been calibrated with respect to the observed growth rates for pre-seed climate ventures in Latin America and West Africa – see below. These differentiated in the MORES model in two respects:

* Region: Latin America vs. West Africa
* Venture survival: a venture’s growth prospects are related to its survival prospects, such that a short-lived venture is expected to grow more slowly than one with a longer life expectancy within its life. The annual growth rates of ventures that survive for 1–3 years are discounted by 20%, and ventures that survive for 4–6 years by 10%, relative to those of ventures that survive for 7 or more years.


Some of the ventures that graduate from pre-acceleration programme may then advance to the acceleration programme – subject to completing entry requirements. Indeed, this activity expected and welcome as it will ensure a supply of high-quality candidates for acceleration programmes. The number of climate ventures that graduate primarily from the pre-acceleration programmes are included in the 148 Debt instruments are considered for growth companies only.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 77

## Ventures fail for a variety of reasons.

The failure rate in early iterations of the MOISE model ventures continued to enjoy sustained, unimodal growth until the point when they suddenly failed. However, this was considered unrealistic and data-driven analysis is used to represent gradual deceleration in a venture's growth in its years leading up to a venture's failure.

### Causes of Failure

- **Run out of cash:** Failed to raise new capital (38%)
- **Business model:** No longer viable (25%)
- **Got cut-out (20%)**
- **Failed to scale (19%)**
- **Regulatory / legal challenges (18%)**
- **Price competition (14%)**
- **Not the right team (14%)**

Some of these factors (such as regulatory or legal challenges or internal team dynamics) may, indeed, manifest themselves in a venture's failure to reach its ultimate value, rather than a gradually deteriorating growth rate. However, other factors (such as cash burn, market competition and market saturation) are likely to be more gradual in their impact on the venture's growth.

The GCF attribution coefficient is not all of the climate impact achieved by the CATALYT!5 Initiative sub-projects. The GCF attribution coefficient is calculated as the amount of GCF budget funding relative to the total VC and other financing expected to be leveraged by the programme, i.e., the GCF contribution to the GHG impact associated with each venture. This means that the GCF attribution coefficient is equal to the ratio of the amount of funding spent on the ventures by the GCF compared with the amount of spending by other parties (e.g., VCs and private equity investors). The total budget for the CATALYT!5 Initiative is Euro 413 million, hence the GCF attribution coefficient is 6.5%. (See Annex 22c for detailed calculations.)

### D.1.2 Model Parametrisation

For the purpose of providing an ex ante estimate of the CATALYT!5 Initiative's mitigation impact, in-depth interviews were conducted with a wide range of stakeholders including representatives from governments, prominent officials, international organisations and donors – held in Latin America and West Africa. These discussions provided insights into the climate change challenges faced by these regions, the potential climate change or disaster risks, start-ups vs growth companies – of the 30 climate ventures that will graduate from each region, their expected mitigation impact (in terms of avoided emissions, avoided costs, increased value of the venture, and) the annual growth rates of the ventures – all over a 20-year influence period. These parameters were then used to estimate the total mitigation impact for each region. The estimates are now considered to be: (i) extremely robust and if (ii) the low (conservative) end of empirical expectations. These parameter values and assumptions are provided in Annex 22b.

The CATALYT!5 Initiative establishes a clear pathway from pre-seed investment through to growth funding, including seed-stage financing for climate ventures, and, however, it does so while: (i) establishing a robust exit strategy for the GCF and (ii) making minimal demands on GCF funds.

D.1 Emission Reduction Impact

180. The CATALYST® Initiative is expected to reduce 58 MCO2e of greenhouse gas emissions during its 20 year lifespan (inflation period). This will be achieved through the sale of low-emission products/services by the 60 climate venture catalysts that are selected for the initiative. The CATALYST® Initiative will screen and nurture these climate ventures, providing them with the technical, financial, market and other resources they need to grow and scale their business. The CATALYST® Initiative also provides opportunities from a VC perspective. This VC equity investment (and, in the case of growth companies, debt investment) is expected to generate returns for investors that are commensurate with the risk taken on. The majority of cases, is expected to occur after the end of the 5th GCF support. Accordingly a GCF attribution coefficient of 0.8 has been applied to the CATALYST® Initiative’s total emission reduction impact, which is fully attributable to the GCF itself. Table S summarizes the modeling results after this GCF attribution has been applied.

181. For Latin America, the cumulative total GCF-attributeable mitigation impact by the end of the CATALYST® Initiative implementation period (Year 5) is 141 MCO2e and 1.7 MCO2e by the end of the initiative lifespan (Year 20), for a total of 142.7 MCO2e.

182. The breakdown of emission reductions by GCF Result Area is provided in Table 10. As with the mitigation estimates, this breakdown should be considered to be indicative rather than definitive, as there are considerable uncertainties associated with the attribution of emissions reductions across different regions. In both regions, whose identities and characteristics will only be known when they are admitted into the regional accel- oration program, it is expected that a significant proportion of emission reductions will come from the energy sector. Over the course of emission reductions will evolve each year over the 20-year programme lifespan (Table 10 shows the breakdown for Year 5 and Year 20).

183. In Latin America, energy access and power generation is expected to contribute the bulk of emission reductions, due to a combination of factors. Structurally, ventures providing energy products and services have wide applica- bility across the region. The sector also contains a number of climate change mitigation opportunities that also contain two 'impact anchors' that manage to survive for measurable periods and thereby sustain their emi- sion reduction impact over time. These are: (1) the ability to provide energy access to previously unconnected or underserved populations, which is an important element of venture finance and such probabilistic factors cannot be ignored; and (2) the ability to provide a reliable supply of power to existing users, which can be achieved through the use of FPU(s) result area, due to this sector's economic importance in the region. In both regions, there is a small but significant proportion of emission reductions that come from energy efficiency.

184. The contribution of transport is predicted to be negligible in both regions. This may be partly a reflection of the challenges presented by the sector. For example, it can take several years for new ventures providing transport services during the 'learning curve' period to start up in practice. However, this is not universally true: smaller-scale ventures that are able to provide a service that is more cost-effective than existing alternatives (e.g., shared taxis built around car-pooling), could represent more promising opportunities for ventures. Moreover, the negligible contribution of transport to emission reductions can be explained by the fact that the GCF attribution coeffi- cient applied in this modeling exercise is 0.2. This is a conservative attribution coefficient, given the strong- lling prediction that transport ventures would account for approximately 20% of the ventures in the regional accel- oration programme.

185. Further details on the transport modelling result are provided in Annex 29a. The negligible emission reductions attributed to transport is partly a chance outcome in the MORSE model, due to the presence of 5 successful ventures that have been selected for the acceleration program. However, this does not mean that no emis- sion can be constructed in MORSE that generate solutions in which transport is a considerably more impactful result area. More fundamentally, however, the transport sector is currently under-served by the MORSE model.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 79

The MORSE model generates emission reduction estimates by using an underlying database of 51 "real-world" ventures, which are used – singly or in multiples – to construct higher level analyses. These ventures were selected based on their ability to provide high-quality data and the result that high-quality data was only available for 3 transport ventures: electric powerboats, smart public transport systems, and electric buses. The MORSE model uses this database of real-world ventures as a reference point against which it tests the data that the MORSE model uses to generate transport mitigation impacts tends to under-estimate such impacts.

166. Improvement of the MORSE model, including expansion of the underlying venture database, is planned under Sub-Activity 3.1.1. A key emphasis of this work will be on strengthening the breadth of transport sector coverage.

157. It should be noted that selection of ventures into admission to the CATALYST® Initiative's pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes will not suffer from these MORSE-related deficiencies. Assessment of these ventures against the criteria for admission is based upon a combination of qualitative and quantitative factors, which will be known at the time of screening / selection. The challenge that arises with the use of the MORSE tool is that the shortcomings are not known – and hence no mitigation techniques need to be used in their place.

### D.1.4 Sensitivity Analysis

168. Clearly, the central estimate is subject to considerable uncertainty. If, for example, during CATALYST® Initiative implementation one or more ventures perform markedly better (or worse) than expected, the GHG impact of the GCF-Attributable CATALYST® Initiative Mitigation Gap could be significantly different from that which currently defines the global GHG estimate; a downward GHG estimate has also been calculated. This estimate removes the top 10% of ventures with the highest projected GHG impacts and replaces them with the bottom 10%. This is considered to be an extremely conservative scenario – there seems little plausible reason why it should be the case that all or most of these ventures perform better than expected, but this calculation provides a useful "almost-worst-case" baseline against which the central estimate can be compared. The downside estimate provides a useful "almost-best-case" baseline.

Table 11: Downside Estimate of the GCF-Attributable CATALYST® Initiative Mitigation Gap

| Region | Programme Implementation Period (Years) | Programme Lifespan (Years) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Latin America | 29.1 | 470.2 |
| West Africa | 35.8 | 600.0 |
| Total | 63.2 | 1,066.2 |

169. The downside mitigation impact – I = 1 MCCHE = ~25% is the size of the central estimate, which shows the disproportionate effect that a small number of ventures can have on the overall GHG impact of the GCF-Attributable CATALYST® Initiative. This mitigates the skewed financial impact that a small number of ventures typically exert on GCF funding. The downside estimate provides an important perspective against which to judge whether it is worthwhile to compensate for the under-performance of a much larger number of ventures in a VC's portfolio. Nonetheless, even the downside estimate represents a substantial climate impact from a GCF perspective.

170. The downslope estimate has been augmented by probabilistic – Monte Carlo – modeling, in which 50 random survival schedules for the 30 accelerated climate variants in each region have been drawn through repeated sampling from a normal distribution.

171. Figure 12 is interpreted as follows: cumulative mitigation impact is shown on the left hand side and annual mitigation impact is shown on the right hand side, Latin America is shown in the top charts, West Africa in the bottom charts. The downslope estimate – which is the lowest of all possible estimates – is shown in yellow. It corresponds to probabilistic scenarios in the first six years, and pessimistic thereafter. Expressed another way, the probabilistic endpoint of the downslope estimate is the most likely outcome for mitigation impact over the short term; it is expected in the longer-term. Over the course of the CATALIST Initiative’s lifespan, the downside estimate – which is the lowest of all possible estimates – will become progressively smaller relative to optimistic scenarios and mitigation impact is likely to be higher, and potentially substantially higher than predicted by the downside estimate.

172. Table 12 provides a complementary means of contextualising the downside estimate against the probabilistic simulation. It shows the downslope estimate as a percentage of all possible estimates from the probabilistic simulations. Thus, even in Year 1 the Latin American downside estimate is already in the top percentile; i.e. is amongst the highest of all possible estimates. The West African downside estimate is also in the top percentile for the first four years, starting in the 95th percentile in Year 1. The downslope estimate of this programme’s mitigation impact is, in other words, very unlikely to be lower than any other possible outcome. By contrast, the Latin American downside estimate is in the bottom 15% of impact estimates and the West African estimate is amongst the lowest 1% of estimates. Thus, the CATALIST Initiative is expected to be more optimistic than conservative, with confidence correspondingly high that the CATALIST Initiative will significantly outperform it.

# D2. Paradigm shift potential

## D2.1 Potential for Scaling Up and Replication

173. The design of the CATALYST Initiative is flexible and allows for scaling to additional regions, sub-regions or countries. For example, it could be applied in other emerging climate finance programs—one for designing markets (Latin America) and one for frontier markets (West Africa)—the CATALYST Initiative will address different regional characteristics and market dynamics. The design of the CATALYST Initiative is also flexible enough to be replicable in different markets.

The frontier market version will target economies with an established VC scene and growing engagement of catalytic capital, but with limited dynamics in the climate solutions space and limited geographical diversity.

The frontier market version is designed for dynamic developing countries with enormous investment needs, where there are significant opportunities for climate solutions and where behavioral and impact VCs have an array of daunting barriers to investment.

As the CATALYST Initiative will initially focus on two distinct regions, the lessons learned in these regions can inform scaling up or replication elsewhere.

## D2.2 Potential for Knowledge-Sharing and Learning

174. The CATALYST Initiative’s knowledge management plan is provided in Annex 23a. Providing new opportunities for the climate venture sector in Latin America and West Africa will generate important lessons to be used in successful scaling of the CATALYST Initiative, as well as other initiatives that aim to build a stronger climate venture ecosystem in the regions; and boosting confidence in investments that are currently being made by VCs in these regions. The CATALYST Initiative will provide opportunities for public-private and public-to-public interactions. The CATALYST Initiative will place considerable emphasis on: (i) collecting and sharing knowledge about the lessons learned from the CATALYST Initiative, including through workshops and accelerators in the context of assessing and shaping venture climate impact; and VC funds in the context of applying their learnings to support the scaling up of the CATALYST Initiative beyond the end of GCF involvement.

175. The CATALYST Initiative will facilitate continuous exchange of knowledge about lessons learned among stakeholders, including through the regional programs. All knowledge products such as annual impact reports that collect and analyze data from actual venture cohorts, will be made available online, free of charge, in every region where the CATALYST Initiative is implemented. In addition to these regular updates, a feedback loop, learning from the CATALYST Initiative climate venture impacts can then be embedded into the wider climate venture support programs (e.g., through regional accelerators) and beyond.

176. Knowledge products to be developed by the CATALYST Initiative include but are not limited to:

- Annual impact reports and benchmarking: The CATALYST Initiative will hold one climate year in each participating country, with delivery partners and entrepreneur participants included into the global Climate Innovation Week (CIW) held annually at COP25. The CATALYST Initiative will also hold a CIW event for each partner every year, in order to coincide with Climate KIC’s Global Climathon Week. Evaluation reports for each dimension can be provided as inputs to future subsequent cohort design and to contribute to the overall learning agenda of the CATALYST Initiative.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 83

## D.2 Contribution to the Regulatory Framework and Policies

### D.2.4 Contribution to the Regulatory Framework and Policies

#### Government actions to create or enhance markets for technologies that reduce GHG emissions are a critical element of the technological innovation process, and their absence will lead to innovation lag. Latin America and West Africa have been identified as key regions in which to focus on these efforts. The GCF is committed to supporting policies and the emergence of venture support systems (e.g., Start-Up Acts) in three of the target African countries. In addition, the GCF will provide technical assistance to governments in Latin America and West Africa based on these positive developments by creating capacities, tools, regional networks and partnerships.

#### The CATALYST Initiative is well aligned with the policy context, notably (but not by means exclusively) in terms of promoting a more enabling environment for MMEs. The GCF will support the development of policies that prioritize MMEs as engines of economic growth and innovation. While the CATALYST Initiative does not have strong direct regulatory implications, it is expected to contribute indirectly to the creation of an enabling environment for ventures, as well as a more granular understanding of barriers and opportunities in the region. Case-studies and other evidence will be collected and disseminated with a view to contributing to informed policy making.

### D.2.5 Contribution to Climate-Resilient Development Pathways

#### The CATALYST Initiative is mission-focused. The objective is to reduce GHG emissions by identifying and supporting climate ventures that offer the greatest GHG abatement / avoidance potential. However, it is acknowledged that there are other important co-benefits associated with these ventures (e.g., adaptation). These include: energy-efficient cooling as a response to warming temperatures, off-grid renewable energy solutions as a means of mitigating the impacts of climate change on vulnerable communities, and innovative approaches to agriculture that simultaneously reduce emissions and reduce exposure to climate-induced hazards (e.g. soil erosion, pests, diseases). The CATALYST Initiative will also seek to identify and support other ventures for acceleration and/or co-investment. In addition, it is expected that the GCF will provide co-financing opportunities for venture accelerators and seed-stage investors. Ventures in this category are likely to include: entities that provide climate-resilient adaptation solutions (e.g., water management, disaster risk reduction), as well as other ecosystem stakeholders (e.g., other accelerators, VC firms) who will be provided with co-investment opportunities.

#### Sub-Activity 1.1.2 in Latin America and Sub-Activity 2.1.1.2 in West Africa: Sustainable development

### D.3 Sustainable Development Goals

#### The CATALYST Initiative directly contributes to the following SDGs:

* **SDG 5: Gender equality:** The CATALYST Initiative will address barriers that women entrepreneurs face, including the cost of accessing the informal sector, limited knowledge

### D3.2 Environmental Co-Benefits

- **D3.2.1 Improved land-use and soil conservation practices**: Ventures with significant impact on land use (e.g., through improved agricultural processes, environmental restoration) will have a significant environmental impact going beyond decarbonization.
  - Reduction of greenhouse gas emissions with direct process impacts: by reducing energy consumption and contamination in economic activities; and improving access to safe drinking water and sanitation.

### D3.2 Social Co-Benefits

- **D3.2.1 Improved livelihoods of communities through the provision of locally-based, low-emission climate solutions**: the same ventures that provide co-benefits for the environment also provide their users with the opportunity to reduce their carbon footprints as well as improve their livelihoods. This could be achieved by increasing energy efficiency, reduced travel times or improved product reliability, depending on the nature of the climate solution.
  - Improved resilience capacities of communities: by deploying products and services that are climate-resilient and adaptive in sectors with strong impacts on livelihoods (e.g., agriculture, energy and transport).

D.3.4 Climate Co-Benefits

196. Private sector investment for climate solutions will strengthen the overall innovation ecosystem. This investment is key in a region like West Africa, for example, where one-fifth of the working-age population are starting businesses and investing in renewable energy (IRENA 2018) in countries such as Senegal, where SMEs represent nearly 90% of all companies and 50% of GDP (ILO).

197. Climate Co-Benefits will support the development of a climate innovation ecosystem by creating a network of climate entrepreneurs and investors. Moreover, the programme's support to venture formation will help to ensure that these jobs are accompanied by other benefits such as insurance, health and safety, etc.

D.3.5 Gender-Sensitive Development Benefits

197. Women represent 40% of the agricultural workforce and broader climate action. Women are significantly underrepresented among entrepreneurs and venture founders. The CATALIST® Initiative will put in place measures to achieve gender parity by 2025, including a dedicated gender advisor for each portfolio company. In addition, the criteria for the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes will equip all supported ventures – as well as other ecosystem actors such as incubators, accelerators, investors, and VC funds – with tools and frameworks to strengthen their gender aspects of their operations, and will build mentoring and support networks to encourage and guide women climate entrepreneurs.

D.4 Needs of recipient

### D.4.1 Climate Risk and Vulnerability

Latin America

188. The IPCC identifies the following key regional changes to the climate (10):

- **Extreme weather events:** These are increasing across the region and will continue to increase at rates greater than the global average. 2020 was the second hottest year in record for Latin America after 2019, with a temperature anomaly of +0.5°C compared to the long-term (1961-1990) mean over the mountain region of the world. This has resulted in the loss of a major source of hydrometeor for con-sumption and agriculture, which is expected to have significant economic impacts on the livelihoods of people in the region's fishing and tourism industries.
- **Drought:** Drought conditions are projected to change with shifting patterns of seasonal rainfall in precipitation in north-west and south-east South America, and decreases in north-east and south-west South America. This will have a major impact on water resources, particularly for agriculture, hydrometeorological and biodiversity.
- **Sea-level rise:** Sea-level rise is likely to continue in the eastern region around Central and South America, contributing to increased coastal flooding in low-lying areas and shoreline retreat along most coasts of the region. This will have a major impact on coastal communities and infrastructure (e.g., coastal urban centers) is additionally threatened by contamination of freshwater aquifers and the increasing salinity of rivers and lakes. The cost of sea-level rise for Latin America is estimated at USD 9.4 billion +1.2 million of annual mean losses for the region's 22 largest coastal cities.
- **Extreme weather events:** These are increasing across the region and will continue to increase at rates greater than the global average. 2020 was the second hottest year in record for Latin America after 2019, with a temperature anomaly of +0.5°C compared to the long-term (1961-1990) mean over the mountain region of the world. This has resulted in the loss of a major source of hydrometeor for con-sumption and agriculture, which is expected to have significant economic impacts on the livelihoods of people in the region's fishing and tourism industries.
- **Drought:** Drought conditions are projected to change with shifting patterns of seasonal rainfall in precipitation in north-west and south-east South America, and decreases in north-east and south-west South America. This will have a major impact on water resources, particularly for agriculture, hydrometeorological and biodiversity.

189. According to the World Bank (2017), Latin America is one of the most vulnerable regions in the world due to climate change, with climate-related disaster increasing in frequency and intensity. On average, approximately 1.1% of GDP is lost per year due to climate related disasters.

## WMO reports that 609 extreme events occurred between 2000 and 2017. These events include droughts and hurricanes, thunderstorms, hailstorms, blizzards, heavy snowfall, avalanches, coastal storms, floods, wildfires, heatwaves, and tsunamis. They caused significant displacement of people, numerous fatalities and significant economic losses.

### West Africa

#### 190. West Africa is considered one of the most vulnerable regions to climate change. Increasing temperatures, shifting rainfall patterns and sea-level rise are already affecting livelihoods, food security, and economic opportunities in the region. Climate change will likely exacerbate existing vulnerabilities such as poverty, conflict, food crises, water scarcity, extreme flooding and environmental degradation.

#### 191. Annex 20c provides details of historical and future climate change, as well as individual countries' climate risks and adaptation capabilities.
- **Temperatures**: A warming trend has been observed over the whole of sub-Saharan Africa since the 1960s. The most significant warming signals are from 2005 to 2014 in West Africa. Historical data suggest a rate of temperature increase of 2.3°C (1960-1980) and 3.9°C (1990-2010). By the end of this century, the projected warming is expected to reach 5.7°C in Burkina Faso and Mali, +4.2°C in Senegal and +3.6°C in Niger.
- **Precipitation**: The historical data suggest a rate of precipitation increase of -8% (1960-1980) and -10% (1990-2010). By the end of this century, the projected warming is expected to reach +3.5°C in Niger.
- **Sea Level Rise**: The historical data suggest a rate of sea level rise increase of 4 mm/year (1960-1980) and 7 mm/year (1990-2010). By the end of this century, the projected warming is expected to reach +3.5°C in Niger.

#### References
- IPCC (2021), Climate Change 2021: Impacts, Adaptation and Vulnerability.
- World Bank (2021), A Key Focus on Climate Change Impacts, Opportunities and Priorities for Latin America and the Caribbean.
- World Bank (2021), Promoting Climate Change Action in Latin America and the Caribbean. Results Briefs.
- World Bank (2014), Climate in West Africa Region Risk Profile.
- World Bank (2021), Climate Change Knowledge Forest: Western and Central Africa.

## Green Climate Fund

### Color Index: West Africa, Togo and Benin (125) This will be accompanied by the increased occurrence and duration of heat waves (+4-28 days) and hot days and nights. By the end of the century, under RCP 8.5, tropical West Africa is projected to experience a mean annual temperature increase of +3°C compared with pre-industrial levels (1860), with an increase in wet season rainfall by over 10% under RCP 8.5 for 2080-2100, relative to 1986-2005.

### Decadal variability and trends: The region has experienced a general drying trend since the mid-1970s with increased variability, with marked periods of decadal variability shifting toward interannual fluctuations. There has been an overall reduction in rainfall over most parts of West Africa (except for some areas such as northern Senegal, Burkina Faso, Mali and Côte d'Ivoire) by –6%, 4%–3% and 30% on years respectively – but there is a lack of consensus about the future direction of this trend. The projected changes are uncertain, with annual rainfall projected to reduce in the Western Sahel sub-region and increase along the Guinean coast.

### Sea level: West Africa is experiencing sea-level rise due to coastal erosion, with implications for coastal communities. In 2015, the sea level was +34 cm above its long-term average (1986-2010). In one year because of coastal degradation and erosion. In 2001–2010, relative to 1950/1960, the sea level was +70 cm in Côte d'Ivoire, +43 cm in Benin, +80 cm in Togo, +100 cm in Ghana and +100 cm in Nigeria. The projected increase is +20–150 cm by 2100.

### Land cover: West Africa has experienced a net loss of land cover due to deforestation and desertification. In the region, 30% of the land area is affected by desertification. Countries including Senegal, Côte d'Ivoire, Benin and Togo are projected to experience more severe impacts from climate change than other countries in West Africa.

### Extreme climate events: Extreme precipitation is becoming more frequent and intense, with its proportion of total rainfall increasing over the region. Climate models project a range of different trends in annual rainfall, but it is generally understood that having consistent data on extreme weather events will be critical to understanding how these events are changing across the region. The 2015/2016 El Niño event was one of the strongest recorded since 1950 and had a significant impact on West Africa.

### Adaptation: Reducing vulnerability in West Africa requires adaptation at multiple scales, sectors and countries. Reductions are projected in per capita food availability as median crop yields fall by 0–2% per decade, while demand for crops is expected to increase by 14% per decade until mid-century.


192. By 2050, the combined effect of extreme rainfall patterns, increased temperatures and increased frequency of hot extremes is expected to result in changes to water resources, leading to issues such as water scarcity, land degradation, food insecurity and migration. The projected impacts will vary across countries, sectors and regions. Reductions are projected in per capita food availability as median crop yields fall by 0–2% per decade, while demand for crops is expected to increase by 14% per decade until mid-century.

# D.4 Financing Needs

Latin America

193. Annex Zb provides a comprehensive overview of the economic, financial and post-COVID contexts in Latin Amer-ica. Overall, the region has seen steady socio-economic progress over the past two decades. Most countries in the region have achieved or are on track to achieve the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) by 2030. The region's GDP at purchasing power parity (PPP) amounts to US$ 6.1 trillion, with a median GDP per capita of US$ 9,500. However, despite this progress, Latin America and the Caribbean remain one of the most unequal regions in the world by income inequality, with the highest pre-pandemic poverty rates of the region.

### Social inequalities and rural-urban disparities

194. Social inequalities are rooted in the geography of the region. Poverty is concentrated in rural areas, where 49% of the population is poor and 23% is extremely poor. Furthermore, despite consistent food production surpluses, hunger remains a major problem in Latin America and the Caribbean, with over one-third of the region's population suffering from undernourishment exceeds 10 percent in seven countries.

### The COVID-19 pandemic

195. The COVID-19 pandemic caused an economic contraction of 7.3% in 2020, followed by positive growth of 3.7% in 2021. This is the largest economic contraction recorded since World War II and has been exacerbated by the war in Ukraine. The pandemic has deepened the region's social and economic inequalities.

### Financial sector response

196. The largest Latin American banks are headquartered in four countries: Brazil, Mexico, Colombia and Chile. The financial system of Latin America is characterized by a high degree of fragmentation and inequality in access to households and SMEs, and it is limited number of instruments and mechanisms for supporting the economy. In 2019, the region's total credit supply was US$ 3 trillion, with a total loan stock of US$ 1.8 trillion. Domestic credit to the private sector is only 47.4% across Latin America and the Caribbean, compared to an average of 65.5% in other regions. The share of loans to small businesses in Latin America and the Caribbean was posting the lowest percentage (16%) and Chile the highest (32%). The situation is particularly difficult for women-owned enterprises, which account for only 40% of all SMEs in the region. In 2019, total credit supply was US$ 3 trillion, while total supply of funding from financial institutions is US$ 347 billion (2017). This US$ 1.8 trillion funding gap is equivalent to 41.7% of regional GDP or 5.2 times the current supply of credit to MSMEs.

### Financial inclusion

197. The region's financial development database shows that Latin America and the Caribbean has a low level of financial inclusion, with only 30% of adults having access to formal banking services, compared to an average of 65% in other regions. This is partly due to the high cost of financial products and services, as well as limited access to credit for women-owned enterprises.

### Conclusion

198. The region's economic recovery will require a coordinated effort from governments, international organizations, and civil society to address the root causes of inequality and poverty in Latin America and the Caribbean. This includes increasing access to finance for small businesses and women-owned enterprises, as well as investing in infrastructure and education to promote long-term growth and development.


---
**Page 93**
---

197. Annex B2 provides a comprehensive overview of the economic, financial and post-COVID contexts in West Africa.

The majority of the population and economic activity—80% and 93%, respectively—in the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS) is concentrated in six countries: Benin, Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Guinea, and Liberia. These six countries account for more than half of the total ECOWAS land area.175 Nigeria alone accounts for over 62% of regional GDP, followed by Côte d'Ivoire (9%), Ghana (8%), and Senegal (4%). The West African Monetary Union (WAMU) is responsible for the French-speaking countries of the Western African Economic and Monetary Union (WAEMU). Its assets are US$1.770 billion.

Six of the ten fastest growing economies in Africa are in West Africa, and Côte d'Ivoire and Senegal are among the top 25 fastest-growing economies globally. The region has experienced strong economic growth in recent decades, which has to some extent been reflected in an associated reduction in poverty levels, levels of unemployment, and improvements in access to basic services such as education and health care.176 West Africa is home to more than 40% of the continent’s population, but accounts for less than 5% of its GDP. The region has a high dependency on agriculture, with agricultural sectors contributing over 60% of livelihoods and 30% of GDP regionally.177 However, with declining agricultural sectors and limited industrialization, West Africa is vulnerable to external shocks.

Of the 8 WAMU states, Côte d'Ivoire is considered a middle-income country; while Benin, Burkina Faso, Guinea, Mali, Niger, Senegal and Togo are all considered least developed countries (LDCs).178 French-speaking West African countries have been among the top-ranking countries on the UN Human Development Index rankings from 15 to 189 out of 189 in 2020.

Due to the COVID-19 pandemic, GDP in West Africa contracted by 3.6% in 2020 and is relatively unlikely to fully recover until at least 2024. The region’s economic growth rate forecast for the pandemic is a decline of 5%, compared with an average pre-pandemic growth rate forecast before the pandemic.179 A number of countries with less strict lockdowns experienced mild positive growth in Q3, but this was not enough to offset the negative impact on GDP in other countries. The weaknesses of West African economies, particularly for vulnerable groups such as women, young people and informal workers, are likely to be exacerbated by the pandemic.180 The number of people living below the poverty line is estimated at 43 million.181 Fourteen West African countries are planning to reduce national budgets by an average of 25% in 2020 (up from 6% in 2019) to partly offset the impact of the pandemic on GDP.

The financial sector in WAEMU is relatively underdeveloped.182 Official projections for the economic and financial outlook of WAEMU show that credit growth in francophone West African countries is significantly constrained; domestic bank credit to the private sector at 21% of GDP on average in francophone West Africa, compared with an average of 35% in Anglophone West Africa.183 The share of SMEs in WAEMU’s economy averages only 9%.184 SMEs are, in particular, struggle to access bank lending. As noted by the World Bank, “access to finance is a major challenge for small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in West Africa, given their limited experience and undocumented performance. SMEs can be very risky to lenders—especially when they have no collateral.”185

WAEMU comprises 13 member states: Benin, Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, the Gambia, Ghana, Guinea, Guinea-Bissau, Liberia, Mali, Niger, Senegal, Sierra Leone and Togo. The West African Economic and Monetary Union (WAEMU) was established in 1974 by the Treaty of Monrovia.186 It is headquartered in Abidjan, Côte d'Ivoire.

### D.5 Country ownership

#### D.5.1 Alignment with Climate Policies

203. The CATALIST® Initiative is aligned with NDCs, national climate policies and strategies, National Communication (NC) updates, Technology Needs Assessments and National Adaptation Plans (see Section B.1.3 and Annexes 2b and 2c).

#### D.5.2 Engagement with Stakeholders During CATALIST® Initial Preparation

A number of workshops have informed the design of the CATALIST® Initiative, including a scoping assessment (Annex 6a), an initial stakeholder engagement workshop (Annex 6b), a gender assessment (Annexes 3d and 3e), an ESG assessment (Annex 8c) and a gender assessment (Annex 8a).

All of these studies incorporated extensive stakeholder consultations, at global, regional and national levels, including with government officials, development partners, private sector representatives, donor support organizations (such as pre-accelerators and accelerators), VC funds and other sources of finance (angel investors, banks, microfinance institutions, etc.), financial service providers, business associations, chambers of commerce, women’s groups, gender, as well as potential bodies, CSOs, including educational/research organisations and environmental and social impact assessment firms. The consultations were augmented by extensive dialogue with NGOs and key validation workshops in December 2021; for Latin America, an in-depth stakeholder engagement workshop was held in January 2022. In Africa, the CATALIST® Initiative is being piloted in Abidjan and a regional virtual workshop. Full details of meetings, interviews and workshops held, including the themes and institutions affiliated to stakeholders, are provided in Annex A.7 to a summary provided below in Table 13.

206. Consultations with venture founders were useful, in particular, for understanding what activities could be financed by the CATALIST® Initiative (e.g., business development, market access, technology transfer, capacity building, co-benefits), etc.) of small enterprises at their early development stages. Consultations held in West Africa, in particular, highlighted that many entrepreneurs were not aware of the climate change risks they faced and that investors, accelerators, and investors served to assess their own needs (climate knowledge and mitigation asset assessment). In addition, there was a need for capacity building on ESG issues, including environmental impact and finance. Ongoing consultations were held throughout the programme development process with the Executive Director of the EBRD, the World Bank, the African Development Bank, the European Union, the OECD, the theory of change, ESMP, gender assessment, paradigm shift and other aspects of the CATALIST® initiative.

# D.5.3 Engagement with Stakeholders During CATALYST™ Initiative Implementation

207. The Global Advisory Committee (GAC) will be responsible for providing strategic direction to the CATALYST™ initiative and will consist of representatives from the GCF, KIC, Tec de Monterrey, Impact Hub Aubervilliers and IPDE, as well as other partners. The GAC will meet twice a year and, additionally, on an ad hoc basis when required. The GAC will be responsible for: (i) ensuring that the CATALYST™ initiative is aligned with the priorities of the KICs and NDCs of participating countries; (ii) representing the views of the KIC industry and from the international pre-accession partners in discussions with the GCF, KIC, Tec de Monterrey, Impact Hub Aubervilliers and IPDE, as well as other development institutions and development partners – in order to ensure full engagement and coordination with stakeholders.

## ESS Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) for CATALYST™ Initiative Implementation

208. The stakeholder engagement plan will be developed by the GAC and will have four dimensions:

- At global level, GIZ and Climatic-KIC will regularly engage with stakeholders in the VC finance industry.
- At regional level, a pilot region will be selected (and possibly two regions) workshops to account for language differences), including financial and impact investors.
- At local/regional level, the regional CATALYST™ implementation partners will permanently engage with local stakeholders and potential venture CATALYST™ initiatives. The GAC will also be asked to assess CATALYST™ initiative quality and usability.
- For each region, the GAC will recommend inclusion of the 60-purpose stakeholder engagement activities in the ventures' business plans.

## D

We will carry out stakeholder engagement as needed. This could include engagement with prospective customers, with people potentially affected (negatively or positively) by the venture’s impacts, with people living near the project site.

The regional EAS specialists may, in some cases, decide to consult directly with specific groups of persons for advice and information on how to address a case or issue. The GIZ EAS will ensure that this process fully applies principles of inclusion and non-discrimination, and/or to verify application of the Gender Action Plan.

### Information Disclosure

209. The CATALYST Initiative will meet the requirements of the GCF Information Disclosure Policy and Section 7.1 of GCF’s EAS policy.

#### External Communication: Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) (including workers) and GRM for SEAH

210. According to the GCF’s EAS policy, the purpose of the GRM is to receive and facilitate the resolution of concerns and grievances related to the project or venture in which the complainant has an interest. The GRM will be implemented at global and at regional level, as well as at country level for West Africa:

- **Collection of grievances by phone or in person:** through the regional EAS specialists.
- **Collection of grievances by phone or in person:** or by email to the GIZ EAS.
- **Working group on SEAH Grievances:** a working group will be established, with grievance-center and gender-responsive GRM will be implemented for SEAH-specific complaints/ incidents.

211. The regional Executing Entities and, ultimately, the GIZ EAS manager, are responsible for resolving grievances, including those related to workers. In case of disputes or disagreements between the parties involved in a project, a decision is taken by consensus among all stakeholders (the complainant, the venture, the Executing Entity etc.), an ad hoc mediation committee will be established and tasked with finding a solution that satisfies all of the affected stakeholders. If no agreement can be reached within 30 days, the GIZ EAS manager may amend the decision. The committee shall seek to make decisions on the basis of consensus but will, where necessary, refer the matter for resolution by the GIZ EAS manager or the GIZ Executive Committee. The latter will determine what all legal and sectoral skills are required. The committee members will be appointed by GIZ in the following manner: 1) the GIZ EAS manager; 2) the Head of the GIZ Country Office (or its equivalent Unit [Latin America, West Africa or Trans-Regional]) and 3 independent representatives (e.g., academics, professors, professionals with relevant experience in the field of dispute resolution, etc.) who have no direct professional backgrounds. In the event that the evaluation committee is unable to resolve a complaint to stakeholders’ satisfaction, the matter will be communicated to the GCF’s Independent Redress Mechanism.

### D.5.4 NDA Approval Process

212. NDAs have been involved throughout the CATALYST Initiative development process, spanning early scoping, development of the concept note and development of the funding proposal. In both regions, all NDAs have been signed by the GIZ EAS manager and the respective regional Executing Entity. The GIZ EAS manager attended meetings with the NDAs of Brazil, Colombia, Honduras and Mexico in Latin America, and Benin, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali, Niger, Nigeria, Senegal and Togo in West Africa.

213. As of September 21st, 2022, GIZ has received 12 NDAs from:

- Latin America: Argentina, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Honduras, Mexico
- West Africa: Benin, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali, Niger, Senegal, Togo
- Latin America: Colombia, Paraguay, Peru

# D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

## D.6.1 Mitigation Cost

### 214. The CATALYST Initiative's GCF budget is EUR 26.8 million. Over 20 years, the CATALYST Initiative will achieve GHG emissions reductions of 3.8 MCO2e. Accordingly, the mitigation cost to the GCF is EUR 7.1/MCO2e.

## D.6.2 Economic Analysis

### Economic IRR and NPV

#### 215. This section should be read in conjunction with the Excel economic model (Annex 3a).

#### The main quantifiable economic benefit of the CATALYST Initiative is the GHG emissions reductions resulting from the projects, according to the model.

#### 216. The central estimate of emission reductions, discussed in detail on Section D.1, indicates cumulative emission savings of approximately 3.8 MCO2e over 20 years from CATALYST Initiative inception, across the region. This is based on a 5% discount rate and an average annual growth rate of 4%. The model uses revenue growth rate schedules presented in the relevant worksheets of the Excel model.

#### 217. It is important to note that the 3.8 MCO2e central estimate represents the emission reductions after the application of a 6.5% attribution coefficient. This coefficient is intended to represent a realistic level of causality between the CATALYST Initiative and the projects, as well as the potential for other factors to influence emissions.

#### 218. The CATALYST Initiative is entirely responsible for the business success and corresponding emission reduction of the projects in the region. The total capital cost of the CATALYST Initiative is estimated at approximately EUR 26.8 million (over a period of 5 years). This represents an average annual expenditure of approximately EUR 166 million in Latin America and EUR 227 million in West Africa. These figures are based on estimates for the total project capital cost, which includes all costs associated with the projects, including EUR per tonne.

#### 219. The base-case assumption for the shadow price of carbon is EUR 50/tCO2e. The OECD recently published a study that suggests that the global carbon price will need to meet the Paris Agreement’s limit of global warming increase to 1.5°C by mid-century. Based on a comprehensive review of studies by academic and policy institutions, the OECD has estimated that the cost of meeting this target is EUR 30/tCO2e in the low-end scenario and EUR 80/tCO2e in the high-end estimate (EUR 30 to EUR 120). To put the OECD’s mid-range estimate in context: The Paris Agreement requires developed countries to reduce their emissions by at least 45% below 1990 levels, which are needed for 2020 for countries to decarbonize in line with the Paris Agreement. In 2030, prices should reach EUR 80–200/tCO2e.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 95

### The Fund recommends an increase in carbon prices by EUR 75 from current levels through to 2030 in a scenario that assumes optimal support for clean technology development.

#### Emission Reductions under the Emission Trading Scheme (EU ETS), the world's largest, stand at approximately EUR 8 as of May 14.

220. The OECD’s mid-range estimate therefore appears reasonable – and possibly even conservative in the light of EU market prices.

221. With emission reduction reductions of 3-9% below baseline, a carbon price of EUR 60/tonne, and a total programme budget of EUR 36.5 million (GCF grant + co-finance), the CATALYST Initiative yields an attractive economic internal rate of return (EIRR) of approximately 18%. The net present value is estimated at EUR 29.5 million, based on a 10% discount rate as customarily used in economic analysis.

222. A sensitivity analysis indicates that the EIRR and NPV are robust in a downside scenario. For the purposes of this sensitivity analysis, the following assumptions were made: (a) a carbon price of EUR 30/tonne; (b) a carbon price of EUR 10/tonne; and (c) a change in the volume of emission reductions from base case, in increments.

The analysis shows that:

* The EIRR remains double-digit even if carbon prices drop to EUR 30. In line with the OECD’s low-end estimate, and the emission reduction volume is in line with the central estimate.
* The NPV is positive as long as the carbon price is at least EUR 30 and the emission reduction volumes are lower than the central estimate.

As carbon prices move closer to the OECD high-end estimate of EUR 120, EIRRs increase to the 30% range. The positive NPV is preserved even if carbon prices are at least EUR 30 and the emission reduction volume is in line with the central estimate.

Other Effects Not Included in Economic IRR and NPV Estimates

224. The CATALYT! Initiative is expected to produce significant socio-economic co-benefits. However, given that the identities / sectors of the accelerated ventures will not be known until implementation of the CATALYT! Initiative is underway, it would be premature at this stage to estimate these co-benefits with any degree of certainty. As such, no estimates are made. Conservatively, therefore, the value of these co-benefits is not captured in the above estimates of economic returns.

* Employment creation during and after the CATALYT! Initiative implementation period: To the extent that the employees of the ventures accelerated are not already employed elsewhere and setting jobs, such employment would be considered as a direct benefit. In addition to this, there may also be indirect benefits in terms of increased productivity and efficiency (e.g., through better training and skills). There would also be an increase in labour quality, formalization of businesses and improvement in conditions of work.

* The jobs created by the ventures are also likely to entail higher skill levels than jobs in the broader economy. Jobs created as a result of the CATALYT! Initiative will be more productive and remunerative than informal employment.230 The climate transition could become one of the drivers for job creation for Africa’s youth population, particularly among women across different sectors and value chains, supporting the goal of promoting more diversified economies.

* Impact on health and nutrition: Climate ventures can meaningfully contribute to the health and livelihoods of communities in Africa by improving access to clean water (e.g., through rainwater harvesting), energy savings and stability of energy supply, improved resource efficiency, reduced travel times and its associated costs, and increased food security.

* Climate change adaptation benefits: Accelerated ventures may produce significant adaptation co-benefits. Ventures that address water security through, for example, sustainable pumping or nature-based solutions could make communities more resilient to climate change impacts such as droughts and floods. Renewable energy solutions would make communities more resilient to natural disasters that can affect the electricity grid. Energy efficiency measures in buildings and transport systems will reduce the vulnerability of African agriculture production systems by improving their adaptive capacity and resilience of vulnerable people, especially when they face the impacts of climate change.

* Pollution prevention: Recycling, sustainable agriculture and nature-based approaches, as well as many other solutions promoted through the CATALYT! Initiative, can contribute to reducing pollution. For example, improved access to clean water (e.g., on a purely indicative basis, the OECD estimates that a 75% reduction in particulate matter concentration in Europe could boost GDP by 0.8%),231

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 97

### D.6.3 Financial Analysis

225. The presence of such a wide range of socio-economic co-benefits, not included in the estimate of economic IRR and NPV, conserves the conservative nature of the quantitative analysis.

#### D.6.4 Best Available Technologies and Practices

229. The CATALIST® Initiative will be guided by market realists and will adopt a highly pragmatic approach to screening and selecting the most promising business ideas. This means that the climate impact will not be the only criterion on which the ventures with the most promising market prospects, as this is how climate mitigation impact will be achieved. The criteria for selection of the best business ideas will include: (i) whether the venture idea can be implemented using a mobile app or drone technology; and/or (ii) whether the venture idea represents good business in one particular sector or national market, but competing with other ventures that are not necessarily cutting-edge — may represent equally good business ideas in other market settings. The ventures supported by the CATALIST® initiative will always be the best in their own particular market contexts.

# LOGICAL FRAMEWORK

## E1 Project/Programme Focus

- Reduced emissions (mitigation)
- Increased resilience (adaptation)

## E2 GCF Impact Level: Paradigm shift potential

### Assessment Dimension

| Description | Rating |
| --- | --- |
| At the time of this Funding Proposal submission, both Latin America and West Africa have very underdeveloped private sector sectors. Latin America in its entirety experiences a private sector contribution to GDP less than 0.2%. Both regions continue to be heavily dependent on fossil fuel GHG emissions (Latin America has the highest per capita rate). Both regions are experiencing increasing interest in renewable energy, but commercially viable opportunities in renewables are still limited by models and disrupt markets with an insufficient number of established service providers. | Low |
| The CATALYST initiative is comparatively proportionate to deliver 10% of GCF funding over a period of approximately ten years, which would represent a significant step towards paradigm shift and focus on leveraging growing climate VC interest in the two regions and for climate-related co-benefits (and if any female-led, it could be an important driver of women’s empowerment in our region’s economy). | Low |

### Replicability

- If pathways for climate ventures to successfully access VC funding can be established, this model can further replicated across Latin America and potentially into other countries and regions.
- The design of the CATALYST initiative is flexible and allows for scaling up (to cover more regions at a later stage). By developing two slightly different regional programmes - one for Latin America and another for West Africa - it will allow for the initial developmental stages and business climate, allowing the replication of successful interventions in emerging and frontier markets elsewhere.

This support ecosystem is available in many countries of Latin America and West Africa. The amount of climate finance in Latin America and West Africa is increasing in both volume and diversity. However, most flows are increasing in small volumes and are directed at mainstream ecosystem actors – including entre- preneurs who have already established a track record. ## GCF Result Area (i.e., total emission reductions)
### MR4A Energy generation and access

#### Core 1: GHG emissions reduced or avoided/re-moved/sequenced
Application of the Climate Investment Funds (CIF) methodology (with third-party expert validation) to determine whether a project is selected for funding, and/or whether data updates/energy facility decommissioning occurs in the final year of the verification period. These CIF applications are reviewed by GCF.
- **Baseline:** 0
- **Target:** 38,290 CO2e
- **Assumptions & Notes:**
  - GHG emission reductions for MR1A, MRA 2, MPA 3 and MRA 4 are calculated based on the total emission reductions achieved by all projects in the respective year.
  - Projects include energy ventures (MRA 1); transport infrastructure projects (MR1B); industries and appliances ventures (MRA 2); and MR4A energy generation ventures. These ventures will be classified as archetype and/or baseline, depending on their status in the programme support (with periodic checks to ensure that they are still valid).
  - Reuse reception through the pilot project is considered as a baseline activity.
  - The total number of reuse (30 such reuses in each year).

#### Core 2: GHG emissions reduced or avoided/re-moved/sequenced
- **Baseline:** 0
- **Target:** 25,869 CO2e
- **Assumptions & Notes:**
  - At the end of the GCF/TATL initiative (i.e., in Year 6), the total number of reuse transactions using the updated venture classification methodology is calculated.
  - Ventures supported/re-moved/sequenced are particularly energy efficient or have significant energy capacity.
- **Target:** TBO
- **Assumptions & Notes:**
  - Emission reductions are achieved through the reduction in emissions from the ventures; emission reductions are calculated based on the average annual usage.
  - The total number of reuse of each venture is unique; each venture sells a different product or service to a particular customer in a particular country, in competition with other ventures.

#### Core 3: GHG emissions reduced or avoided/re-moved/sequenced
- **Baseline:** 0
- **Target:** TBO
- **Assumptions & Notes:**
  - At the end of the GCF/TATL initiative (i.e., in Year 6), the total number of reuse transactions using the updated venture classification methodology is calculated.
  - Ventures supported/re-moved/sequenced are particularly energy efficient or have significant energy capacity.
- **Target:** TBO
- **Assumptions & Notes:**
  - The total number of reuse of each venture is unique; each venture sells a different product or service to a particular customer in a particular country, in competition with other ventures.

Targets for the Supplementary Indicators (1.1-5) will be set during programme implementation. These targets are project-specific and hence technological/sectoral nature of the project will determine the target. The progress against these targets will be reported in the programme APFs.

  - The Latin American venture sector is also developing at different paces. Africa, with more technical and

- **The cleantech sector is young.**
  - There are many similarities shared by both entrepreneurs and investors in terms of the types of solu-

#### West Africa
- **low**
  - The concept of cleantech is still evolving. The financial and technical requirements associated with it are at a relatively early stage.

  - (iii) High-quality technical assistance providers (accelerators and end-users).

The Latin America acceleration programme aims to achieve the commercial investment readiness of 30 Latin American ventures within a period of 2 years, with an average contribution benefit from 10 months of acceleration support.

- **Sub-Activity 4.1.1.2:** Acceleration programme technical assistance

- **Sub-Activity 4.1.2.2:** Acceleration programme technical assistance
- **Sub-Activity 4.1.2.3:** Acceleration grants

## Output 2.4: Selected climate sites in West Africa MIPs to local markets are empowered to rapidly scale their business and climate impact
### Activity 2.4.1: West African climate venture acceleration programme
* The West Africa acceleration programme aims to accelerate the growth of 60 climate entrepreneurs / ventures over a period of up to 5 years, with each venture benefiting from an average annual funding of EUR 100,000 (on average) per venture.
* Unlike the acceleration programme in Latin America (Activity 3.1), the West African acceleration programme will be implemented on a rolling, venture-by-venture basis and not all 60 climate entrepreneurs / ventures will occur continuously in the first 2 years of the West Afri-cain acceleration programme.

### Sub-Activity 2.4.1.1: Venture selection
* Approximately 60 climate ventures (approximately 20 per country) are selected to benefit from the acceleration programme annually over a period of up to 5 years.
* Comprehensive technical assistance is provided for each venture, including mentoring and coaching.

### Sub-Activity 2.4.1.2: Acceleration programme – repayable
* Approximately 60 climate ventures (approximately 10 per year for 3 years) are accepted into the acceleration programme, having secured sufficient funding to cover their business development in the context of the venture’s Value Creation Plans.
* Repayable Value Creation Plans are co-developed (ventures and partners) with the GCF and MOSEs.
* Repayable accelerator grants are issued to 30 climate entre-preneurs / ventures, which will be repaid over a period of up to 5 years.

### Sub-Activity 2.4.1.3: Acceleration programme – technical assistance
* Grant repayment is triggered by a milestone associated with the venture’s Value Creation Plan.
* Tools are developed, including updated, regionally applicable methodologies for the CAF and MOSE tools; climate resil-ience assessment tests and methodologies, accompanied by training programmes for staff of GCF and MOSEs.

* A 4-week training workshop on updated CAF and MOSE tools are conducted in collaboration with partners.
* A training programme for Enabling Experts (staff), as well as a training programme for the GCF and MOSEs’ technical staff and associated consultants, consisting of at least 10 participants.

* Tools are developed, including updated, regionally applicable methodologies for the CAF and MOSE tools; climate resil-ience assessment tests and methodologies, accompanied by training programmes for staff of GCF and MOSEs.
