E.7 Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation Arrangements

**E.7.1 Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation Arrangements**

26. Monitoring and evaluation (M&E) (in addition to GDFP and SAP monitoring) will comply with the relevant GCF policies, as stipulated in the AMA, FAA and project-related Financing Agreements and Implementation Agreements with Executing Entities and Implementation Partners, which Executing Entities will extend to sub-grantees.

27. The CATALYST 5/1 Initiative will apply a combined results-based monitoring and evaluation (M&E) system. The system will be based on:
- GIZ Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) for evaluation policy – principles, guidelines and requirements.
- The CATALYST 5/1 Initiative logical framework.
- The M&E implementation schedule.
- Requirements of the GCF’s Annual Performance Report.
- Reporting to the GCF Board, donors, partners and stakeholders.

28. The M&E system will track CATALYST 5/1 Initiative Outputs, Activities, Sub-Activities and Impacts, as well as associated financial flows, across all components:
- The overall responsibility and oversight for M&E and reporting lie with the GCF AE Unit of GIZ head office.
- The GIZ AE Unit will coordinate with the 3 CATALYST 5/1 Initiative Management Units (Component 2), consisting of GIZ Impact Hub Abidjan and IPEI; and trans-regional (Component 3), consisting of GIZ Executive Entity – to carry out the M&E operations.

29. The budget for implementing M&E measures is presented separately in the Programme Budget (Annex 4a) and is outlined in the Monitoring and Evaluation Plan (Annex 5).

Recruitment of M&E staff

30. Immediately when the CATALYST 5/1 Initiative commences, GIZ’s AE Unit at head office in Germany will make available one expert to oversee, coordinate and manage the programme M&E and reporting routines. He/she will cooperate closely with the 3 CATALYST 5/1 Initiative Management Units and GIZ Executive Entity units in West Africa.
- As soon as GCF proceeds become available in Latin America and West Africa, the regional Management Units will recruit one full-time M&E specialist.


---
**Page 125**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 121

Independent monitoring and evaluation studies

231. GIZ’s AE Unit will initiate an interim evaluation in Year 3 of the CATALIST Initiative (or at any time that GIZ, the NDAs and/or the Global Advisory Committee (GAC) determine is appropriate), to assess progress made towards achieving the design and objectives outlined for the Initiative. The Interim Evaluation will duly involve CATALIST Initiative stakeholders, including target groups, beneficiaries and contributing partners. The Interim Evaluation will:

- A review of the institutional, administrative, organisational, environmental, social, economic, technical and financial aspects of the CATALIST Initiative based on the assumptions and risks included in the design (among others, as specified in the Funding Proposal and Regional Feasibility Studies).
- A review of conventions to assess whether they still are relevant or need to be changed or waived due to altered conditions.
- A review of progress made towards achieving the design and objectives outlined for the Initiative.

232. GIZ’s AE Unit will make available an Interim Evaluation Report to the GIZ Secretariat and CATALIST Initiative stakeholders.

Final evaluation: In due time before the completion of the CATALIST Initiative, GIZ’s AE Unit will initiate a programme completion mission in which the implementation of the CATALIST Initiative based on the financing and implementation agreements, the delivery of outputs and the achievement of CATALIST Initiative objectives are evaluated. The final evaluation will be conducted by an independent entity (e.g., an external consultant) with no direct link to GIZ or contributing partners. At the time of the CATALIST Initiative’s physical completion and commissioning, and before the expiry of the guarantee period, GIZ’s AE Unit will make available a Final Evaluation Report.

Interim evaluation and final evaluation: GIZ evaluations are carried out by GIZ’s independent evaluation unit. This unit is steered by a corporate unit that is separate from operational business and reports directly to the Management Board. Moreover, evaluations are conducted with the support of external evaluators who have no direct link to GIZ or contributing partners.

These are based on the 6 evaluation criteria agreed by the Development Assistance Committee (DAC) of the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD). In addition to these six DAC evaluation criteria, the following 5 GCF criteria will be evaluated: gender equity, country ownership, innovativeness in results areas, impact and scalability.

Regarding GIZ’s Evaluation Principle: Impartiality, Objectivity and Unbiasedness. And Use and Participation All provisions for upholding the GCF evalu- ation principles described in Chapter V (a) of the Evaluation Policy will be followed (see DAC standards and OECD/GC evaluation standards). In line with UNCED/UNDP’s 1994 Guidelines on Evaluations, GIZ is committed to ensuring that its evaluations are conducted independently. The following principles apply Regarding GIZ’s Evaluation Principle: Credibility and Robustness; as a matter of principle, GIZ takes care to use an appropriate combination of quantitative and qualitative methods in order to ensure the credibility and robustness of evaluation results.

To ensure robust evidence, GIZ uses a theory-based approach as a minimum standard for its central project evaluations. The contribution analysis has proven to be a suitable design. The aim of contribution analysis is to collect empirical evidence on whether and to what extent contributions to objectives

## Data collection and frequency

### E.7.2 Measurement, Reporting and Verification (MRV) of Climate Mitigation Impacts Scope of MRV

#### 237. The CATALYST Initiative's emission reductions will be achieved through the use of low-emission goods/services sold to customers by the Intrapreneur-supported ventures. Because ventures in the regional pre-acceleration programmes will be selected precisely for the purpose of developing minimum viable products (MVPs), they are not expected to be at a sufficient stage of development to make any commercial sales of products/services – and certainly not at scale – until after completion of their respective regional pre-acceleration programmes. As such, emission reductions associated with MVPs will not be considered in the CATALYST Initiative's MRV.

#### 238. Indirect emission reduction benefits are certainly anticipated from the CATALYST Initiative – for example, stemming from venture sourcing and intellectual support (including among women entrepreneurs), capacity building of ventures and entrepreneur support organisations (ESOs), the replication and role-model effect of successful ventures, and the creation of new jobs in the region. However, these indirect emission reduction benefits are not considered in the CATALYST Initiative's MRV.

#### Data Collection for MRV

To calculate CATALYST Initiative emission reductions, two data points are required:

- The emission reduction estimate per functional unit of each good/service sold: for example, the emission reduction associated with each energy efficient appliance sold, or each rooftop PV unit installed, or each tonne of compost produced, or each hectare of low-emission agriculture applied. These data will be collected by the Intrapreneur-supported ventures.

Application of the Cif tool, accompanied by third party expert validation, will take place 3 times during programme implementation: (i) at the end of the pre-acceleration programme; (ii) when a venture is accepted into the acceleration programme (except the case where the applicant venture has recently graduated from the pre-acceleration programme); and (iii) at the end of the acceleration programme, using updated data (updated emission

The CIF tool enables unique, very specific GHG mitigation estimates to be computed for each venture, incorporating (for example) Tier 2 and Tier 3 emission factors.

## Sales Data

Sales data - the number of functional units sold by each venture per time interval. This will be collected from the accelerated ventures on a quarterly basis (matching the frequency of sales data collection). The CIF tool will use the GCF's approach to emissions accounting, the CIF tool's life cycle emission reduction benefits will be annualised: the life cycle - for example 8 years for home battery solutions, 20 years for ground solar PV systems or 1 year for low-emission packaging (bioplastics, multi-use plastics, etc.). Product/service lifetimes are provided in Annex 22c.

The Executive Entities will need to validate the reported sales data as far as possible - for example by verifying revenues: sales receipts or bank statements. If this is not possible, then alternative figures may be used. Where such figures cannot be obtained, suitable documentation measures will be put in place at the venture or relevant ventures) to ensure reliable data reporting in the future.

The Executive Entities will pass the sales data onto the Trans-Regional Management Unit for aggregation, processing and onward reporting (e.g. to G2E, the GCF, programme publications, etc.).

## Reporting of Climate Impacts

### An estimate of each venture's future mitigation impact will be generated using the CIF tool when each venture enters the acceleration programme.

These ex-ante estimates will be reported to the GCF in the relevant Annual Progress Report (APR), on a venture-by-venture and aggregated basis as is by Phase 1, Phase 2, by cohort, by region, etc. They will also be reported in other literature generated by the programme for both internal and external audiences.

### Where a venture generates sales during its participation in the acceleration programme, these sales will be collected and validated on a quarterly basis (matching the frequency of sales data collection). The CIF tool will use the GCF's approach to emissions accounting. If this is not possible, then alternative figures may be used. Where such figures cannot be obtained, suitable documentation measures will be put in place at the venture or relevant ventures) to ensure reliable data reporting in the future.

These sales data will be used to produce ex-post emission reduction estimates. Such estimates will be generated on a quarterly basis (matching the frequency of sales data collection).

E

## Collection of GHG Emissions

They will certainly be reported on an annual basis in APRs to the GCF; they may be reported more frequently than an annual basis in programme literature.

### Update of the CFP Tool

As part of a venture's acceleration programme, the CFP tool will be re-run. As a result, the per-functional-unit mitigation parameter may be updated – e.g., to reflect more precise emission factors or new baseline conditions. Where relevant (i.e., where a venture has recorded sales during the acceleration programme), the GCF and its partners will respectively apply the updated per-functional-unit mitigation parameter to these paid taxes; emission reduction estimates for the period will be updated using this updated per-functional-unit mitigation parameter. These amendments will be reported to the GCF in the APR of the year in which the amendments are made. They may also be reported in programme literature.

### Updated ex ante GHG Emission Reduction Estimates

The updated CFP tool will be used to generate updated ex ante GHG emission reduction estimates for the programme lifespan. These updated ex ante estimates will be reported on an annual basis, in APRs to the GCF; they may be reported more frequently than an annual basis in programme literature.

### Updated per-Functional-unit Mitigation Parameter

The updated per-functional-unit mitigation parameter will be used to produce ongoing quarterly ex post emission reduction estimates for each venture. They will certainly be reported on an annual basis, in APRs to the GCF; they may be reported more frequently than an annual basis in programme literature.

## Mid-Term and Final Evaluations

A mid-term evaluation will be undertaken after Year 3 of programme implementation and a final evaluation will be undertaken after Year 6 of programme implementation. In addition to other functions, these evaluations will provide opportunities for evaluation to assess (i) the CATALYST Initiative's GHG impact methodology and (ii) the ex-ante and ex-post estimates of programme mitigation impact. The evaluations will be shared with the GCF. Where the evaluations make specific recommendations regarding changes in the CFP tool or other assumptions, these recommendations will be discussed with programme stakeholders (notably, Executing Entities) and with the GCF Secretariat, and will be acted upon accordingly.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## Selected Risk Factor 1: Economic shocks impacting the venture economy

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description

A country / regional / global financial or economic crisis that impacts either or both regions and leads to low liquidity and/or a reduction of capital available for investment in new ventures, including venture capital funds. This could lead to a drop in funding for sufficient numbers of robust, high-quality business ventures for those regions where the CATALYST Initiative’s regions enter 2021 having an underdeveloped or non-existent venture ecosystem. A potential consequence is that the region will not be able to recover from the economic impact of the COVID pandemic, a potentially negative effect on future economic growth and, in some cases of Sahelian West Africa, could lead to food insecurity. There are signs of potential macroeconomic distress, partly due to growing national debts and inflationary pressures.

This risk is not directly manageable by the CATALYST Initiative. However, it is important to note that as the world emerges from the COVID pandemic, there will be a need for countries and regions to restructure their economies and services will be an important part of the recovery, which is expected to underpin market opportunities for climate venture capital.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Political instability and policy reversals

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |

### Description

Political instability, leading to degenerating of climate change as a policy priority and/or lower confidence in economic prospects – and, consequently, lower demand for climate-smart ventures and innovation. Historically, both have experienced a combination of high inequality, poor economic performance and weak political institutions, leading to low levels of trust in government and the rule of law. This risk is considered to be a regional risk with an overall regional risk, with 3 West African countries in particular – Burkina Faso, Mali and Niger - facing domestic security challenges.


---
**Page 130**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL 3.0 | PAGE 126

## Mitigation Measure(s)

This risk is not directly mitigated by the CATALIST Initiative. However, the risk is somewhat mitigated by the fact that the CATALIST Initiative is not tied to a single country’s political stability for success and that the largest national economies are also the largest market change. Moreover, the CATALIST Initiative is expected to be the most politically stable.

Screening and due diligence of venture firms prior to their admission into pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes will ensure that they have access to mature market growth prospects, and political instability will form part of this assessment.

Climate change mitigation and SME support are increasing significantly areas of policy development; although there may be some policy reversals (such as the Mexican government’s recent closure of the Climate Change Fund), it is unlikely that future governments will wholly damage the development of these sectors. Moreover, the CATALIST Initiative will not be directly affected by such changes in policy, since it will rely on the “market mechanism” for climate impact; public sector involvement is welcome (and will be encouraged), but it is not a pre-requisite to success.

The CATALIST Initiative will also be able to adapt its approach and strategy as required in response to changes of national policies and objectives (including NGOs, but are not so closely tied that they change that they will prevent the achievement of the Initiative’s goals).

NDAs will be invited to participate in the CATALIST I/Initiative’s Advisory Global Committee (GAC meetings), which will be held twice a year (plus ad hoc meetings as required). This will enable the CATALIST I/Initiative to closely follow local policy developments and changes.

### Selected Risk Factor 3: Executing Entity performance

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

**Description**

Executing Entities fail to perform satisfactorily. This could include sub-optimal delivery of the pre-acceleration or acceleration programmes, poor quality of support provided by the executing entities (e.g., lack of technical expertise), or the provision of inferior or poor-quality technical assistance that jeopardises the climate ventures’ ability to grow sustainably and attract VC funding.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

G42 has selected Executing Entities on the basis of robust eligibility criteria that require, inter alia, demonstrated experience in implementing pre-acceleration programmes, a track record of implementing technical support programmes in conjunction with international development partners, and regional expertise (see also section 8.4.2). G42 has also established a rigorous selection process for Executing Entities to ensure that they are capable of delivering the CATALIST I/Initiative.

The CATALIST I/Initiative contains dedicated activities precisely to address Executing Entity capacity needs identified during Initiative preparation. G42, as well as the AE, closely monitor the performance of the Executing Entities to ensure that they meet their obligations and deliver any required programme engagements through contractual or capacity building measures.

### Selected Risk Factor 4: Access to VC funds for growth stage

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

**Description**

Venture sustainability is negatively affected if access to follow-on VC funding for the venture growth stage is not available. More than 60% of entrepreneurs in Latin America report that lack of follow-on funding is the major factor limiting their growth, and the majority of venture failures in Latin America are attributable to lack of funding (or, just as importantly, lack of knowledge of how to access it).

The CATALIST I/Initiative will integrate pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes at every stage, also giving entrepreneurs the opportunity to meet and pitch to VC funds, and to adapt business ideas and strategies. The fact that the CATALIST I/Initiative is expected to be in its early stages (and therefore still very early-stage), is expected to increase VC firms’ readiness to invest in the CATALIST I/Initiative-supported ventures.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 127

## A key VC fund in West Africa (and parent to IPED, an Executing Entity) and Gatsby Capital, a key VC fund active in Latin America, have, among other investors, collaborated closely with GIZ to develop the CATALYTIC Initiative to support climate venture creation and growth. The Initiative is designed to be regionally specific (with different focus areas in the regions), and beyond, will ensure that CATALYTIC Initiative beneficiaries have maximum exposure to potential VC funding.

Additionally, the CATALYTIC Initiative's technical assistance for the ventures has been designed in such a way that it is responsive to VC needs; i.e. it is explicitly intended to equip entrepreneurs with the business skills and resources necessary to attract investment from VCs. The Initiative will also provide support to climate venture creation and enhancing their prospects for receiving Series A finance. The recent funding by VC funds in both regions in 2021 is a strong indication of the appetite among VCs for climate solutions, and the CATALYTIC Initiative will help the VC sector generally, and the climate VC sector specifically, will keep growing with appropriate barrier-removal measures.

## Selected Risk Factor 5: Challenging local business environments

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Low |

### Description
The business environment in both regions can be challenging due to sectoral contexts, being challenging for enterprises – particularly for new ventures that lack experience, an entrenched market presence and brand, and established stake-holders (e.g. customers, suppliers, distributors, etc.).

In addition to general issues of doing business, climate venture in particular may struggle with: (i) finding qualified workers and contractors to support them with the development and implementation of climate solutions; running their day-to-day operations; and (ii) accessing finance for working capital, as well as long-term financing. The geography is particularly rural areas and often unstable, which could impede climate businesses with a significant digital component or in a country with weak infrastructure.

### Mitigation Measures
The CATALYTIC Initiative will be guided by market realities. Regardless of the theoretical attractiveness of particular ventures, if they are not able to demonstrate that they have a realistic chance of attracting investment from existing climate venture for pre-acceleration and acceleration support, such ventures do not represent attractive prospects for inclusion in the CATALYTIC Initiative. If a venture is unable to demonstrate its ability to attract investment on the market, they will not be selected for inclusion in the CATALYTIC Initiative. Given: (i) the highly entrepreneurial population particularly in West Africa and Latin America; (ii) the increasing number of climate entrepreneurs; (iii) the increasing and diversifying the talent pool available to the initiative; and (iv) the involvement of 3 respected pre-accelerator partners, the CATALYTIC Initiative will be able to identify a sufficient number of ventures that are likely to succeed. Records of running and nurturing new businesses in the regions, the CATALYTIC Initiative expects to successfully recruit a sufficient number of climate entrepreneurs.

Particularly in the acceleration programme, but even in the pre-acceleration programme, ventures will be able to exert influence on the selection process by demonstrating their ability to attract investment from existing climate venture. This extends to the EUR 15,000 pre-acceleration grants and EUR 100,000 acceleration repayable grants, which, subject to the terms of the grant agreements, will be used for business development activities, including market research, surveys markets and technology. This will enable supported climate ventures to focus on the specific challenges that they face in their particular market contexts.

The CATALYTIC Initiative will also provide a peer networking will also serve to pair ventures with role-models who can advise ventures on how particular market challenges have been successfully addressed in the past or in other countries.

## Selected Risk Factor 6: Limited mitigation benefits or climate maladaptation

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description


---
**Page 132**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 129

Ventures pursuing negative solutions that are either unabtainable or positively climate-harmful—for example, entailing climate misadaptation or increased GHG emissions.

#### Mitigation Measures()

The CATALIST® Initiative will screen and select ventures in a competitive, transparent environment using a set of explicit criteria and weightings—the most important of which will be ventures’ prospects for market growth and reducing carbon intensity. Ventures that are not expected to achieve significant mitigation impacts will not receive support. Any venture with mitigation impacts will be assessed using a credible, internationally-recognised assessment tool (CfT) and will be valid for the duration of the CATALIST® Initiative. The programme will have a clear exit strategy, including a mechanism of setting the GHG impact to zero for the top 10% of supported ventures—the programme would still be expected to generate significant overall mitigation benefits.

Incremental climate solutions will be eligible for CATALIST® Initiative support, provided they offer substantial overall mitigation benefits; for example, a product or service may offer only a 5% reduction in GHG emissions compared with business as usual. However, if the venture is expected to scale up significantly and/or has a large market potential then its aggregate mitigation performance may justify programme support. However, during the pre-acceleration and acceleration phases of the CATALIST® Initiative, where the number of supported ventures will be small and still being formed, climate results will not yet have been achieved. In this case, alternative approaches will represent good practice for future scaling up in practice. Where disruptive, highly transformational solutions are possible, the ventures developing these ideas will be strongly supported by the CATALIST® Initiative.

VCs should provide feedback on their ideas—subject to confidentiality—to ensure that they receive the best feedback provided by VC firms (risk vs reward trade-off).

The CATALIST® Initiative will closely monitor the development of supported ventures and will modify or cease its support should signs emerge that venture mitigation performance is significantly falling short of expectations or if envisaged results are not achieved. The CATALIST® Initiative will also provide training for Activity 2.1.1 (East Africa) and Activity 2.1.1 (West Africa), ecosystem actors, including the ventures, the regional Executives Entities and VC firms, will be educated on climate mitigation, climate adaptation, gender and ESG frameworks.

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment

#### G.1.1 Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF)

247. The ESMF (Annex 6a) ensures that the CATALYT5 Initiative will comply with GIZ’s Safeguards and Gender Action Plan (GAP) as well as the International Finance Corporation (IFC) Performance Standards (PSs). Since 2021, the CATALYT5 Initiative will also comply with the national legislation of countries that supply climate compatible technologies. The IFC PSs are used to assess the environmental and social risks associated with intermediaries (IFC; 2012) has been used as guidance on the environmental and social management systems of such entities.

248. From an environmental and social safeguards perspective, the ESMF rates the programme risk as medium (b). The potential negative environmental and social impacts of the CATALYT5 Initiative during its implementation phase are related to the activities of the climate technologies. These include: land use change for implement- ment; small-scale production or testing of agricultural solutions on land plots; carried out by the CATALYT5 Initiative in collaboration with local communities; and, where applicable, the construction of infrastruc- tures. Activities will be limited to employment, small-scale land use, moderate generation of pollution and waste, and only for a maximum of 49 workers (though probably far fewer). Impacts would occur if ventures perform activities that are not aligned with the ESMF or the IFC PSs. The potential negative environmental and social risks will be limited, site-specific and can be reduced to acceptable levels with appropriate E&S management.

249. The ESMF is accompanied by a Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) and a Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM). The SEP includes the following: Ia) all ventures; ii) Gender Assessment (Annex 6b); and iii) the Gender Action Plan (Annex 6b).

#### G.1.2 Summary of Main Impacts, Risks and Mitigation Measures

250. The CATALYT5 Initiative’s principal environmental impact is the reduction of GHG emissions, which will be achieved through the deployment of climate compatible technologies that are likely to reduce climate impacts supported by the CATALYT5 Initiative. Additional environmental and social co-benefits, including enhancement of ecosystem services (e.g., water quality, soil fertility), improved livelihoods for local communities (e.g., restored ecosystems), job creation and enhanced capital investment in low-emission technologies, are anticipated.

251. Negative environmental and social impacts may occur when the CATALYT5 Initiative specifies “identifiably specific” activities (i.e., activities that are not likely to generate environmental and social impacts) [as per the terminology employed in ESSI].

- During implementation: such impacts are mostly related to the activities of the climate ventures. The pre- acceptance phase will be limited to a maximum of 49 workers, while the post-acceptance phase (i.e., implemen- tation): workers; the acceleration programme in each region will encompass more developed climate technologies and will involve a maximum of 100 workers (though probably far fewer). Impacts would occur if ventures perform activities that are not aligned with the ESMF or the IFC PSs. The potential negative environmental and social risks associated with the CATALYT5 Initiative’s technical assistance activities will also generate environmental and social impacts.

- After the implementation period: successful ventures are expected to substantially scale up their activi- ties. This is a period when the ventures may realize significant co-benefits, as well as unmit- tigated negative environmental and social risks. The potential negative environmental and social risks at this stage. This is also the period when the ventures exit direct responsibility of the programme; however, the CATALYT5 Initiative has the potential to prepare them for appropriate E&S management, and hence mitigate future risks.

# G.1.3 Detailed Safeguards Assessment and Programme Risk Mitigation Approach

## Environmental and Social Standard 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

To ensure that the CATALIST Initiative reaches its overall climate mitigation target, in compliance with the principles of ESSI, a detailed safeguards assessment is required.

### The CATALIST Initiative's approach to managing negative EAS risks and impacts:

- **The CATALIST Initiative** will identify and assess negative EAS risks and impacts on the list.
- **The CATALIST Initiative** does not fund activities carried out by the CATALIST Initiative-supported climate venture that are likely to result in significant negative EAS risks or impacts, unless such risks can be mitigated through additional support.
- **Potential negative EAS risks and impacts of the ventures' current and future activities (other than their climate change mitigation impacts) are identified and enhanced.**

The CATALIST Initiative will support the ventures with the highest potential climate impact mitigation. Among ventures that have a high potential for climate adaptation co-benefits, 50% or more of the venture's staff are women-led.

### The CATALIST Initiative will also need to select ventures with the best negative environmental and social risks and impacts (EAS) management practices. Ventures selected by the CATALIST Initiative may: (i) result in over-representation of certain types of ventures, such as those in the information technology sector; (ii) be more likely to have a high potential for climate adaptation co-benefits than other sectors that are important in need of assistance; and (iii) prevent ventures with strong climate change mitigation potential, but higher EAS risks or impacts from being selected. The methodology offered by the CATALIST Initiative to improve their capacity for EAS management and their overall performance.

The CATALIST Initiative will further develop methodologies for capturing EAS co-benefits as one of its outputs.

### Environmental and Social Standard 2: Labour and working conditions

ESMF observation: Labour laws exist in all the potential participating countries, but it is not always sufficiently enforced. Depending on the sector activity, enforcement may vary from zero to complete. Enforcement is expected to be more effective for larger enterprises than small businesses. In some sectors, such as agriculture, some workers can be vulnerable. In sectors with higher skilled personnel, national labour regulations are ex-pected to be more effective in protecting workers' rights and health and safety risks.

In general, small businesses will not have worker protection laws that are as strong or enforced as those of larger enterprises. The CATALIST Initiative expects to see poor working conditions in small businesses than in large companies. In general, small businesses will not have worker protection laws that are as strong or enforced as those of larger enterprises.

In the context of the CATALIST Initiative, it is expected that there may be a high risk of informal labour occurring among the funded ventures. Informal labour can include workers who do not have formal contracts and whose working conditions are often poor. In compliance with relevant standards on health and safety, non-discrimination, inclusion of vulnerable people and work-life balance, the CATALIST Initiative will require that all funded ventures comply with these standards.

Health and safety risks can exist at early-stage business development, mainly in research and development activities where there is a high risk of exposure to hazardous materials. In addition, there may be health and safety risks associated with cultural activities. These risks will typically be minor during pre-acceleration, but it can, in some cases, become significant as the venture progresses.

### Impact rating: The magnitude of impacts and risks will depend on the sector and on the growth stage of the venture but is expected to vary from negligible to low in the pre-acceleration stage, and from negligible to moderate in the acceleration stage.

# Environmental and Social Standard 4: Community health, safety and security

## ESMS observation:
At pre-acceleration stage, impacts of the ventures' activities on communities are very unlikely to be significant in terms of community health, safety or security, and may therefore generate minor to moderate risks and impacts for the public and communities living near production sites.

### Impact rating: 
The impact level will depend on the sector and on the growth stage of the venture but is expected to remain at a low level during pre-acceleration. During acceleration stage it could be moderate in some cases.

## Mitigation approach:
ESMS applies to the CATALYST® Initiative as follows:

- The ESMS for the initiative should not generate negative risks and impacts for the public.
- All end-products should be safe and healthy for end-users.

Risk mitigation is required in cases when technical assistance activities lead to gathering of people in buildings, communities or other locations. In such situations, potential risks and risks will be identified during the venture screening. Supported ventures will receive awareness-raising and training, and will be required to comply with ESMS requirements. The CATALYST® Initiative will not have any significant environmental impact testing which will be carried out at pre-acceleration stage. If it occurs at acceleration stage, a specific support program will be developed.

## Environmental and Social Standard 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

### ESMS observation:
Some of the beneficiary ventures may require the use of land during their early development stages.

- Urban land for construction of buildings or offices.
- Land for production purposes in the nature, forestry and agricultural sectors.
- Land for demonstration or research purposes in the nature, forestry and agricultural sectors.

Use of land, or restrictions to access to land, may result in negative impacts on livelihoods, biodiversity, sacred sites, cultural heritage and other natural resources. These risks will be assessed during the venture screening and the institutional frameworks for land tenure, for protection of human rights, for stakeholder engagement and for environmental impact assessment will be applied.

The CATALYST® Initiative will not cause any significant involuntary resettlement. There are, however, very minor remaining risks in some cases:

- Previous owners have evicted dwellers in view of selling the land to the ventures.
- Some communities may be displaced by the venture's lending, take place; provide a major fraction of the vendor's revenues, and cannot be easily relocated.

The risk is significant for the affected persons, and the activity cannot easily take place elsewhere. Such cases of expropriation/displacement can be difficult to identify, and hence are more difficult to avoid.

### Impact rating:
The risk of occurrence of such impacts is extremely low, as it would concern only a very small number of ventures. Impact magnitude (even without mitigation) would vary from very low to moderate.

## Mitigation approach:
Under no circumstances will involuntary resettlement be tolerated by the CATALYST® Initiative. However, in some cases, there may be significant risks related to economic or physical displacement, and restrictions of access to land, during pre-acceleration and acceleration stages. Ventures will be required to provide a detailed risk assessment if such risks are identified.

In case of any new land use by a supported venture: screening by the EAS specialist will verify that CATALYST® does not violate ESMS for land acquisition (when relevant for their activity sector).

## Environmental and Social Standard 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

### ESFM observation:
At the pre-acceleration and acceleration stages, the activities of the climate ventures are expected to have adverse impacts on biodiversity, particularly in areas where such impacts can be related to the use of land for agriculture, deforestation, noise or pollution, etc. A potentially significant impact may occur if starting at a small scale. Some activities could have biodiversity impacts in their supply chain, but at this stage, it is not possible to identify which ones and how significant they are. The construction and maintenance of infrastructure could impact animal and plant species at a moderate scale. Interventions in biological corridors and river banks may also have an adverse effect on the environment.

Any significant reduction of river flows, and impacts on rivers basins, can be irreversible if not properly managed and should be avoided.

### Impacts:
The likelihood of occurrence of such impacts is low, and their magnitude would generally vary from low to moderate. However, some impacts can potentially be irreversible (invasive species, aquatic habitat destruction).

### Mitigation measures:
Risks and impacts on biodiversity of the activities of each venture will be screened at pre-acceleration stage and assessed in a more detailed way at acceleration stage. Supported ventures with physical assets that could have significant adverse effects on biodiversity should include a biodiversity risk assessment as part of their ESFM. Biodiversity risk assessment and management will be included in the project design, and projects will be required to provide evidence of compliance with this requirement.

Ventures that receive training on ESF and on applicable standards such as organic/sustainable forestry certification programs.

The implementation of ESF is challenging for impacts in natural and critical habitats; therefore, activities that would take place in habitats that are potentially natural or critical habitats, among which are nature protected areas (NPA), will be excluded from the CATALYST initiative. Ventures with physical assets that could have significant adverse effects on biodiversity should include a biodiversity risk assessment as part of their ESFM, which is not feasible at the CATALYST Initiative time-frame. The concerned ventures can receive training to comply with ESF and applicable standards such as organic/sustainable business stages.

Activities that are also excluded from the CATALYST initiative are:

- Clear-cutting (except for the FASCO project)
- Disturbance of sea bottom or river beds
- Damages to natural habitats
- Invasive species

In case of impacts in other types of habitats, the biodiversity screening will determine if a further biodiversity assessment is still necessary and, if so, a biodiversity management plan should be prepared.

### Environmental and Social Standard 7: Indigenous peoples
ESFM observation:
Most of the CATALYST Initiative co-ops include population groups who are identified as indigenous. The ILO Convention No.169 on Indigenous and Tribal Peoples in Independent Countries is an international law that provides specific rights for indigenous people (according to the ILO definition) in all Latin American countries except El Salvador and Honduras, where the ILO Convention 107 applies instead.

The IWGIA identifies indigenous peoples in Burkina Faso, Mali and Niger. The NGO, Minority Rights Group International (2005), provides more detailed information on other countries; it identifies potential risk situations related to:

- As per the definition of IFO P&G and the guidance note dated June 2019
- https://www.iwfgia.org/indigenous-peoples-in-independent-countries

## Catalytic Initiative: Indigenous People

The programme's policy with respect to indigenous peoples is as follows:

- The CATALYTIS Initiative will ensure that indigenous people (and historically under-served traditional communities) are included in the project design and implementation, and co-benefits are maximized.
- The programme will ensure that potential negative impacts on indigenous peoples and other similar groups are avoided during the funding period.

The programme will screen all venture sponsors: capacities to comply with the GCF's indigenous peoples policy over the long term (post-implementation period).

Assisting indigenous people in accessing the market through ventures from ethnic minorities (including those qualifying as indigenous economies, as per the GCF's policy) with viable business ideas to apply for funding under the programme. The CATALYTIS Initiative will ensure that ventures are language-capable and languages that are culturally adapted to indigenous peoples, and identify benefits to be derived by indigenous peoples. These benefits should be negotiated beforehand, so they can fully benefit from the programme.

The GCF's indigenous peoples policy is in compliance with ESAT and the GCF's indigenous peoples policy (as per the paragraphs below).

### Impact assessment

The Environmental Evaluation will screen all ventures on applicability of ESAT and the GCF's indigenous peoples policy. The CATALYTIS Initiative will ensure that ventures are language-capable, and identify any potential risks and impacts to manage these risks and impacts according to the GCF's indigenous peoples policy.

By means of due diligence at acceleration stage, Indigenous Peoples experts will be engaged in assessing the venture's compliance with ESAT and the GCF's indigenous peoples policy. These risks are more frequent in some sectors, such as the agroforestry sector, and more generally all sectors where land use is a key issue.

The CATALYTIS Initiative's policy is to avoid, during the pre-acceleration and acceleration stages, the funding of ventures that have not been assessed for compliance with ESAT and the GCF's indigenous peoples policy. However, the programme may in some cases accept into the programme ventures that are planning such assessment at the time of application or shortly after. In these cases, the CATALYTIS Initiative will build on the venture's own assessment to ensure that the venture is compliant with ESAT and the GCF's indigenous peoples policy appears achievable.

Some ventures may suggest business ideas that are not expected to negatively impact indigenous people in a significant way (e.g., small-scale agroforestry projects). In such cases, the CATALYTIS Initiative will require, at minimum, careful and participative impact assessment of the project. The venture must also ensure that it has obtained free, prior informed consent from the indigenous peoples involved.

In such cases, Indigenous Peoples Plans (IPP) will be developed to define the scope of the project, full participation and full impact mitigation, as well as being stronger than any other plan.

### Environmental and Social Standards: Cultural Heritage

- ESME evaluation: Potential impacts of the CATALYTIS Initiative on cultural heritage are mainly related to the use of land and the risk of affecting burial places, archaeological remains, sacred sites or other types of tangible and intangible cultural heritage.
- Impact rating: As for the other land-related impacts, the risk of occurrence of such impacts is extremely low, as it would concern only a very small number of ventures. Impact magnitude without mitigation would be negligible.

### Mitigation approach

The risks will be identified at an early stage in compliance with ESAT, and impacts either avoided or fully mitigated. Use of cultural heritage in a commercial manner will not be allowed. Significant risks will be addressed through the main safeguards and the general CATALYTIS Initiative mitigation hierarchy can be applied to this topic.

## Non-compliance and inclusion of vulnerable people

ESSI consultation and inclusion of vulnerable individuals or groups and assess potential impacts of CATALYTIC initiative activities on these persons. In the case of the CATALYTIC Initiative:

- Vulnerable persons among venture founders or staff with specific needs will be identified by the ESSI specialist at screening stage and will receive specific, confidential support.
- Vulnerable persons will not be involved in any initiatives. Mitigation for this end is included under ESSI2.

## Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH)

Based upon the GCF’s revised ESSI policy and is, therefore, included in this section of the Funding Proposal and in the ESFP:

- **Potential risks and impacts:** For the CATALYTIC Initiative, risks of sexual abuse, exploitation and harassment (SEAH) are considered to be present. The risk is particularly high for women and girls working with the public. In Guinea, for example, women report that they have sometimes asked for sexual services in exchange for jobs. It is therefore essential that all ventures take steps to mitigate this risk by including mandatory training on SEAH, to monitor occurrences, and to implement a zero-tolerance policy.

- **Mitigation:** The following minimum measures in relation to SEAH risks are applied to all ventures:

  - Awareness raising: All ventures will be required to include awareness raising and training on SEAH as part of the ESSI awareness raising and training ‘workstream’ that is being developed. Awareness raising will include the roles of contracted workers and workers in the supply chain.
  - The mandatory training for all staff, including contractors, who are directly responsible to SEAH, at intake into the pre-acceleration programme (screening) and the acceleration programme (onboarding).
  - Ventures receiving grants will be required to apply a zero-tolerance policy with regard to SEAH. All ventures will report on SEAH occurrences as part of the ESSI incident reporting.
  - All ventures will report on SEAH occurrence as part of the ESSI incident reporting.

- **In the acceleration stage:** The risk is considered to be higher in this stage, particularly for women and girls working with the public, as having higher risks will receive additional training on handling complaints and enforcement of zero-tolerance. Workers at each venture will be trained by a third-party provider (e.g., UN Women) who will train all staff and each worker will be surveilled at least once individually during the programme.

- **In the acceleration stage:** The risk is considered to be higher in this stage, particularly for women and girls working with the public. Workers are sensitive to the zero-tolerance policy for any kind of SEAH and sufficiently equipped to address these risks. Ventures will receive additional training on handling complaints and enforcement of a zero-tolerance policy at intake into the acceleration programme (onboarding). The ESSI team will provide an adapted version for Latin America. Actions will be integrated in each venture’s action plan (for example, mandatory training for all staff, including contractors, who are directly responsible to SEAH; raising awareness for the staff; implementation of mechanisms to respond and assess impacts).

## Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) and monitoring

A survivor-centred and gender-responsive GRM for SEAH will be established, as well as a dedicated monitoring, as included in the CATALYTIC Initiative.

### Emergency preparedness and response

During CATALYTIC Initiative implementation, emergency preparedness and response standards apply to premises receiving members of the public during training, capacity building or co-working spaces. The Executing Entities have affirmed that they comply with building safety regulations and will be required to maintain compliance every year.

Human rights

GCC’s SPS policy, as well as GCC’s safeguards management system, put significant emphasis on avoiding infringement of the human rights of others and addressing adverse human rights impacts that ventures may cause or contribute to. ## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan

### G.2.1 Gender Assessment

252. The Gender Assessment (GA) is provided in Annex Bb. The GA was conducted to ensure a gender-sensitive, gender-responsive and gender-inclusive approach for all projects and programmes under the CATALYST/1 Initiative and to meet the requirements of the gender equality objectives of the GCC and GCF. Specific objectives of the GA were to: (i) identify the gender dimensions of the project activities in each sector; (ii) address the gender equality issues raised by local communities, women’s groups, civil society organisations, and other stakeholders; and (iii) provide recommendations on how to integrate a gender perspective into the CATALYST/1 Initiative design. The GA is designed to: (i) reduce gender-based violence and discrimination, as well as promote gender mainstreaming and increase women’s access to the initiative.

Gender and climate change

253. In order for the global economic transformation required to achieve the Paris Agreement, all sectors of the economy, from energy to agriculture, must adopt climate mitigation strategies. Significant technical and market barriers exist that prevent women from participating in these efforts. Women are disproportionately affected by climate change and are more vulnerable to its impacts than men. Women also play a critical role in adapting to and mitigating climate change.

254. A World Bank study concludes that investments in the energy sector can have significant gender co-benefits when interventions are carefully designed and targeted, based on a context-specific understanding of energy access needs and barriers. For example, women’s participation in the design of renewable energy or transport systems, there is growing evidence that gender awareness in the design of such programmes can result in increased efficiency and effectiveness. Women’s participation in the design of energy systems has been shown to reduce costs, producing -25% of all greenhouse gas emissions globally. Industrial agriculture is also a major cause of ecological degradation and climate change. Women are disproportionately affected by these impacts but have important roles across agricultural value chains – as entrepreneurs, producers, processors, distributors and

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 137

## Status of regional gender equality

255. At the international level, West Africa and Latin American countries have ratified most international conventions on women's rights, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and its Optional Protocol. The same cannot be said for many African countries, which have not yet ratified CEDAW or its Optional Protocol. The same is true with regard to implementing the recommendations of the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (1995), Cairo (International Conference on Population and Development; ICPD 1994), Beijing+5 (2000), and the World Summit for Children (WSSC) in 1990. The region has made progress in implementing the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs). For example, Latin America has made significant progress towards gender equality in education, health, and eradication of violence against women (Convention on Belém do Pará) and the Sustainable De-velopment Goal (SDG) 5. West Africa has also made progress in this area. The region is a leader in promoting women's rights, women, peace and security in Africa, for which Côte d'Ivoire was the first signatory. They are also State Parties to the Protocol on Women's Rights in Africa (PRA), signed at Addis Ababa in 1998. In Latin America, the region has made progress towards gender equality since the adoption of the Inter-American Convention on Human Rights for Women in 2004 and the Gender Equality Index (GEI) in July 2004 (the so-called Macri Protocol). Overall, Latin American countries have made greater progress in gender equality than West African countries. This is due to a generally higher rank in the Human Development Index (HDI)178 and the Gender Equality Index (GEI).

256. Latin America. Over the past 30 years, women participating in Latin America's workforce has increased by 11%. Women's participation in the workforce varies significantly across countries: from a low of 49% in Bolivia to a high of 86%, and among the lowest, Costa Rica at 42% and Mexico at 44%. In Peru, for example, 50% of women with children under age five are employed. Women's participation is also higher in rural areas than urban areas (71% vs. 69%). In Venezuela, with similar correlations as other countries in the region, it is 83% in rural areas and 42% in urban areas. In addition to improvements in education, there has been progress in reducing gender disparities in access to credit and land ownership. However, there are still discriminatory regulations against women in the workforce. Nonetheless, there is still a substantial wage gap between men and women (15%). Women also have less access to social security benefits than men. Women's time burden (housework and childcare) is disproportionately allocated to women, resulting in an uneven balance of workload and earnings. In Bolivia, for example, 80% of women work more than 42 hours a week compared with only over 33% and 32% of working age men are engaged in early stage business activities in Ecuador and Chile, respectively. Women's participation is also higher in rural areas than urban areas (71% vs. 69%). In Venezuela, women's entrepreneurship has grown at an unprecedented rate since the mid-1980s. This is a result of the country's economic situation, which has led to a significant increase in female entrepreneurship over the last few decades because they recognize the opportunity and potential innovation, rather than out of necessity.

257. West Africa. Since the mid-2000s, almost every West African country and regional organisation had adopted a gender equality strategy or action plan. However, despite these efforts, there are still significant gender disparities in employment opportunities for women. Women and girls are disadvantaged in many areas and do not enjoy the same opportunities as men to access education, health services, credit, land ownership, and other resources that would allow them to enter the labour market. Only 10% of the West African ventures that have cumulatively raised USD 1 million or more in the past five years were led by women, and neither do they receive venture capital.

## Key barriers faced by women entrepreneurs in Latin America and West Africa

258. Urban-rural divide. The most significant difference in the ability for women to become entrepreneurs is determined by where they live—in rural or urban settings. This disparity is grounded in unequal educational levels, diminished access to credit and other financial services, and different social norms. For example, in Côte d’Ivoire, rural women represent 67% of the workforce and produce 80–90% of food—yet, 75% are illiterate. In contrast, in urban Latin America, women own more than half of all businesses, control over 40% of land and income, and earn more than men. In Latin America, access to credit is a major barrier for women, as many lack collateral or the ability to provide it. Women also face restrictions on land tenure and formal land use rights. This access is often limited to rural areas. In urban Latin America, women have better access to land tenure and formal land use rights. This access is often limited to rural areas. In urban Latin America, women have better access to land tenure and formal land use rights.

260. Sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV) and Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH). Although a number of measures have been implemented to combat SGBV on paper, SGBV remains prevalent in West Africa. For example, the 2014 Demographic and Health Survey (DHS) found that 35% of women aged 15–49 reported having experienced sexual violence at some point in their lives. In Mali, Burkina Faso, and Niger, the prevalence is even higher than elsewhere in the world[1]. Latin America, on the other hand, has the highest rates of pregnancy among adolescent girls (ages 10-19) in the world. The rate of maternal mortality for women aged 20–49 years old is 346 deaths per 100,000 live births[2]. Customary laws continue to be applied on a regular basis in many Latin American countries and are often used as justification for violence against women. These laws have repeatedly affected women, notably in terms of inheritance rights, land rights, access to credit and accessibility of the justice system. In Mexico, 50% of women aged 15–49 years old report having experienced sexual violence at some point in their lives. Femicide (the killing of a woman by her partner or ex-partner) is also a significant problem[3], with Brazil reporting the highest number of cases at 1,728 and Mexico at 940 cases in 2016.

261. Access to entrepreneurship support and capacity building. Gender inequality with regard to literacy and lack of access to credit are major barriers for women entrepreneurs in Latin America and the Caribbean as well as in West Africa. For example, the latest census of Côte d’Ivoire (in 2014) indicates that 60% of Ivorian women are illiterate[4]. In addition, women have less access to credit than men in all countries except Brazil and Uruguay[5]. In Latin America, the problem is not typically related to education, as the region has one of the highest literacy rates in the world.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 139

## Access to finance remains a major barrier for women entrepreneurs in West Africa and Latin America.

### Lack of access to finance is a significant challenge for women entrepreneurs.
- **Limited access**: Women face restrictions regarding opening and running a business, as well as accessing or preparing to access credit. This is particularly true in West African cities where financial institutions are concentrated.
- **Inadequate legislation**: Laws related to registering and operating a business are often inadequate, leading to limited access for women entrepreneurs.

### Limitations in access to finance.
1. Lack of access to formal financial markets challenges women's ability to secure loans or other forms of credit needed for investment.
2. Women’s lack of access and control over collateral assets limits their access to credit.
3. Limited access to savings accounts hinders the accumulation of capital in micro- and large-term amounts that hinder business expansion, especially for women.

### Narrow perceptions of entrepreneurship.
- **Limited role models**: Many women entrepreneurs are limited to an idea of Western businessmen and women in offices. For many women, the concept of entrepreneurship is associated with a lack of family support or financial resources.
- **Short-term focus**: Women often form teams of employees and observe short-term growth. However, many of these same women engage in work after their children reach school age, which can limit long-term business development.

### Gender gaps in decision-making power.
- **Latin America, stereotypes and gender roles continue to prevail**. In Mexico society, for example, 48% of women aged 15–29 years old have no say over the spending of their children’s property, while only 10% consider men and not women should hold decision-making positions.

### Under-representation of women trainers/coaches.
- **Limited mentorship**: The combination of needing general business experience, understanding gender issues, and having a track record in mentoring or coaching is challenging. Many staff members are pre-accelerators or accelerators who can advise climate ventures. When the gender element is beyond their expertise, they may not be able to provide adequate guidance.
- **Limited training**: Women trainers have more empathy for other women and, by sharing own experiences during the training and coaching, they are better equipped to help women overcome their challenges.

### 27.11 Recommendation: 11 At the pre-acceleration stage, ventures’ leadership and management tend to be fluid. This provides an opportunity for the venture to start on gender mainstreaming in ventures' management and operations. A tailored approach (products / services / business model / capacity) will be essential. For the ventures to become gender-smart, the following topics for specific training are recommended:

- Ventures should gather data on gender differences.
- Ventures should recognize the value of including sex-disaggregated data in their market research and in their employee recruitment practices. Clarifying gendered differences will reveal opportunities, enable validation of assumptions, and help to identify areas where further research is needed.
- Ventures should be willing to identify priority areas and the type of support needed to have their own development plans.

### 27.12 Recommendation: 12 At the acceleration stage, ventures, and especially those that have not gone through the pre-acceleration programme may already have rigid management structures which may not allow for gender mainstreaming in how they operate. The lack of awareness of the connection between gender equality and business performance is a major barrier to gender inclusion. For many, the link of improving the connection be- tween gender equality and business performance is not clear. If female gender is viewed as something that can impact the bottom line, it is less likely to be prioritised by the venture. Therefore, the following benefits of gender inclusion should be emphasised:

- Tailor mass-products and services.
- Gender-disaggregated data, which can inform marketing tactics, strategic messaging and sales efforts.
- Awareness raising, particularly from international financiers with gender strategies and gender key performance indicators.

| Entities have the capability to provide gender mainstreaming | Sub-Activities 2.1.4 and 2.1.6 include development of regional reports and action plans specifically on gender equality and diversity. |
| Ensure that all training materials are gender-sensitive, including cross-learning and document cross-learning during streaming between Executive Board meetings | The log frame contains gender differentiated indicators and targets, as well as Action Plan activities. |
| Review and update the Gender Policy to include gender diversity in the CATALIST+T Initiative’s governance structure | Sub-Activities 1.1.4 and 2.1.4 include development of regional reports and action plans specifically on gender equality and diversity. The report will inform infield collection and collective actions on gender. |
| Ensure that all communications and accreditations are inclusive of all genders, including applicants of all genders | The Spanish version will be excluded if the venture’s product or service has obvious cultural barriers to its use by women. |
| Apply a gender lens when selecting ventures for pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes | All major communication materials will be reviewed and approved by the CATALIST+T Initiative’s regional gender specialist once per region. |

### Screening

Selection: as part of the scaling system, a venture is favoured (up to 10% of the overall portfolio) if it has more than one founder and at least 50% women founders. If there is only one founder, the majority of founders (50%) are women; and/or (b) it will offer increased access to finance for women entrepreneurs; or (c) it will have a positive impact on increasing women’s wages, increasing female participation in male-dominated sectors.

Adapt the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes’ language to be inclusive of all genders

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 143

## G.3 Financial management and procurement

### G.3.1 Disbursement and Procurement Arrangements

#### Fiduciary standards

279. The Executing Entities – Climate KIC, Tec de Monterrey, Impact Hub Abidjan and IPED – will sign subsidiary agreements with GIZ as the Accredited Entity, based on GIZ standard operating procedures for financing contracts. Within these agreements, the Executing Entities are responsible for implementing and managing the funding received from GIZ. The Executing Entities are to manage and operate the programmes. The Executive Entities are responsible for implementing and managing the funding received from GIZ. The Executing Entities will be responsible for ensuring that the funds disbursed by GIZ are used exclusively for the purposes specified in the subsidiary agreement with GIZ.

280. The financial management of the programme will follow GIZ’s internal rules and regulations. GIZ has bank accounts with Deutsche Bundesbank and Commerzbank. GIZ will not open a special bank account for GCF projects and programmes funded by GCF. GIZ will use its own bank accounts to manage the funds received from GIZ’s other bodies set up under separate cost units exclusively for the funds disbursed by GIZ for each budgetary year. The financial management of the programme will be carried out in accordance with GIZ’s internal rules and regulations, including generally accepted accounting principles and procedures accepted by the German Government.

As a general principle, GIZ disburses funds to recipients in accordance with the progress of the programmes.

#### Procurement

281. In the case of procurement by GIZ, GIZ will follow its own procurement guidelines. GIZ is required to comply with the relevant contracting rules established in the German Act against Restraints of Competition (GWB), the General Public Procurement Law (GGV) and the Federal Public Service Code (Bundesverwaltungsgesetz – BVerwG). The Federal Public Service Code provides for a simplified procedure for public service contracts. When procuring services, construction work and supplies. When awarding contracts for supply of goods or services to be used in connection with the execution of a contract or grant agreement, the external Executing Entities will observe their own national regulations for public procurement.

In principle, the regulations of the Executing Entities’ country are to be observed; the procurement procedures

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 / PAGE 144

## Independent external auditing

Independent external auditors will perform annual financial audits of the programme in line with International Auditing Standards. GIZ will be responsible for selecting and engaging the external auditors.

### G.A. Disclosure of funding proposal

No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
- With respect to the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity confirms that it will provide two copies of the funding proposal:
  - a full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanation of the nature and extent of the confidentiality and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure policy; and
  - redacted version to be published on the GCF website.
- The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

# ANNEXES

## H.1 Mandatory annexes

- **Annex 1**: NDA no objection letter(s) (template provided)
- **Annex 2**: Feasibility study - and a market study if applicable
- **Annex 3**: Economic or financial analyses in spreadsheet format
- **Annex 4**: Detailed budget plan (template provided)
- **Annex 5**: Implementation timeline including key project/programme milestones (template provided)
- **Annex 6**: E&S document corresponding to the E&S category (A, B or C; or I1, II2 or III3) (E&S disclosure form provided)

### Optional annexes

- **Annex 7**: Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF)
    - Others (please specify e.g. - Resettlement Action Plan, Resettlement Policy Framework, Indigenous People’s Plan, Land Acquisition Plan, etc.)
- **Annex 8**: Summary of consultation and stakeholder engagement plan
- **Annex 9**: Legal due diligence (regulation, taxation and insurance)
- **Annex 10**: Procurement plan (template provided)
- **Annex 11**: Monitoring and evaluation plan (template provided)
- **Annex 12**: AE fee request (template provided)
- **Annex 13**: Co-financing commitment letter if applicable (template provided)
- **Annex 14**: Term sheet including a detailed disbursement schedule and, if applicable, repayment schedule

## H.2 Other annexes as applicable

- **Annex 15**: Evidence of internal approval (template provided)
- **Annex 16**: Maps(s) indicating the location of proposed interventions
- **Annex 17**: Project implementation plan (template provided)
- **Annex 18**: Appraisal, due diligence or evaluation report for proposals based on up-scaling or replicating a pilot project
- **Annex 19**: Procedures for contracting procurement by third parties or executing entities undertaking projects financed by the entity
- **Annex 20**: KYC (Know Your Customer) assessment
- **Annex 21**: Operations manual (Operations and maintenance)
- **Annex 22**: Assessment of GHG emission reductions and their monitoring and reporting (for mitigation and adaptation)
- **Annex X**: Other references

*Please note that a funding proposal will be considered complete only upon receipt of all the applicable supporting documents.*

# Undersecretariat of International Financial Relations for Development

To: The Green Climate Fund ("GCF")

Buenos Aires, 19 September 2022

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by Deutsche Gesellschaft fuer Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH regarding CATALYSEIT Initiative: Concerted Action To Accelerate Local ES Technologies - Latin America and West Africa

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the title of GIZ's initiative "CATALYSEIT Initiative: Concerted Action To Accelerate Local ES Technologies in Latin America and West Africa" in Argentina, Bolivia, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Ecuador, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Mexico, Nicaragua, Paraguay, Peru, Brazil, Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Kenya, Liberia, Nigeria, Senegal, Sierra Leone, Togo.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of Argentina, Mr. Leonardo Gargal, the Undersecretary of International Financial Relations for Development of the Ministry of Economy, the National Designated Authority of Argentina.

Pursuant to GCF decision B/II/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of Argentina has no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal.
(b) The programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities and strategies.
(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the programme as included in the funding proposals is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal has been completed.

We also confirm that our no objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Kind regards,

**Mr. Leonardo A. Gargal**

Undersecretary of International Financial Relations for Development

National Designated Authority of Argentina
