# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s review of FP198

## Proposal name: CATALIST Initiative: Concerted Action to Accelerate Local 15<sup>th</sup> Technologies – Latin America and West Africa

### Accredited entity: Deutsche Gesellschaft für internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH

#### Countries covered:
- Latin America:
    - Argentina, Bolivia, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Ecuador, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Mexico, Nicaragua, Panama, Peru
- West Africa:
    - Benin, Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Guinea, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Senegal, Togo

### Project/programme size: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: Medium – High

#### 1.1 Impact potential
- Countries in Africa and Latin America have a rich base of renewable energy resources.
- Despite this resource base, renewable energy has not penetrated at a level that would justify the reserve capacity standard (widely accepted, best-practice) tools and frameworks.

#### 2. Basic theories upon which the success of the proposed intervention hinges are given in the following:
a) Innovative, low-emission technologies and business models exist to demonstrate the market feasibility of leapfrog emissions-intensive developments at limited risk to the economy.
b) Climate ventures in developing countries have significant opportunities to leap-frog emissions-intensive developments at limited risk to the economy.


---
**Page 181**
---

### Climate Ventures can provide solutions that are currently neglected by markets (so-called "breakthrough technologies") and can improve products and services that are already available ("incremental technologies").

#### c) Climate ventures in developing countries are however being prevented from achieving their full potential because:
- The barriers to the development of a robust and sustainable state of the clean sector in both regions and the barriers indicated in paragraph 1 above, notably the sector's limited access to venture capital finance.
- The lack of an enabling environment for climate ventures and their required tools and frameworks, and the mismatch between the needs of climate ventures and those required to successfully access venture capital finance.

#### Although governments of countries in W&LA are playing an important and active role through various initiatives and policies, including financial support, this alone will be inadequate to support climate ventures in the two regions to undertake game-changing projects that can deliver significant mitigation impacts. The proposed project will contain actions that can incentivize and catalyze government interventions and foreign direct investment (FDI) into climate ventures. It is important to note that climate ventures can play in both regions to deliver innovative climate actions; and

#### When the objectives of the funding proposal are achieved, venture capital flows into the clean sector in W&LA will increase significantly. The amount of international venture capital inflow to countries of both regions is increasing rapidly, albeit from a low base. This is due to several factors including: (i) the growing recognition that mitigation represents a small but growing proportion of overall venture capital; and the countries and local venture finance firms have a strong appetite among regionally active venture investors.

The proposed interventions – Concerted Action to Accelerate Local 15T Technologies (CATALYTIC) – will provide a regional framework for the development of targeted technical assistance and investment grant platforms that will facilitate the building of a portfolio of early-stage climate ventures in Latin America and West Africa. The objective of each regional CATALYTIC will be:

a) Trigger the flow of venture capital investments into start-ups and young businesses with the highest climate mitigation impact and business growth potential; and

b) Provide support to:
i. Climate ventures;
ii. Pre-accelerators, accelerators and entrepreneur support organizations (ESOs); and
iii. Venture capital firms and other venture investors.

5. At the core of each regional CATALYTIC will be:

a) An acceleration programme – With each acceleration programme focusing on providing support to early-stage climate ventures in both regions to help them develop their minimum viable products and ensure investability for venture capital; and

b) Pre-acceleration programmes – CATALYTICs foresee 60 less mature, pre-venture seed ventures and each of the two regional pre-acceleration programmes will provide support to these projects or services through the two regional pre-acceleration programmes. The support will cover:
i. Technical assistance (capacity-building, mentoring, networking, etc.);


---
**Page 182**
---

## Financial assistance (in the form of a grant to each climate venture of EUR 15,000 over pre-agreed costs) and Further support to climate ventures will include:

- Community-building and idea creation activities for potential climate entrepreneurs in both regions with focus on women and other underrepresented groups in entrepreneurship; and
- Technical assistance for the broader venture ecosystem in both regions, to enable the development of a more robust and sustainable business environment, building businesses to build and sustain (climate) businesses;

### The independent Technical Advisory Panel (independent TAP) observed that the approach used by the GCF in its climate venture initiative (CVI) in West Africa (WA) and Latin America and the Caribbean (LA) is different from the approaches used in many mitigation projects (either single projects or programmes). While the CVI is a stand-alone project, it does not require submission to GCF for funding, usually emphasizes project level activities at country levels. Even where the CVI is part of a larger programme, the CVI is often funded separately and independently from other projects can be directly taken from documents such as the nationally determined contributions of countries, climate change plans, or national development plans, all of which are equally available to all countries. The CVI will be implemented in two regions: West Africa (WA) and Latin America and the Caribbean (LA). In addition to the CVIs, there is also a separate project that will be implemented to deliver greenhouse gas (GHG) emission reductions. In addition to the CVIs, there is also a separate project that will be implemented to deliver greenhouse gas (GHG) emission reductions.

### The key differences between the approach proposed in the CATALYST programme and the status quo are described on pages 4 through 6 of this document. A summary description of the CATALYST's approach as follows:

a) Funding allocation for the CATALYST Initiative per region is determined by the existing budgetary allocations of CVIs in each region. At this stage, information on climate mitigation projects that the CVIs will be pursuing in this programme is not available.

b) Only climate ventures from countries whose national designated authority has provided a no objection letter (NOL) will be eligible to apply to the regional programmes (pre-approval).

c) Allocation of investment between the LA and WA regions is determined by the budgetary needs of each region, taking into account the CVIs for each region for the programme. The characteristics of the budgetary needs in each region are different due to a relationship with the mitigation projects that will eventually be organized by each participating climate venture in each region but on the realities of the different national designated authorities and their respective capacity in each region (and hence the amount of training ventures "hand-holding" country missions).

### ii. Participating climate ventures do not need to specify mitigation project at the level of entrance into the screening process, since the budgets per region will be based on the number of climate ventures that are selected for further screening and frontier markets in the two regions. The climatons that will be held per year in each selected country following the screening (one climatons per year


---
**Page 183**
---

over three years for each participating country) will link selected countries will provide a basis for activities in each country that will participate in the CATALYST Initiative. This will dictate the number and types of activities (training, mentor “hand holding”, country missions etc., that must be provided in each country.

iii. A Climathon is a place-based programme that harnesses the power and imagination of stakeholders to co-create ideas to tackle local climate mitigation challenges. Over a span of 12-72 hours, a diverse group of participants, typically including government officials, private sector representatives, civil society organizations (CSOs), academics, students, and software developers, come together in one location to generate new climate ideas. Climathons represent a useful first step in the idea generation and business creation process.

iv. If the application is successful, the climate ventures will receive technical and financial assistance from the CATALYST Initiative. This financial assistance will consist of (i) seed funding for pre-acceleration programmes (Latin America and West Africa); and (ii) repayable cost grants for participation in pre-acceleration programmes (Latin America and West Africa).

v. The CATALYST Initiative will follow a market-led approach to scaling up the most promising climate ventures, which will be subject to some limitations on eligibility (screening) and competitive selection (for both Sub-Activities 1.4.2/2.4.2 relating to pre-acceleration programmes , and Sub-Activities 1.4.1/2.4.1.1 relating to venture selection).

vi. vii. For this reason, it is not possible to estimate ex ante how much GCF funding will go to countries, as allocations per country will be a pull-out from successful applications.

8. In the CATALYST Initiative, the climate ventures will be selected without country quotas, as these would undermine the programme’s intent to select only the ventures with highest potential for mitigation and adaptation. This will therefore be determined on a case-by-case basis according to budget allocations.

viii. The only direct budget will however be allocated for country annualisation, but this represents only 10% of total programme funding. The Climathons are important activities in the identification of the CIV that will be sources of pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes.

ix. Through the climathon and subsequent open screening, the CATALYST Initiative will develop pipelines of pre-seed and seed-stage CIVs in both regions and countries that have submitted applications. These will be selected for further development and investment opportunities through the sale of their products or services. (For reasons of conservative calculation, only the seed stage ventures are considered to generate emission reductions.)


---
**Page 184**
---

10. The CATALYST Initiative differs from typical programmatic approaches in that the venture capital companies are the vectors for emission reductions, not projects. The finance companies will be selected for CATALYST Initiative support based on their market prospects and their ability to deliver emission reductions through their investments in low-carbon, cost-competitive demand, business models, management team execution capabilities, etc. The mitigation estimates are calculated using a specific tool (the Climate Impact Forecast [CIF] tool) that enables users to input very specific GHG mitigation estimates to be compared with the baseline emissions of the good/service sold by each venture capital company. According to our desktop report on the use of CIF tool combined with actual market sales data, the result is the estimate of post-GHG mitigation estimates. Therefore, to calculate CATALYST Initiative emission reductions, the following steps are required:

a) An emission reduction estimate per functional unit of each good/service sold, for example: 10 kg CO2e per ton of soybean oil sold; 50 kg CO2e per ton of corn silage sold; rooftop photovoltaic unit installed, tonnes of compost produced; or hectare of low-emission agriculture applied. These per-unit emission reductions will be calculated using the CIF tool.

b) Sales data: the number of functional units sold by each venture per time interval. This data is provided to us by the venture capital companies and verified by third parties responsible for the regional acceleration programmes (Tecnológico de Monterrey in Latin America and Investisseurs & Partenaires Entrepreneurs et Développement [IPED] in West Africa) as part of their venture engagement processes.

13. Application of the CIF tool, accompanied by third-party expert validation, will take place three times during project implementation: once to estimate GHG emission reductions achieved by CV sales activity:

a) At first at the end of the pre-acceleration programme;

b) Second when a CV is accepted into the acceleration programme (except when the applicant's venture has recently graduated from the pre-acceleration programme); and

c) Third at the end of the acceleration programme, using updated data (updated emission factors, etc.).

16. There are uncertainties in the ex-ante estimation of GHG emission reductions that will be delivered by the CVs that finally selected in the context of the CATALYST Initiative. These estimates can be reduced through:

a) Using a road-tested and completely transparent estimation tool (the CIF tool), so that assumptions and parameter values can be scrutinized and tested;

b) Use of robust data on the performance of the venture growth and survival rates, tapers rate, the GCF attribution coefficient; etc.;

c) Use of Monte Carlo sensitivity modelling to contextualize mitigation estimates against statistical distributions; and

d) Periodic updating of parameter values, such that the modeling reflects the latest empirical realities.

15. The independent TAP evaluated the CIF tool used to estimate GHG emission reductions in the CATALYST Initiative and came to the following conclusions:


---
**Page 185**
---

a) The CIF tool is not in the same class as the project-level methodologies commonly used in mitigation projects submitted to GCF. While the status quo methodologies are designed to estimate GHG ER at the project granular level, the CIF tool has been designed to estimate the climate metrics delivery (mitigation) and services are the sources of GHG ER.

b) The tool used for the CATALYST Initiative, which is based on the principle of avoided emissions, is expected to deliver a very conservative GHG ER, which in the view of the independent TAP is acceptable for establishing the climate metrics delivery (mitigation) - one of the two key climate metrics.

c) The avoided emissions potential - defined as the difference in lifecycle GHG emissions between an existing baseline solution (e.g., an internal combustion engine vehicle) and a climate venture solution (e.g., an electric vehicle) - is used to estimate the GHG ER.

d) This will deliver conservative estimates of GHG ER because it focuses on the difference in lifecycle GHG emissions between an existing baseline solution (e.g., an internal combustion engine vehicle) and a climate venture solution (e.g., an electric vehicle).

e) This will be calculated for each climate venture that is successful in receiving funding for each region and for countries that have delivered a NOL to the CATALYST Initiative; and

f) As stated in paragraph 13 above, the results of this estimation process will be reviewed and monitored by the independent TAP during the project implementation phase and upon implementation. An independent expert will also be engaged to validate the data used and the GHG ER estimates.

| New area | Ambittype | Description |
|---|---|---|

| Smart city solutions | Systems and equipment that integrate digital energy saving or emission reduction technologies into the urban environment (e.g., smart lighting design (e.g., use of shading, orientation and natural ventilation). |
| Smart buildings | Manufacturing processes and equipment that use less raw material, water and energy than traditional alternatives. |
| Sustainable building materials | Materials – such as cement, wood and insulation foam – that have a lower environmental impact during their production process or are made from recycled or renewable resources (e.g., it is CO2 neutral). |
| Smart buildings | Systems and equipment that integrate digital energy saving or emission reduction technologies into the urban environment (e.g., smart lighting design (e.g., use of shading, orientation and natural ventilation). |

### c) IMPACT HUB ABDIDAN

IMPACT Hub Abdidan is a non-profit association dedicated to encouraging entrepreneurship in Africa.

### d) Impact Hub Abdidan?

Impact Hub Abdidan is a commercial pre-accelerator and accelerator (limited liability company) founded in 2019, offering services to entrepreneurs and innovators in Côte d'Ivoire. It is part of the IMPACT Hub global network, a collaborative community of >25,000 entrepreneurs, support organisations and investors.

### e) Stichting Climate-KIC International Foundation

The Climate-KIC International Foundation is an independent Dutch non-profit with public benefit status in the Netherlands. The Climate-KIC International Foundation owns 90% per cent of Climate-KIC Holding BV. The remaining 10% are held by Associate Climate-KIC, a limited liability company focused on raising and allocating funds for climate-relevant innovations. Stichting Climate-KIC International Foundation will receive funding from GIZ to deliver the acceleration programme in West Africa as well as to deliver climate impact advisory services and climate impact support in the framework of the regional pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes.

### 24.
It is the independent TAP's view that the risk of the CATALLS-T Initiative not delivering effective climate ventures in the two regions during the period of implementation, and thus not achieving the GHG emission reductions from the CVs significantly contribute to the long run to deep GHG emission reductions in the countries involved. The national and regional GHG reduction metrics will depend on the success of the CATALLS-T Initiative and the ability to scale up and expand the impact of the CVs identified by this interaction.

### 25.
The CATALLS-T Initiative is expected to reduce 58 MCO2e of GHG emissions during its first six years (influence period). This will be achieved through the sale of low-emission products/services by the 60 CVs that will be supported by the two regional funds and the GCF.

### 26.
The GHG reductions that will be delivered by the CATALLS-T Initiative in the two targeted regions (LA and WA) were presented in annexure 22 submitted by the AE with the funding proposal. It is expected to reduce 58 MCO2e of GHG emissions during its first six years; it is expected that some or all of the 60 CVs will yield GHG emission reductions and that after the GCF intervention, their GHG reduction will increase as better practices are adopted and more efficient technologies are applied. Given the fact that the methodology framework applied (MOREE and CIF tool) use the best available information at the time of application and the replacement products that will be sold by the 60 acceleration CVs, then the GHG ER calculations will be conservative.

### 27.
The independent TAP considers that the expected mitigation impacts of this programme should be ranked as medium to high. This is because the characteristics of the activities that will produce the GHG ER (product of the climate venture) are not known with certainty and there may be a significant degree of uncertainty in the fund leveraging ability of the CATALLS-T Initiative intervention. Proper monitoring of programme results can be used to update the GHG ER results to document the actual mitigation impacts, which would allow for the application of the complete mitigation metric of the intervention at a high scale.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential
**Scale: High**

The programme will deliver a paradigm shift as it will catalyse the development of CVs that can effectively deliver products that were absent in the base years in the medium to long term. Not only will this be a significant contribution to the achievement of the GCF, but it will also catalyse and engender the leveraging of private sector funds that can expand the horizon, quality and quantity of GHG ER that will be delivered by this intervention over a medium to longer term.

The flexible design of the CATALLS-T Initiative will enable scaling up and expanding the ability to scale up and expand the impact of CVs beyond the two regions. It will also allow for other regions in Africa, and other Latin American countries and other developing regions and countries globally. This is important as the bottom-up nature of the approach used in the


---
**Page 194**
---

CATALYST Initiative will deliver ways and means to reduce or eliminate barriers that usually mitigate against the success of developing low carbon infrastructure in developing countries.

The successful delivery of products from the CATALYST Initiative will contribute to increase the potential for scaling up the programme in the regions covered as well as in other similar regions. The CATALYST Initiative will also provide a replicable model for scaling up through capacity created in the intervention, proven cases of successful business models, lessons learned and best practices.

Successful implementation of the CATALYST Initiative in the two regions will deliver very robust lessons learned. A knowledge management plan of the CATALYST Initiative is provided to ensure that these lessons are captured and disseminated. The knowledge management plan will catalogue important lesson learned on climate venture sourcing, identification, pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes, gender entrepreneurship, and other relevant topics. This will enable others planning similar initiatives in the two regions as well as other regions. The CATALYST Initiative will also provide a replicable model for scaling up through capacity created within and between the regional programmes. The knowledge-sharing platform will be:

- Online, free of charge, in every participating country in the regions covered; and
- Available in other regions covered by the CATALYST Initiative globally, thereby facilitating widespread diffusion of knowledge.

Feedback from participants and stakeholders involved in the activities by the CATALYST Initiative will deliver a lesson learned database that can be built into new programmes in the regions and beyond, which will result in the replicability of the CATALYST’s initiative elsewhere.

- Annually, a summary report on the progress made with respect to the proposal and annexes reviewed, a summary of the knowledge base that will be created by successful implementation of the current initiative will include:

a) Mentoring and coaching;
b) Regional pre-acceleration programmes;
c) Regional acceleration programmes;
d) Climate gender entrepreneurship tools; and
e) ESC tools for pre-accelerators, accelerators and venture capital funds.

The CATALYST Initiative will therefore deliver important knowledge-sharing, learning and information.

Other potential key paradigm shift metrics that will be delivered by the CATALYST initiative include:

a) Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies;
b) Contribution to climate-resilient development pathway.

These metrics, especially the means of successful delivery and their impacts on the success of the CATALYST Initiative, will provide a strong basis for scaling up and for a paradigm shift.

The programme will also be able to demonstrate paradigm shift potential by ensuring an effective monitoring, reporting and verification system and coordination of the programme aspects with other relevant stakeholders. The programme will also ensure that all components of the CATALYST’s Initiative in each region and country covered by this programme. This will help avoid the risk of creating a new carbon bubble or “locking-in” of a high carbon alternative path as seen in previous efforts and actively promoting the implementation of low carbon development.


---
**Page 195**
---

1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The programme is well aligned with Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and aims to reduce GHG emissions. It will contribute significantly to SDG5 ("Achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls"), SDG8 ("Promote sustained, inclusive and sustainable economic growth, full and productive employment and decent work for all"), SDG13 ("Build resilient infrastructure, promote inclusive and sustainable industrialization and foster innovation"), SDG 10 (“Reduce inequality within and among countries”), SDG 13 “Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts”, and SDG 17 (strengthen the means of implementation and revitalize the global partnership for sustainable development).

Beneficial CVIs in the CATALYST Initiative will deliver further contributions to other SDGs. The expected sectoral breakdowns of beneficial ventures in the West African acceleration programme are: agriculture and food security-20 per cent; water, sanitation and power generation-30 per cent agriculture, forestry and land-use-20 per cent buildings, cities, industry and transport-15 per cent; and energy and urban transport-15 per cent. Such ventures have the potential to contribute to the following SDGs:

a) SDG 2 (“End hunger, achieve food security and improved nutrition, and promote sustainable agriculture”) - through ventures that offer solutions in areas such as agroforestry, land-use monitoring systems, rural infrastructure and improved agricultural practices;

b) SDG 6 (Ensure availability and sustainable management of water and sanitation for all) - through ventures that offer, for example, enhanced sanitation or water quality solutions that simultaneously reduce GHG emissions (e.g. through more efficient pumps or enhanced wastewater treatment plants that also produce energy from the waste stream or water);

c) SDG 7 (Ensure access to affordable, reliable, sustainable and modern energy for all) - through ventures that focus on energy efficiency, distributed renewable energy solutions or pay-as-you-go energy access models;

d) SDG 11 (“Make cities and human settlements inclusive, safe, resilient and sustainable”) - through ventures that offer mobility solutions, waste management products or services, and alternative and sustainable building material for inclusive and sustainable urbanization;

e) SDG 12 (Ensure sustainable consumption and production patterns) - through ventures that focus on resource efficiency in areas such as water resources, source management, recycling, composting and resource efficiency.

Major environmental co-benefits from this programme will be reduced emissions of air pollutants. The CATALYST Initiative is expected to deliver a range of energy sector projects that will be delivered by energy sector products of the CVIs included in the CATALYST Initiative. Environmental co-benefits are expected through reductions in PM10 and NOx emissions. The major concern for air pollution in the cities is PM10, and NOx.

Apart from the environmental co-benefits discussed above, the programme is expected to also deliver significant economic benefits. These include improved air quality and reduced noise in urban areas through use of cleaner energy systems in the CATALYST Initiative. Economic co-benefits will be delivered through the monetary value of reduced air emissions (GHG and air pollutants). The programme includes a gender action plan


---
**Page 196**
---

(GAP) is expected to be applied to all activities in the programme. According to the funding proposal, the programme’s GAP includes actions and targets on (i) improved access for women e-motion public transportation; (ii) improved sex-disaggregated data collection on urban transport; (iii) increased awareness of gender equality issues among stakeholders involved in raising and infrastructure to reduce sexual harassment in public transport. The programme implementation will be through the CATALYST initiative.

Given alignment of the programme to the range of SDGs discussed above and the various co-benefits that will be delivered, the independent TAP concluded that the potential of the CATALYST Initiative significantly contribute to the sustainable development of countries in the two regions.


### 1.4 Needs of the recipient
Scale: High

The two regions targeted by the CATALYST initiative (the LA and WA regions) and the countries in the two regions that are able to successfully access CFI in the initiative with NCA in place are most vulnerable to climate change in the region, for example mean average temperature over 30 years has translated into melting and shrinkage of glaciers with the loss of a major source of fresh water for consumption and irrigation and hydrological changes. The region is also experiencing increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as heat waves, droughts, floods, and storms due to climate change causing shifting rainfall across the region, with increase in rainfall in the north-west and south-east of the region and decrease in rainfall in the central part of the region. Climate change problem in many of the coastal areas of the A region: Relative sea-level rise is extremely high in the region, which has led to increased flooding and erosion contributing to increased coastal flooding in low-lying areas and shoreline retreat along most sandy coasts. The increase in frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as heat waves, droughts, floods, and storms due to climate change causing shifting rainfall across the region, with increase in rainfall in the north-west and south-east of the region and decrease in rainfall in the central part of the region. Climate change problem in many of the coastal areas of the A region: Relative sea-level rise is extremely high in the region, which has led to increased flooding and erosion contributing to increased coastal flooding in low-lying areas and shoreline retreat along most sandy coasts.

The West African region is also exhibiting various negative impacts of climate change including increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as heat waves, droughts, floods, and storms due to climate change causing shifting rainfall across the region, with increase in rainfall in the north-west and south-east of the region and decrease in rainfall in the central part of the region. Climate change problem in many of the coastal areas of the A region: Relative sea-level rise is extremely high in the region, which has led to increased flooding and erosion contributing to increased coastal flooding in low-lying areas and shoreline retreat along most sandy coasts.

Scale: Medium - High

another (mm. 2b and 2c), the CATALIST® Initiative is aligned with nationally determined contributions, national climate policies and strategies, National Communications to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change, Technology Needs Assessments and National Adaptation Plans, as well as other relevant international and regional responses. To qualify, countries must have: signed a NDA; and selected CVs during the screening process of the CATALIST® Initiative.

Another strong indication of country ownership is the type of stakeholder interaction that was held during programme inception and planning. Some of the work activities during programming were carried out in collaboration with stakeholders. These include:

53. Over 20 years, the CATALYST Initiative will achieve GCF-attributable emission reductions of 3.8 MCO2eq. Accordingly, the mitigation cost to GCF is EUR 6.8/MCO2eq.

54. The total estimated carbon price for the period covered by the project is 160 €/tCO2eq. The CATALYST Initiative will achieve GCF-attributable emission reductions resulting from the ventures accelerated in the two regions. It is important to note that the 3.8 MCO2eq central estimate represents the emission reductions after the application of a 50 per cent discount rate on the total estimated carbon price for the period, taking into account the probability of failure of the projects and the likelihood of causality between the CATALYST Initiative's activities and the venture survival and growth rate. The total estimated carbon price is based on the central estimate of 160 €/tCO2eq in the total capital estimated to be raised by the ventures over 20 years, well beyond expiry of the GCF project period. This implies that the CATALYST Initiative will achieve emission reductions at a cost of approximately EUR 35/MCO2eq for the period covered by the project (over 20 years - approximately EUR 179 million in Latin America and EUR 215 million in West Africa).

55. Some of the important results of the analysis carried out on the CATALYST Initiative reported above are:

a) The base-case assumption for the shadow price of carbon is EUR 60/MCO2eq.

b) This estimate is the mid-range estimate of the required carbon price needed to meet the Paris Agreement's goal of limiting global temperature increase to 1.5°C by mid-century; and

c) With cumulative emission reductions of 3.8 MCO2eq, a carbon price of EUR 60/MCO2eq, and a discount rate of 10 per cent, the CATALYST Initiative yields an attractive economic internal rate of return of 19 per cent. The economic net present value is estimated at EUR 512 million, based on a 10 per cent discount rate as customarily used in economic analyses.

# EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

Governments, international organizations and stakeholders across the globe have recognized that gender equality and women’s empowerment are fundamental to socioeconomic development of countries. The importance of gender equality has been articulated in various global frameworks, including the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs), the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the Paris Agreement on climate change. Harnessing gender equality is one of the most powerful instruments available to implement the Paris Agreement and an unequivocal way to mitigate trade-offs or negative impacts of climate change while achieving multiple co-benefits. Gender equality is also key to reducing the existing gender credit gap in both regions as well as closing the gender gap in entrepreneurship, which would have a significant impact on women-led SMEs.

In Africa, the female gender gap in 2021 was estimated at US$42 billion (€39.8 billion). The Latin American countries are ranked higher in the Human Development Index (HDI) and Gender Inequality Index (GII). Most West African countries are ranked among the bottom two on both indices, with the exception of Côte d’Ivoire, Senegal, and Nigeria.

In the Executing Entities' (EEs') host countries of Côte d’Ivoire, Senegal and Mexico, women entrepreneurs face a range of challenges in accessing finance, overcoming traditional gender- and culture-based barriers and lack of broader enabling gender ecosystem. Although the lived experience of women entrepreneurs in West Africa is very different from that in Latin America, lack of stable access to finance remains one of the most significant constraints for women entrepreneurs across both regions.

In Mexico, women face a number of challenges including: limited access to capital; low levels of education and training; and fewer role models and mentors. In Côte d’Ivoire and Senegal, women face similar challenges in terms of lack of access to finance, traditional gender barriers, and lack of enabling ecosystems.

In the Executing Entities' (EEs') host countries of Mexico, Côte d’Ivoire, Senegal and Nigeria, women entrepreneurs face a range of challenges in accessing finance, overcoming traditional gender- and culture-based barriers and lack of broader enabling gender ecosystem. Although the lived experience of women entrepreneurs in West Africa is very different from that in Latin America, lack of stable access to finance remains one of the most significant constraints for women entrepreneurs across both regions.

In Mexico, women face a number of challenges including: limited access to capital; low levels of education and training; and fewer role models and mentors. In Côte d’Ivoire and Senegal, women face similar challenges in terms of lack of access to finance, traditional gender barriers, and lack of enabling ecosystems.

Based on the Gender Assessment (GA) concrete recommendations have been developed. The recommendations act as the basis for the project Gender Action Plan (GAP) to be implemented by Impact Hub Africa and IPED in West Africa and Tecnológico de Monterrey in Latin America, with trans-regional support from Daimler AG and GIZ.

## GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ANALYSIS REPORT

# INTRODUCTION

The CATALIST initiative, hereafter named "the project", is grounded in local knowledge and context, an investor mind-set and a determination to unlock entrepreneurial potential in order to spread climate innovation at scale and in accelerated timelines. The project works with local regional pre-accelerators and accelerators to co-design and co-implement regional climate venture ecosystems. It has three main regional initiatives: CATALIST Africa (CATALIST Afrique), one in Côte d'Ivoire/ Senegal for French-speaking West Africa; CATALIST Latin America, which focuses on the Spanish-speaking world; and CATALIST Hub (Hub), a global initiative for climate ventures, accompanied by technical assistance – tools, training and networking – to render them ready for investment. The project also provides grants and repayable finance in the form of grants and repayable loans to cover pre-agreed costs.

The project has three main regional Executive Entities (EEs), which will coordinate the regional initiatives and provide oversight:

* IPED in West Africa
* Impact Hub (Hub) Addis Ababa in West Africa
* Tecnópolis in Buenos Aires, Argentina in Latin America

A fourth EE, Climate-KIC International Foundation, will support beneficiaries in the climate change impact assessment, business planning, and provide capacity building to the executing entities. It also has a wider network of European Social Support Organisations (ESOs) including regional investors. Additionally, Climate-KIC will provide capacity building on how to integrate gender equality into the project design and implementation, as well as how to increase diversity in climate entrepreneurship.

GIZ is responsible for the overall management overseeing the project, providing capacity building to the executing entities running the pre- and acceleration programmes, and manage local implementation partners where required primarily in the context of the West African pre-acceleration programme.

The following section details the structure of the Gender Assessment:

Chapter 1 details assessment background. In particular, the chapter provides the overview of the study methodology, the gender-related barriers and tools used in the Gender Assessment (GA). It also enumerates the stakeholders involved.

Chapter 2 presents the case for gender equality, including details on the nexus between gender and climate change.

Chapter 3 provides the progress and challenges of gender equality in Latin America and West Africa. The chapter highlights the existing gender-related barriers and tools used to address typical gender-related risks that are often the underlying drivers of inequalities. Second, the chapter goes a step further and provides an overview of key barriers faced by women entrepreneurs in the region.

Chapter 4 undertakes a deep dive and provides extensive details on gender and climate innovation in Latin America and West Africa. The chapter also highlights the existing gender-related barriers and tools used to address typical gender-related risks that are often the underlying drivers of inequalities. Second, the chapter provides details on the existing institutional mechanisms that govern the entrepreneurial ecosystems in the three countries. It also details the status of climate innovation in the education, employment and enterprise sectors (both formal and informal) as well as the venture capital (ventures) and supply incubators, pre-accelerators, accelerators and venture capital sides of climate innovation.

1 EE = Executive Entity; 2 EEE = Executive Entities.


---
**Page 214**
---

Chapter 5 provides an overview of the existing gender-related capacities of the Executing Entities (EEEs).

Chapter 6 provides details of projects that could provide opportunities for learning and leveraging, particularly in terms of gender equality.

Chapter 7 draws the general conclusions of the assessment.

Chapter 8 provides recommendations for the project and its key stakeholders. These recommendations form the basis for the development of the Gender Action Plan (GAP).

### 1.1 OBJECTIVE OF THE GENDER ASSESSMENT

Gender is a cross-cutting issue that needs to be considered in the project. This is because climate change interventions are more likely to succeed when they consider gender and are more likely to achieve their objectives when gender equality and women's empowerment are taken into account.

The Gender Assessment (GA) provides guidance on the design and implementation of projects (see Tentative Table 1).

The GA has been conducted to ensure: a gender-sensitive approach and implementation of the project, and to meet the standard requirements of the Global Climate Fund (GCF) and Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) gender mainstreaming.

The specific objectives of the GA are to:

- Provide an assessment of the gender dynamics in the context of climate change adaptation projects implemented by GIZ in the GEF/GEF SGP (GGP) host countries of Côte d'Ivoire, Senegal and Morocco.
- Assess gender equality status in key sectors as well as analyzing the gender equity issues for climate entrepreneurs and enterprises.
- Provide recommendations on how to make the project more gender-sensitive.
- Provide recommendations on how to make the project design and implementation more gender-sensitive.

The specific objectives of the project (EEE) will provide recommendations on how to make the CATALY1T project design and implementation more gender-sensitive. The GA provides guidance for the project.

The GA is complemented by a Gender Action Plan (GAP) in order to make the CATALY1T project more gender-responsive.

### 1.2 METHODOLOGY

The implementation of the GA and the development of the Gender Action Plan (GAP) are based on the following approaches and stages:

A. Conducting a literature review, development of gender baseline topics and a concept paper which formed the basis for the GAP and GA. During this phase, a list of stakeholders to be consulted as well as a guiding list of questions for the stakeholder's consultation meetings and interviews were developed.

B. The consultant conducted stakeholder consultations with the key stakeholders identified by GIZ. These stakeholders include all the EEEs, namely GIZ, Climate Change Foundation, Tecnologia de Montenegro, Lake Alliance, Agence Française d'Action Économique & Partenaires (AFD), International IDEA and Impact Hub Abidjan for West Africa. In addition, interviews with selected entrepreneurs took place in Côte d'Ivoire, Senegal and Morocco. The data were collected through semi-structured interviews through online key informant interviews (KII). Analysis of secondary data and extensive literature review was also conducted to provide a comprehensive understanding of the gender dynamics.

The table in Annex 1 provides a summary of the stakeholders consulted and the tools used.

# 2. GENDER EQUALITY, CLIMATE CHANGE AND CLIMATE INNOVATION

## 2.1. WHY DOES GENDER EQUALITY MATTER FOR CLIMATE CHANGE?

Both governments and international organizations have recognized that gender equality and women's empowerment is a central feature of the 2030 development agenda (SDG) on gender equality and women's empowerment, as well as achieving many other SDGs.

At the core of these international agreements lies a shared understanding that women should enjoy the same social, political and economic rights as men. This includes the right to education, access to health services, employment participation in decision-making, and equal representation in government and women’s rights is a prerequisite for the achievement of the SDGs (see Textbox 2).

Gender equality is, at the same time, an opportunity for business development. Unlocking the full potential of women and girls is crucial in the quest for inclusive, sustainable growth and development. The evidence shows that women are more likely to invest their earnings locally and global development given that women often make the best investments and their economic empowerment has a positive impact on household income. Women reinvest 90% of their earnings back into their families and communities, compared to 30% for men. Besides, women’s entrepreneurship is also an opportunity to increase total global gross domestic product (GDP) by US $12 trillion (£11.4 trillion). The economic power – covering women in business, women in the value chain


---
**Page 216**
---

and women in the community – therefore carries enormous potential and will be a key driver of future growth.

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) sees mainstreaming of gender perspectives as an essential de- cision-making element for developing countries to ensure that their projects are not only an integral element of its programming architecture. Its commitment to gender equality concerns on gender- responsive climate change adaptation and mitigation is reflected in the GCF’s Gender Mainstreaming Instrument states that gender equity considerations should be mainstreamed into the entire project cycle to enhance the efficacy of climate change mitigation and adaptation interven- tions.

UN Women sees the harnessing of gender equality and women's rights concerns as one of the most important ways for countries to ensure that their development efforts are sustainable, in order to avoid or mitigate trade-offs between climate and sustainable development action, and instead lead to substantial development benefits. 

The World Bank (2012) argues that it makes the case that gender equality is intrinsically im- portant to development, as well as being smart economics. The mainstreaming of gender trans- formation in all aspects of climate change adaptation and mitigation policy and practice needs to be done. According to the World Bank – in order to know 'why' and 'how' to do more on gender equality, it is important following three key aspects related to gender and climate change: need to be taken into account.

1. Women, low-income populations, and other disadvantaged groups are disproportionately affected by climate change. The fact that women's access to land, education, health care rights and socio-economic status are not equal to those of men; and where they have less legal protection and fewer resources, makes them more vulnerable when a disaster occurs is used. Women’s rights, socio-economic status and voice can be all strengthened through gender-sensitive and climate-smart development approaches (WBG). This will allow for better adaptation strategies focused on women's and girls' vul- nerability and their role as victims towards emphasizing agency this encourages a more inclusive approach to climate change adaptation and mitigation. Empowering women, increasing gender equality and women’s leadership can jump-start climate action and climate-smart solutions.

2. Empowerment of women is an important ingredient in building climate resilience. There are countries examples where empowering women to exercise leadership within their communities has led to better forest management practices in Indonesia, improved water resources in Bangladesh, Indonesia and Nicaragua; to better forest governance in India and Nepal; and to more effective disaster risk reduction strategies in the Philippines. The evidence at the country level that improving gender equality contributes to policy choices that lead to better climate change adaptation and mitigation outcomes is growing. Women’s voice and women within their communities, society at large, and at the political level, or through civil society organizations can make a difference.

3. Low-emission development pathways can be more effective and more equitable where they are designed using a gender-informed approach. Billions of women around the world are responsible for cooking in their homes, which is one of the largest sources released into the atmosphere. This influence differs from that of men; owing to women's socially defined roles as caregivers and cooks. Women’s role in climate change mitigation e.g., from domestic cooking), as farmers (influencing soil carbon emissions) and con- suming (purchase of goods and services throughout the entire life cycle of production, consumption and waste disposal); see Chapter 4 below for sector specific

# Gender and Climate Innovation

In order to drive the rapid economic transformation required to reach net zero emissions for meeting climate goals, countries must address the energy sector, which accounts for about two-thirds of global energy use; must adopt climate mitigation strategies. While huge technical innovation is needed in this sector, it will not be sufficient without a significant increase in public and private participation of citizens, including women. Women’s unique experience, knowledge and skills set them apart as key actors in the transition to low-carbon economies. This was highlighted by the organizations of the 2XClimate Finance Task Force—in its recently launched Toolkit² Further more, women are disproportionately affected by climate change, and their role is critical for emission reductions and energy transition. Given the importance of clean energy to climate change mitigation, adaptation, resilience and just transition, together with the under-representation of women in decision-making positions, it is clear that gender equality and climate action and gender impact are undeniable.

These challenges are particularly acute in Europe but also recognized and stated by one of the stakeholders consulted in Colombia²: “women have different perceptions of the significance of climate change, and behave differently as a result. Women appear to be more likely to use or decide on energy-efficient appliances, such as LED light bulbs, and to buy organic food, locally produced foods, recycling household waste and making decisions about the purchase of household goods and services.” This is also reflected in the fact that women are more likely than men to develop more mission-driven businesses, such as green ventures or inclusive business models. Women’s role in climate change mitigation is therefore critical. However, there is a need to take gender into account in the design of measures to reduce carbon intensity across sectors.

A World Bank study concluded that interventions in the energy sector could have significant gen- der co-benefits when interventions are carefully designed and targeted based on a context-spe- cific analysis of women’s needs. In the area of urban transport, for example, in the context of development and in the development of public transport systems, there is growing evidence that gender-sensitive approaches can lead to more sustainable outcomes with respect to both environ- mental co-benefits, regardless of whether or not they also seek to lower net carbon emissions, as well as social co-benefits. This is because women are disproportionately affected by multiple climate and gender challenges in tandem, across regions and values.

The agriculture sector is a major contributor to climate change effects, producing an estimated 18-30% of global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. It also plays a key role in land use and ecological degradation, directly reducing the resilience and future productivity of lands and eco- systems. However, women play a critical role in agriculture and are often responsible for climate mitigation efforts in other sectors. A gender lens should be a central component in food system


---
**Page 218**
---

transformation. Women have important roles across agricultural value chains – as entrepreneurs, producers, processors, distributors and consumers. Yet significant gender gaps across these value chains limit the ability of women to innovate, implement and lead climate solutions in agri-cultural systems. Addressing these gaps is critical for building resilience and adaptation, contributing to a just transition by building an inclusive and resilient global food and agriculture sector.

# The Status of Gender Equality at Regional Levels: Progress and Challenges

## 3.1. STATUS OF REGIONAL GENDER EQUALITY

At the international level, West African and Latin American countries have ratified most international conventions and regional instruments, including the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and its Optional Protocol. The countries have also committed to the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (Beijing+5), the Dakar Framework for Action (DFPA), the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights (ACHPR), the Maputo Protocol, the African Union’s Agenda 2063, the International Conference on Population and Development (ICPD, 1994), Beijing+5 (2000), the African Women's Decade Programme 2010-2020, the 1994 Inter-African Conference on Women and Children, the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC), the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (Convention of Belém do Pará) and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). Additionally, some countries in West Africa, for example Côte d'Ivoire, are signatories to the Dakar Framework for Action. For instance, Côte d’Ivoire was the first signatory. They are also State Parties to the Sulema Declaration on Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment in Africa, which was adopted at the 2014 Summit of African Heads of States and Government in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia, July 2014, the so-called Maputo Protocol. The implementation of all these international conventions, protocols, and laws has been a big challenge to the realization of gender equality in the three regions under our purview.

The Latin American countries have made significant progress in gender equality – e.g., progress in increasing women’s representation at the highest levels of government and in political parties – compared to West Africa. These changes are analysed in the sections below and at the regional level. # Latin America

According to the United Nations, over the past thirty years, women participating in the Latin Amer-ica region’s workforce increased by 11%. A study undertaken by the International Labour Organ-isation (ILO) found that in 2018, 54% of women aged between 15 and 64 were employed or looking for work. In the region are working, with Peru taking the lead at 68%, and among the lowest, Costa Rica at 42%. The ILO study also found that women in Latin America are more likely to be self-employed (which, in this case, refers to schooling beyond high-school level) and working, and 80% in Ven-ecuela, with similar correlations in neighbouring countries. Apart from improvement in educa-tion, the steady reduction in discriminatory regulations against women has also contributed to this increase. However, a recent study by the World Bank shows that there is still a substantial wage gap, with women earning on average 20% less than men of the same age and economic status, and unpaid labour is dis-proportionately assigned to women, resulting in an uneven balance of workload and compensation.

Entrepreneurship in Latin America is also a dynamic sector. Women are increasingly becoming entrepre-neurs at impressive rates. According to a 2018 study from Techstars, 57% of the entrepreneurs who participated in accelerator programmes were female-founded. This is, in part, due to countries such as Brazil and Colombia having increased access to venture support initiatives*. In 2019, Latin America was ranked third globally for women’s entrepreneurship, after Europe and North America to be the second-most enterprising environ-ment in the world. The 2020 Statista report identifies Latin America as the region with the highest female entrepre-neurship rates in the world; specifically, over 30% of working age women are entrepreneurs.

Latin America is also a region that is highly innovative and characterised by optimism, innovation and growth. The World Bank reports that two-thirds of Latin American countries have been identified as having a high level of technological innova-tion, rather than out of necessity. These opportunities appear most often in countries the OECD has classified as being at an advanced stage of development. Female entrepreneurs have been impacted more than their male counterparts by the COVID-19 pan-demic.

The World Bank’s Index of Women Entrepreneurs*, which measures the success of women entrepreneurs, shows that more than 50% of women in Latin America are negatively affected by the pandemic.

### West Africa

According to the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD), in West Africa, gender disparities persist. Women and girls are discouraged in many areas and do not enjoy the same opportunities as their male counterparts. This is true for almost all public sectors, including education, health care, agriculture, and trade. The OECD also notes that there are gender gaps in the labour market and in the public sphere. Since the mid-2000s, however, almost

Within re- gions, there is also a great deal of diversity in terms of the extent to which women are discriminated against. ### 3.2. GENDER-RELATED RISKS AND BARRIERS IN WEST AFRICA AND LATIN AMERICA

The following section will discuss some of the general gender risks and barriers that exist in both regions. The barriers exacerbate factors of inequality and, in most cases, are the underlying drivers of gender inequalities.

#### 3.2.1. THE RURAL VS. URBAN DIVIDE

The rural-urban divide is a significant risk in the ability for women to become entrepreneurs not based on religion, ethnicity or political association but rather where they live – in rural or urban settings. This is because the settings and situations have an impact on their capacity, opportunities and potential for women; particularly when it comes to becoming business leaders and entrepreneurs.

---
**Page 222**
---

This disparity is based on unequal educational levels, diminished access to finance, and traditional social burdens of household care and childcare (unpaid care work) which often widens the rural urban divide.

For instance in Côte d'Ivoire, rural women represent 67% of the workforce and produce 80-90% of food. Yet, according to the World Bank, 75% of women live under the poverty line. The majority are engaged in subsistence farming or informal sector work, which is often considered a lower category than urban women, who have higher levels of education and the capacity to build scalable businesses, access finance and business-related information.

According to the World Bank, only about 10% of rural households own land; those that do live on rural areas own agricultural land and less than 5% have access to technical assistance. Further, in the agricultural sector, women are more likely to be involved in subsistence farming (62%) compared with men (48%), respectively. Land tenure is a major barrier for women, as women in many countries in Latin America have lower access to land ownership and credit than men.

The environment also poses security and makes it difficult for many women to access credit finance.

The rural urban divide also has a major influence on women’s perceptions and attitudes. Generally speaking, women living in the city are more likely to be employed outside of the home compared with when it comes to their belief in their own entrepreneurial abilities. Despite the seemingly impossible task of balancing work and family life, women in cities are more likely to have access to childcare, making a more accepting society. Urban women are also more likely to be literate and have access to higher education, which paves their way for more work and entrepreneurial opportunities – which is not always the case with rural women who may lack these opportunities.

Women living in urban areas are more likely to have access to information on family planning rights and accept roles of caring for children, a husband and extended family. The metropolitan nature of the cities also means that women can be more involved in paid work outside their homes compared with roles in society as compared to those of the rural or more traditional areas of the country. How- ever, this is not always the case; some women are still expected to take care of household chores and neighbourhoods could even be more disadvantaged than rural women, given their lack of access to basic services.

In addition, access to essential services and actors is often limited to the capital cities. In Côte d'Ivoire, for example, international actors are almost entirely based in Abidjan, which is well ser- viced by infrastructure and has a large population. This means that many rural areas and peri-urban and rural areas are left out of programme implementation. There are a few exceptions, such as the health sector where some NGOs have established satellite clinics in rural areas outside the capital city of Côte d'Ivoire, which has an incubator, and some organizations have occasional satellite pro- gramming, but this is not a widespread trend.

### 3.2.2. CUSTOMARY AND TRADITIONAL NORMS, PRACTICES AND BELIEFS

In both West Africa and Latin America, child marriage and teenage pregnancies are common practices. In Côte d'Ivoire, for example, the Ministry of Health has estimated that in 2017, based on a 2020 brief - estimates that countries in the West African region will have the highest number of girls married before age 18 (UNICEF, 2019). In Côte d'Ivoire, child marriage is still common. Burkina Faso has 52% of its girls married before the age of 18 years; 10% of whom are even married under the age of five and they turn 15. Niger: 78% of girls are married before the age of 15 (see figure below). The Latin American region has a high prevalence of child marriage, especially among adolescents, with 74 births per 1000 adolescents between 15 and 19 years of age, and a high maternal mortality of 26.4 deaths per 1000 live births annually. Maternal mortality is even higher

in West Africa, at 26 deaths a day, a rate that is four times higher than anywhere else in the world". In addition, child marriage and pregnancies affect girls' education and limit their choices later in life.

Catalysts Consulting's research identifies a number of other cultural factors that hold back women from exploitation of their entrepreneurial potential. In West Africa, traditional beliefs, superstitions and taboos assign value and mysticism to women based on virginity, chastity, menstruation and pregnancy, often with a negative impact. These breed detrimental systems that hinder women's autonomy and limit their access to education, health care and economic opportunities. This is particularly true in religious groups in West Africa. The practice prevents autonomy for women. Polygamy's value system reinforces the idea of female subordination and limits women's ability to make decisions about their children - off economically should they behave in a way disapproved by their husband, such as not finding a job.

Customary laws continue to be applied on a regular basis in many countries in West Africa. These customary laws differ for each region and tribe and are often based on religious, ethnic or traditional beliefs. The following table provides some examples of how the implementation of customary laws over statutory laws affects women's rights: i) access to land; ii) inheritance rights; iii) access to credit; iv) access to land rights; and v) accessibility of the justice system.

In West Africa, women's land rights and access and control have further weakened with the unpredictable weather changes that affect small landholders, who are often women with little or no education. The number of people living in poverty has increased significantly over time, and the impact of climate-related crop changes has significantly increased, with up to 48% reported in Burkina Faso (see Figure 3). In addition, women's access to credit is limited.

In Latin America, significant legal strides have been made in the past decade, and many of the countries have repealed laws identifying the husband as the head of the household, which was


---
**Page 224**
---

seen to limit women’s capacity to administer properties. FAO sees this as a positive opportunity to provide female household members with access to land, but it may also undermine women's bargaining power within the household – and thus their social position, since the land titles are often registered in men's names.

### 3.2.3. SEXUAL AND GENDER-BASED VIOLENCE (SGBV)

Women in West Africa and in Latin America encounter many challenges related to their physical integrity, such as sexual aggression and gender-based violence. FAO sees this as a positive opportunity to provide female household members with access to land, but it may also undermine women's bargaining power within the household – and thus their social position, since the land titles are often registered in men's names.

#### 3.2.3. SEXUAL AND GENDER-BASED VIOLENCE (SGBV)

A case in point is the level of SGBV reported during the COVID-19 pandemic. Although a number of measures have been implemented to combat the spread of the virus, the situation has worsened for women and girls. The prevalence of SGBV in West African countries and not all countries have a specific law in place to address it.

The most common form of SGBV in West Africa is female genital mutilation (FGM). Although laws criminalizing FGM exist in many countries, the practice continues due to cultural and social norms. According to data from UNICEF, Mali and Burkina Faso have rates at above 75% among women aged 15 to 49 years old (UNICEF, 2021). In addition to loss of life, the practice causes lifelong problems that hinder women from achieving their full potential.

# KEY BARRIERS FACED BY FEMALE ENTREPRENEURS IN WEST AFRICA AND LATIN AMERICA

Existing research and evidence from a gender assessment in both regions shows that the following challenges hinder women from becoming both entrepreneurs and successful business leaders.

- **Lack of accessible entrepreneurship education and capacity building:** A lack of literacy combined with limited access to information and communication technology (ICT) is a major barrier to regard. Literacy and lack of education are the most important factor affecting women's entrepreneurial activities in West Africa, according to the World Bank. The 2014 report on the status of Côte d'Ivoire (in 2014) indicates that 59.4% of Ivorian women are not educated, or only completed primary school, and that literacy is more pronounced among older generations. In West Africa, education is often a luxury for girls; however, in Latin America, access to education has one of the highest literacy levels. However, as in West Africa, alternative education such as vocational training and entrepreneurship courses are not always available or accessible to all parts of the population, including women coming from families with high resources, and are often located far away from their homes.

- **Challenging legal frameworks:** In some countries, such as Côte d'Ivoire, Benin and Togo, for example, the division in the law where customary and statutory laws coexist is a major challenge. This can lead to conflicts between women's rights regarding property ownership, use and transfer of land. Though few smallholders are legally registered (land ownership generally requires formal registration), many women have been able to secure their right to land. In Côte d'Ivoire, despite the fact that there is progressive legislation related to women's rights, the reality is that more than 75% of the country continues to operate under customary law. This means that women are often excluded from accessing credit and maintaining the fixed capital often needed to access finance. Though not a common problem, legislative frameworks in some countries also require women to obtain permission from their husbands or the marriage law stipulates that a woman must seek permission from her husband before she can start a business, thereby making it difficult for women to start a business.


---
**Page 226**
---

- Lack of access to information: Given women’s limited access to networks, that no matter the socio-economic or education level, women face an overall lack of access to information related to registering and opening a business, as well as accessing (or preparing for) access to credit. This lack of access to information remains largely unknown beyond capital cities and are thus underutilized or unimpressed.
- Limitations in access to finance: First, the lack of information makes accessing financial markets challenging beyond mobile money systems. The lack of information also limits women’s ability to understand how to use these services effectively. Second, women’s growth potential is restricted when they are denied credit as a result of excessive demands for collateral. Third, assets held in women’s names or under their control (e.g., savings and collateral) limits their access to credit more than men. Fourth, there seems to be an exacerbation of the gender gap in financial inclusion in areas where women have limited business expansion, research and development (R&D), and long-term success - especially for women.
- Narrow perceptions of entrepreneurship: According to Catalysts Consulting*, the local perception of what entrepreneurship is tends to be narrowly limited as a idea of Western businessmen who are self-employed. In contrast with this image of entrepreneurs being employees, the concept of entrepreneurship is reserved for suits and ties, formal offices and has little relevance in the informal sector where women are most active and have observable short-term growth. However, many of these same women engage in work out- side their homes and are involved in small-scale economic activities. This type of organ- ized, small scale and informal economy of buying and selling activities is known as “Gombo.” While Gombo is not limited to women, because traditional West African culture relegated women to the domestic sphere, most entrepreneurial activities conducted by women outside of formalized and salaried jobs are usually considered Gombo. In large cities, women’s entrepreneurship is more likely to be in the informal sector than in the educational system, especially outside of the capital cities.
- In Latin America, 50% of women ages 18–24 live below the poverty line. In Mexico, for example, 47.6% of women aged 15 and older who work are not able to take care of their children properly, while 10.4% consider men and not women should hold decision-making positions in the family. Although women’s participation rates are increasing for women in the region, there remains a steep gender pay gap. The Eco- nomist, “Women in Latin America: A Gender Gap,” June 28, 2019. International Labour Organization (ILO) researches the issue in 2019.* After analyzing the data and figures, the ILO concluded that women are underrepresented as entrepreneurs compared to men in Latin America. In the study, researchers held position, education, age and eco- nomic activity as factors that could explain why women were less likely than men to be recognized as entrepreneurs. The study recognizes resolving these problems is a top priority. Elevating women in the workforce would also be a positive step for economic growth and development.

# 4. FOCUS ON THE EXECUTING ENTITY HOST COUNTRIES OF THE CATALL*5T INITIATIVE

This chapter provides extensive details on gender and climate innovation in the three EE host countries (Côte d'Ivoire, Senegal and Mexico). It also provides details on the existing institutional mechanisms that govern the entrepreneurial ecosystems in the countries and describes the status of women's entrepreneurship in each country. The chapter looks at specific gender-related barriers faced by climate ventures and entrepreneur support organizations, including access to finance, incubators and venture capitalists.

## 4.1. CÔTE D'IVOIRE

### 4.1.1. INSTITUTIONAL MECHANISMS GOVERNING IVORIAN ENTREPRENEURIAL ECOSYSTEMS

Given the use of customary laws outlined in Chapter 3, the overall picture of gender inequality in Côte d'Ivoire is not as clear-cut as it was portrayed by Camille Castagnet, consulting in 2020*, found that although there are efforts towards women's empowerment in the country, they are still far from being fully implemented. The National Strategy for Gender Equality and Entrepreneurship, launched in 2016, has been revised to include a gender equality strategy, as far as gender equality is concerned the country presents an image of "an elephant with feet of clay".

At the national level, the formal framework for gender equality is established by the Constitution of 2016, which enshrines the principles of gender equality between women and men, fights against all forms of discrimination on grounds of sex, marital status, age, and equal opportunities in the labour market, in employment and elected assemblies.

Among the most important action plans and national strategies relating to gender are: the National Strategy for Gender Equality and Entrepreneurship (2016-2025), which was updated in 2018; the National Strategy for Women's Empowerment and Economic Empowerment of Women, which was launched in 2019. The latter is still under discussion and the empowerment of women is in the process being finalized. Furthermore, it is noteworthy that the Ivorian government has been working on domesticating its international gender commitments.

A key objective of the 2016-2020 National Development Plan (NDP) included economic pro-growth policies, putting Côte d'Ivoire into the emerging markets. The country has not yet reached this goal, although there have been significant efforts made through the use of strategies such as the 2019 National Strategy for Women's Empowerment and Economic Empowerment of Women, which aims at large scale, especially for rural areas, through the use of mobile banking, micro-finance institutions.

Entrepreneurship regulations are also becoming a major issue for the informal sector in the country. By 2017, the government had introduced a tax on informal workers, which is a form of tax collection for formal sector companies working with informal actors. The tax applies a rate of 2% to 5%, depending on the type of activity and the number of employees. This is not a major issue but it is a starting point in the ongoing effort to strengthen regulation. Many business representatives in Côte d'Ivoire believe that this is evidence that the government is eager to regulate the informal sector, which could have positive implications for the economy if implemented correctly.

In the coming years and address and regulate the informal economy. This could mean economic disruption as well as a loss of jobs and income for many people who rely on it. It also means a lack of technical capacity and an inability to bear the financial burden necessary to operate as fully formalized businesses.

During the prior stakeholder feedback meeting in West Africa held on 30 November 2021, the workshop participants pointed out the presence of female entrepreneurship networks, platforms and initiatives in Côte d'Ivoire. The Women's Entrepreneurship Development Commission of the General Confederation of Ivorian Employers (CGECI) has a Gender-Women-Entrepreneurship Commission. Other groups of entrepreneurs include the Ivorian Association of Women Entrepreneurs (AIJE), the Ivorian Federation of Women Entrepreneurs, the Women Entrepreneurs of the World Côte d'Ivoire, the Federation of Women Entrepreneurs of Côte d'Ivoire, and the Ivorian Association of Inventors and Entrepreneurs of Côte d'Ivoire. They all promote the collective success of female entrepreneurs in Côte d'Ivoire. The CGECI is a member of the Confederation of Employers of Côte d'Ivoire (CGECI). Despite their low numerical weight, the CGECI has set up an entrepreneurial commission aimed at building the future generation of Ivorian women entrepreneurs and businesswomen through training, mentoring, and networking.

Despite array of efforts in overcoming remaining national mechanisms for the promotion of gender equality, studies show that major gaps exist within the national mechanisms, which can be summarized as follows: a lack of tools and mechanisms for integrating gender into policies, plans, development strategies, and action plans; a lack of data on women entrepreneurs; the absence of aggregated databases and up-to-date harmonized gender indicators in different sectors in order to provide evidence-based policy-making; and a lack of coordination between structures. A comprehensive and integrated coordination framework between the structures in charge of gender promotion and the structures in charge of entrepreneurship development is still missing. In motion – for instance, the percentage of national budget allocated to the MFIE is 0.1%, and it is the same for the Ministry of Women's Affairs. The Ivorian government has not yet adopted copies of gender equality - for example, the fact that gender mainstreaming continues to be treated as a separate issue whose added-value is not well perceived". All these factors have an effect on women entrepreneurs, on both the demand and supply sides of the market.
