### 4.1.2. STATUS OF CLIMATE INNOVATION IN EDUCATION, AGRICULTURE AND ENERGY SECTORS

#### 4.1.2.1. GENDER AND CLIMATE INNOVATION IN EDUCATION

Currently, Côte d'Ivoire ranks a rate of 65% illiteracy for women in the country, and it is particularly high among those who did not complete any education level (90.4%) or those without any education level and 23.5%, for those who stopped at a primary school level. The low literacy levels constrain female entrepreneurship, as well as blocking access to higher-paying jobs. Illiterate women are less likely to be able to read the instructions on products and services, which they often have difficulty assimilating the fundamentals and basics of management". The illiteracy problem not only affects women's ability to manage their businesses but also limits their capacity to create innovative products and stimulate demand for products and services that can favourably compete in the market.

Based on 2018 data from the World Bank, only 7.6% of women and 11.0% of men in Côte d'Ivoire were enrolled in university. These low figures are further exacerbated by the fact that the higher education system is dominated by public universities, which have a weak link to private enterprises. Further, the institutions were not established from the outset with the aim of preparing students for entrepreneurship and innovation. The universities also do not provide opportunities for mainstreaming gender sensitive pedagogy and curricula in their courses. This limits women's access to higher education and their entrepreneurial spirit to explore creativity and build business models (in student associations, etc.).

In the Technical and Vocational Education and Training (TVET) centres, the proportion of men and women is almost equal (48% of women against 52% of men). However, the majority of graduates from these institutions are employed in the public sector or in the informal sector in training courses in industrial sectors. Hence, tertiary sectors are commonly called "female sec- tors" because they are dominated by women and have a low level of innovation. This limits the economic participation of women in non-traditional jobs. For example, in the construction sector, women represent 20% of workers but only 15% of managers. Women also dominate working in the tertiary and informal sectors (80% of hotels and restaurants workers). Such stig- mias can kill innovation in these crucial sectors where women are under-represented.

As a result, there is a need to mainstream gender equality into all TVET centres that work on gender mainstreaming. Actions, such as workshops to reflect on the access of young girls to education and training, are essential for this purpose. The creation of a shop provides spaces for discussion and reflection to analyse and understand the factors that contribute to women's under-representation in these sectors. The appointment of the first female university president from the six public universities symbolises the advancement of women in this Ministry. However, the discussions need to be moved into practical actions that support women's access to education and training as well as their integration within the institution.

The technology-related education – "EdTech" - ecosystem in Côte d'Ivoire still remains nascent and is not focused on climate innovation. So far, none of the EdTech start-ups in the country have raised significant funding from investors. The Ministry of Higher Education, Research and Innovation (MIRECI) has launched the Togo Regional Education and Climate Change Consortiums (TRECC) report in 2018, grants of insufficient size are the main source of funding and there is a general lack of know-how about fundraising by founders. Ac- cording to the TRECC report, the average amount raised per start-up was CFAF 35 million.


---
**Page 232**
---

affected by three factors: first, the investors find it very hard to work with government schools, as the sales cycles are very long; second, EdTech ecosystem support is seen to be lagging in Côte d'Ivoire compared with other countries in sub-Saharan Africa. Third, many ventures are trying to serve a large number of students and therefore have to scale up quickly, which is difficult when resources are too high.

Women entrepreneurs face these challenges just like their male counterparts. In addition, structural issues, low education levels and perceptions limit their ability to get their products in front of the right audience and reach a mature stage. A number of public, private and CSO initiatives have been launched with the aim of supporting women entrepreneurs in Côte d'Ivoire. However, Catalysia Consulting's study found that, despite their high potential to create impactful business ventures, women entrepreneurs (who are often young, single mothers or widows who lack access to formal training), do not have the capacity to provide the individualized business and pre-incubation training required to support them in developing their businesses. This is a major barrier to increasing their participation in more elite incubation programmes. This study also found although some of the initiatives are well structured, they often fail because they are not tailored to women entrepreneurs' specific, elite and niche types of services. Some these activities include: i) EmpowerHer's programme, which aims at providing training for women entrepreneurs on how to scale up their businesses in a larger scale; and ii) Fondation Jeunesse Numérique (FJN), an incubator that focuses on women within the digital economy. However, both initiatives are not tailored to women entrepreneurs from the horizon population that does not fit into this small socio-economic and educational segment.

Until recently, EdTech incubators and accelerators were lacking in the country. This gap has been partially filled by a new initiative launched at Université de Côte d'Ivoire (UCI) called Incubateur UCI: a new institution that, since October 2020, offers innovative training courses in digital skills for young people from all socio-economic backgrounds and educational levels throughout Côte d'Ivoire. It also supports students wishing to access business school education in Europe. The incubator is currently working on the development of an online platform with identified international tests as support of their applications. Given that the University has just started its operations, it will be a few years before we can see the impact of this initiative on APTE and Comité Capital's women empowerment strategy, which is a key aspect of their UIC-funded programme.

### 4.1.2.2. GENDER AND CLIMATE INNOVATION IN AGRICULTURE

A general analysis of the Ivorian context reveals that there is poor consideration of women’s interests in actions to combat climate change itself; in addition, there is insufficient proven experience on the use of gender-sensitive approaches.

In the agriculture sector in Côte d'Ivoire, women represent 90% of the actors in the food crops sub-sector, which employs 85% of the active agricultural population and represents two-thirds of the agricultural labour force. However, despite their significant contribution to production, women are often given that the production is not always valued or counted but, instead, intended mainly for the subsistence market. In addition, women have less access to land than men: in 2015, only one man dominated the sector and women constituted 62% of the unpaid labour force[3]. In addition, only the 40% of women who are involved in agriculture are involved in export crops and high value-added perennial crops. Women, on the other hand, are involved in both perennial and food crops.

In order to support women's empowerment through the Association Villagissime de l'Épargne et de Crédit (AVEC) in addition. In the support with the development of women’s groups benefit from functional financial services, these projects were supported by the National Rural Development Support Agency (ANDADER). ANADER and its partners have developed approaches so that women have access to technological innovations and are trained in good agricultural practices, both in terms of food crops and in export crops like cocoa. Specifically, female field schools are sometimes set up to facilitate learning.

In Côte d'Ivoire, like many other West African countries, the bulk of “green tech” has hitherto been concentrated on large-scale commercial farms. However, there is a growing interest among cycling ventures and the energy sector. In recent years, technological advancement in agriculture has established a foothold in Côte d’Ivoire and is causing significant excitement within the country’s agricultural community.

The technology is envisaged to reduce extensive farm labour and increase productivity while also benefiting women farmers. The technology is not only more efficient but also less expensive than what was needed when introducing such technologies, as they may not be gender-friendly. First, the large-scale commercial farms are now being replaced by smallholder farms, which are more accessible for women farmers. A reduction in labour requirements also reduces the opportunities for female employment and empowers them.

A number of ventures in agriculture further down the value chain (such as processing of agricultural foods), where women have a stronger presence, are emerging in the country. Innovative ventures include those that use biotechnology to produce seeds with improved traits or through accelerators. Recent ventures that benefited from these accelerators and venture capitalists include: cassava (Manihot esculenta) varieties; sweet potato (Ipomoea batatas); sorghum (Sorghum bicolor) (cassava semolina) and pluclat (casava paste) in the Grand-Bassam area south of Côte d’Ivoire. These ventures are also being used to produce seeds with improved traits or through accelerators. **Page 234**

energy sector. The impetus for the policy was threefold: i) men and women in the community typically exhibited distinct patterns of energy needs, levels of access and impacts; ii) significant aspects of the community's energy policy and institutional architecture were gender blind, and iii) an investment opportunity existed to support women entrepreneurs who could be positively contributing to the regional transition to cleaner, more just and sustaina- ble energy.

Based on the government’s projections and IFC estimates, achieving Côte d’Ivoire’s renewable energy target in full can create a US$ 9 billion (€8.53 billion) investment opportunity by 2030. In the electricity sector alone, this represents an estimated US$ 1.7 billion (€1.6 billion), which corresponds to the gender-differentiated needs of women, their households, businesses and commu- nities. This includes increasing access to energy for women in rural areas, as well as investments such as wind farms, as well as increasing opportunities for women’s employment and innovation.

In some cases, this is happening through new ventures being established within the energy sector. For example, Proparco financing Africa’s Renewable Energy Fund (EARF)-funded Bivio Ener- gie in Abidjan, a town in Côte d’Ivoire, is expected to be the biggest agricultural waste-powered plant in West Africa. The project will generate 10 MW of electricity and is estimated to help reduce 450,000 tonnes of palm tree waste that will, instead, be supplied to the power plant. The project is expected to create over 200 jobs for women and men, with women representing 20% as they provide biomass to the power plant. Once operational, it is anticipated that the Bivio Energie plant will generate enough electricity to supply a population of around 150,000 people. Such a project has the capacity to improve gender equality through the jobs created as well as mobilizing women’s participation in the energy sector.

### A. THE CLIMATE VENTURES

Discussions with Impact Hub Abidjan (IHP) and a number of ventures have revealed the follow- ing high-level challenges faced by female-led ventures in Côte d’Ivoire:

#### 4.1.3 KEY BARRIERS AND CHALLENGES FOR CLIMATE VENTURES AND ENTREPRENEUR SUPPORT ORGANIZATIONS (ESOs)

**A. THE CLIMATE VENTURES**

Access to Finance: Ventures in Côte d’Ivoire often face extremely high barriers to source initial seed funding. Generally, access to bank financing is scarce, the legal and fiscal frame- work is not conducive to women entrepreneurs, and there are few financial service providers. In addition, the cost of credit is excessive (12–15% per annum) for long-term investments and the supply of microfinance institutions is limited.

These initial high capital costs are often out of reach for many women and have overall effects on women’s ability to start or expand their businesses. Specifically, female entrepreneurship access to finance is further hampered by:


---
**Page 235**
---

(i) The lack of a regulatory mechanism for ensuring gender equality at the financial institution level. This is because Côte d'Ivoire currently does not have any law that prohibits gender-based discrimination for accessing credit facilities.

Women interviewed during the gender assessment report that female entrepreneurs face difficulties in accessing credit from banks. Some borrowers (see Testimony 3) and in single women, female entrepreneurs who are married or have children, they were denied loans by their bank. The World Bank's Africa Gender Innovation Lab found that unconscious bias against female entrepreneurs results in higher rejection rates for female borrowers than male borrowers and requires higher levels of collateral required. Although some banks have a policy to ensure that loan applicants is driven primarily on male employees, others find that bias is rooted in institutional norms and is common among both women and male staff.

(ii) Women entrepreneurs interviewed reported that when applying for a loan, they are asked about their marital status because the bank claims the business is less profitable if it is run by single women. If they were married with children, they were denied loans (see Testimony 3).

Testimony 5: "Women entrepreneurs interviewed reported that when applying for a loan, that if they were un-married or if they were married without children and if they were married with children, they were denied loans."

4. Female entrepreneurs face more technical challenges than their male counterparts. Most vent- lures are technology-driven. Compared with other sectors, such as information and communications technology, where the idea of ven-ture is more tech-savvy and therefore cannot develop technology-based products and services. This stops them from pursuing tech-related ventures that are crucial for driving innovation in the renewable energy sector. Impact Hub Abidjan shared some of the key gender challenges they face in its pre-acceleration programmes.

5. Female-led ventures find it harder to acquire talent and quickly constitute a team. This is where core competencies in renewable energy are required, and female entrepreneurs lack these skills. In addition, female-led ventures are often careful and hire male team members only after considering the impact the poten-tial staff member will have on the company's growth trajectory. Impact Hub Abidjan, for example, has similar level of education and background more readily came up with ideas and often pushed for energy innovation.

6. Female entrepreneurs in non-traditional sectors such as renewable energy and construction have difficulty finding cli- ents. Even Although it is a hearten-ing statistic that 80% or more of female-led ventures have women as team members, including the founder, they are still not able to implement their abilities. On the other hand, in female-led ventures with 80% or more male mem-bers, women are often seen as the administrative staff and not as a key player in the company's capacity and often assuming the leadership roles themselves. In the latter group, Impact Hub Abidjan noted that this has a negative impact on the group's productivity. While this cannot be generalized across all mixed ventures.

7. Female entrepreneurs need to be paid close atten-tion when working with mixed groups.

So in most cases, women are not able to get the contracts and sometimes I have tried to advertise my company around the town especially at the market place where there are many men. All with female venture in Côte d'Ivoire

ENTREPRENEUR SUPPORT ORGANIZATIONS (ESOs): PRE-ACCELERATORS AND VCs

Pre-accelerators and accelerators In Côte d’Ivoire, there is a gap between the strong entrepreneurial ecosystem, which constrains the development of pre-accelerator and accelerator models for climate innovation. Compared with other sectors, such as information and communications technology, where the idea of ven-ture is more tech-savvy and therefore cannot develop technology-based products and services, climate technologies, given that such technologies typically require longer pre-acceleration/accel- eration programmes.

Approximately 20% of the ventures Impact Hub Abidjan is supporting are engaged with climate-related business models in the sectors of renewable energy (solar power), energy efficient con- struction, and sustainable agriculture. Impact Hub Abidjan shared some of the key gender chal-lenges they face in its pre-acceleration programmes.


---
**Page 237**
---

1. **Difficulties in recruiting women ventures into its programmes:** Although this has been resolved with time, it took a long time before Impact Hub Abidjan could find the right channels to attract female entrepreneurs into its pre-acceleration programmes. With time, targeting specific events and talks that specifically target women and building networks has helped ease this problem.

2. **Resource constraints:** Women entrepreneurs have additional needs that must be factored into the design of pre-acceleration programmes. These are broadly categorised as follows:

   i. Women entrepreneurs need additional soft skills in the pre-acceleration modules, as well as more time to balance their work and family responsibilities. This is especially a concern for women who are heads of households. It comes hard for women to attend given their need to balance the training time with other commitments.

      * Impact Hub Abidjan has tried to address this by providing an additional session a week for female entrepreneurs only, which stretches the training time and the cost per participant. This is not ideal as it increases the cost of the programme but is specifically designed to help women voice their own challenges arising from unpaid work, family responsibilities or other gender-based constraints.

      * In addition, in the second pre-acceleration programme, Impact Hub Abidjan provided an additional session where women meet role models, share technical and social experiences and tips.

   ii. Given that women entrepreneurs are more likely to be new entrepreneurs they need more support and accompaniment time to develop and test ideas compared to most male entrepreneurs in general. Women entrepreneurs also tend to have a lot of family responsibilities and often have a hard time sharing their business ideas before they were ready for market. During the pre-acceleration programme, women entrepreneurs are given the opportunity to share their ideas, female entrepreneurs needed nice as long (a month). In addition, as stated at the ventures section above, many women have emotional attachment to ideas that they have developed and need more time to let go of them.

      * Women entrepreneurs have also been supported by Impact Hub with market research and the available information clearly shows that women are more likely to be open to feedback and are more willing to iterate more (7) ideas and are more willing to quickly try something else.

Both issues have been addressed in the third pre-acceleration programme. As a result of the thrust, Impact Hub Abidjan increases the financial resources allocated for trainings by one-third to take care of the additional time and extra training modules required. Impact Hub Abidjan also provides women entrepreneurs networking opportunities through its alumni network, which is reviewed every three months and pairing women entrepreneurs with role models (including 100 women mainly in business and leadership) and mentors in Côte d’Ivoire.

3. **High drop-out rates:** Even when the above efforts are made, women entrepreneurs still immediately dropped out of Impact Hub pre-acceleration programme. This is because they left to start their own businesses or to take up a mentorship-related work after the course. For example, 10 women who were initially targeted in Impact Hub Abidjan’s second pre-acceleration programme are no longer actively engaged in entrepreneurial activities. It con-


---
**Page 238**
---

Instr., over 70% of the female entrepreneurs are still actively engaged in businesses. A discussion with a female entrepreneur revealed that some of women who go through the programmes continue to expect support and handholding after the pre-acceleration programme ends.

At the acceleration stage, IPED and Impact Hub Abidjan shared the following challenges:

- **1. Difficulties in finding mature female-led ventures for acceleration**: Women ventures often operate at a lower level of maturity than their male counterparts and are usually engaged in low-value-added activities, especially in the agri-business field. Shifting from these activities to more complex business models is challenging.
  
  To address this challenge, IPED had to devise strategies to attract female ventures into its acceleration programme. The target was to select a portfolio of high-performing ventures with an investment potential between €50,000 and €300,000. However, attracting mature female-led ventures remains a challenge.

- **2. Inadequate financial models**: Female entrepreneurs often lack the confidence to describe their business cases in detail or to present them effectively during pitching sessions. This is partly due to the time they spend on day-to-day operations and partly because of the stereotype that women are less capable than men, especially when it comes to financial matters.

  To deal with such issues, IPED has adopted a pre-application process for female entrepreneurs. The latter is not initially easy as positions are often occupied by men who have more experience in business development and finance. This opportunity to support the venture to restructure and increase women leadership at the venture management level is crucial.

- **3. Stereotypical gender roles become an obstacle to maintaining gender equality among the ventures**: Women entrepreneurs sometimes face challenges related to gender equality in the management and operation of the ventures they run, especially when it comes to financial matters or men given supervisory roles that women can easily win all the allegation that "women are less qualified to be supervisors and that they are not as good at doing the same job (e.g. packaging products because men are seen to be strong and can carry heavy loads)". To deal with such issues, IPED has adopted a pre-application process for female entrepreneurs. The latter is not initially easy as positions are often occupied by men who have more experience in business development and finance.

  Female entrepreneurs' lack of ability to prepare and present adequate business cases and well-structured financial models. In the application process for acceleration programmes, IPED has put in place a pre-application process that requires female entrepreneurs to submit their proposals with those of their male counterparts. To deal with these challenges, IPED has adopted a pre-active approach where female entrepreneurs are supported by mentors and enabling them to structure the proposals more effectively.

Venture Capital (VC) firms in Côte d'Ivoire and Senegal

The following information is extracted from the literature review and discussion with the VC firms. From the discussions, most of the challenges at the VC levels for Côte d’Ivoire and Senegal are similar and, as such, are combined under this chapter instead of repeating the same information again for Senegal and Côte d'Ivoire.

According to the UNFCCC2016 Climate Technology Incubators and Accelerators. Accessible at https://unfccc.int/sites/ndp/files/2016/03/UNFCCC_2016_Clima te_Technology_Incubators_and_Accelerators.pdf


---
**Page 239**
---

According to VCA41, the VC ecosystem in West Africa is characterised by a lack of early-stage finance and fractured ecosystems as a result of limited investor interest; and a lack of local mentor-driven capital (also see Testimony J). For female-led businesses, the situation is even more dire. Given that women-led businesses are generally smaller than their male counterparts – generating, on average, less than $150K in revenue annually – they operate in an informal level and often operate without the necessary infrastructure to attract investors. They are ineligible for legal benefits and financing opportunities. As a result, about 44% of women entrepreneurs in West Africa do not have access to any form of finance from banks, microfinance, and other institutions23. Access to financing and accurate market information therefore continue to limit women-led businesses’ opportunities for substantial growth.

According to Africa The Big Deal, in 2021 start-ups in Africa raised more than 2.5 times the amount of funding as female-only founded teams. In fact, only 4% of all funding going exclusively across the continent, were with a female founder or a founding team made up exclusively of women. Similarly, only 3% of all funds going to male-founded teams were exclusively male. It is evident that female single founders and female-only founding teams raised only 0.7% (less than 1%) of all the funding raised by start-ups in Africa in 2021. Male single founders and male-only founding teams raised 38.5% of all funds, while male-and-female founded teams raised for every 1 ($69) raised by female-only founding teams, male-only founding teams raised € 28.8 (p=0.047).

A study by Harvard, MIT and Wharton School24 showed investor discrimination at the level of pitching. According to the study, for every 100 pitches made with a male voice, there were 38% more female voices. Two-thirds of investors picked the male voice. This bias could be a product of the fact that 86% of decision-makers in venture capital firms are men25. According to Catalysts Con-sulting, West Africa is estimated that only 5.1% of major VC companies have women on their boards.

# Testimony 10

> "Most small businesses on the continent are not built for a 'Silicon Valley industry' because investors *not* more interested in investing in founders who speak their language and have the same risk appetite—unfortunately, for many local companies, this is a problem. This has been detrimental to their capital-raising process."

## Daily Views from the Founders Factory Africa.

According to the 2021 Venture Capital Journal, there is also an expectation of a rapid cycle investment in Africa. However, most African startups are not yet established enterprises since they face not only financial challenges but also lack management experience. The same journal concludes that venture capital in Africa needs to be more risk-taking and need to be better managed.

With regard to Côte d'Ivoire and Senegal, the two countries have emerging and dynamic entrepreneurial ecosystems. In Côte d'Ivoire, there are several factors contributing to a significant number of ventures at scale. However, in recent years Côte d'Ivoire has developed an ecosystem that is more focused on the private sector (driven by many factors such as: national wealth being reserved among government officials and the upper class, who do not invest in private sector development; the general lack of investment culture in the country; poor financial management practices and a general lack of guidance and experience for investors, as well as the perception that the private sector is not a priority). In Senegal, there has been a significant increase in the fast-growing economy due to perceptions of risk based on political volatility and outbreaks of violence.

Senegal, on the other hand, has the most mature start-up ecosystem in Francophone Africa, and serves as a hub for the emerging regional venture capital industry. The country has significant concentrations of entrepreneurs who are focused on the private sector. According to VCA, Senegal's growth has been driven by: (i) a strong effort from the private sector to support entrepreneurship; (ii) the presence of several accelerators, incubators and innovation programmes; (iii) funds available for skill development, enterprise creation and business expansion; (iv) access to finance (local and international) in innovation funding; and (v) a growing community of successful investors and entrepreneurs with growing track record.


---
**Page 241**
---

The following is a summary of the gender-related challenges VCs face, gathered from Kits with Impact Hub and IPEd as well as from an extensive literature review:

1. **Difficulties in finding mature female entrepreneurs in climate innovation that have reached commercialization stage**: The majority of funding for women entrepreneurs in climate innovation are concentrated in the early, pre-acceleration stages, where less funding is required. This results in a bottleneck at the commercialization stage, where the required amount of funding is higher.
2. **Female entrepreneurs face challenges in accessing finance and capital**: A lot of women innovators are forced to go up before reaching this stage; i.e., many female entrepreneurs are not able to reach the commercialization stage due to lack of funding. The World Bank impact evaluations[1] finds that female entrepreneurs in Africa are less likely than male counterparts to have access to finance and capital, which is a major barrier for their business.
3. **Female entrepreneurs face challenges in accessing finance and capital**: The World Bank review also finds that female entrepreneurs “do not have the same access as men to financial services or credit facilities, which can be a significant constraint on the success of their business”[2]. Given the cultural constraints that women face, female entrepreneurs need a supportive environment with high potential for venture growth.

### 2. Most women entrepreneurs are attracted to smaller funding packages

Re-leased data from the World Bank’s Impact Evaluation[3] and investors find that women often apply for smaller loans than their male counterparts, which is not always profitable (see Tendency 11). For example, in the early-stage funding stage, there is a 50:50 ratio of women to men applying for loans. However, with their efforts to reach out to business associations and financial institutions for advice to support women, the larger funds only apply for 20% of female applicants.

This might indicate that women business owners lack self-confidence in their ability to manage a larger amount than their male counterparts and are in need of more guidance and building support to expand their businesses. In addition, it is often noted that the companies pre-selected with early-stage funding tend to be small and have no plans for expansion or to sign business plans to attract additional outside investment or quality for bank loans.

Some women entrepreneurs may also choose not to apply for larger amounts due to a specific approach, by enlisting businesses they choose to back in long-term capacity building programming in their specific field, often sourcing industry experts from outside the country to help support the business’s fast-paced growth and adaptation.

3. Women entrepreneurs are less viable in networking events and conferences where investors meet and network with potential investors. Women are also less likely to network because of social stigma of going out alone to an event, for safety reasons (especially in rural areas), and the expectation of taking care of family and household responsibilities. ### 4.2. SENEGAL

#### 4.2.1 INSTITUTIONAL MECHANISMS GOVERNING SENESEGALESE ENTREPRENEURIAL ECOSYSTEMS

Senegalese gender relations are shaped by socio-cultural norms and customs that establish hierarchical relationships between men and women, with men in dominant positions. Although the degree and dynamics of these relationships vary by urban/rural residence and ethnic group and may be influenced by religion, women generally occupy lower status than men. Women (unpaid) household responsibilities such as housework, child rearing and subsistence agricultural labour are expected to be primary decision-makers, earn income through formal employment or informal entrepreneurship, and provide care for children and elderly family members and officials. Ongoing gender issues, such as forced child marriage, a discriminatory family code (code de famille), and the lack of access to education and health services, contribute to the perpetuation of sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV), including female genital mutilation (FGM); limit Senegalese women’s ability to participate in economic activities.

The Government of Senegal has made significant progress in creating a gender-sensitive environment through the adoption of the Party Law, The Standard Operating Procedures on GBV; a National Action Plan for Women and Girls (2015-2020) and the implementation of the new National Strategy for Gender Equality and Equity. These measures have been developed with the participation of civil society organizations and are aligned with the MDGs and ChildRights.

These laws, policies and plans form the basis for a strong legal and policy framework for women’s rights and gender equality in every sector and sphere, from the household to community to local and national levels.

Legal guarantees and political representation. The 2001 Constitution and current legal framework establishes substantial equal rights for women and men. It reaffirms the principle of non-discrimination on grounds of sex, race or ethnic origin, religion or sex. All development strategies must take into account a better balance between men and women in all spheres of life. The constitutional provisions (Article 24: gender equality) and 15 equal access to land) of the constitution provide a key framework for gender equality.

Senegal has also endorsed the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW, 1985), the Maputo Protocol to the African Charter on Human Rights and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (2005). Additional key measures, while not without gaps, also provide key protections for women and girls. These include the National Action Plan against Domestic Violence (2005); the HIV Act (2010), the Gender Equality Act (2010) and the Tax Equality Act (2008).

* * *

**GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ANALYSIS REPORT 31**


---
**Page 243**
---

There is greater openness among political leaders, ministers, and parliamentarians, with increased willingness to women’s political participation and to ensuring gender mainstreaming in public policies.

There has been a significant strengthening promotion of equal rights in agriculture, fisheries, livestock, health, education, employment and social protections...

The numbers of women in occupations traditionally reserved for men, such as mechanical and electrical engineering, have also increased. For example, in 2014, there were about 590 students enrolled in the Programme d’Appui au Développement et à l’intégration de l’équipementier industriel (PADIE) at the National Institute of Technology (INAT), which covers areas such as metalwork, 8% of cooling and refrigeration, and 9% of auto mechanics etc.

The Plan for an Emerging Senegal (PES), adopted in 2014, is Senegal’s new development strategy. The PES has three main pillars: (i) structural transformation of the economy and growth; (ii) human capital, social protection and sustainable development; and (iii) governance and institutional reforms. The National Strategy for Gender Equality and Equity (SNEG 2005-2015), which was developed in 2004 as part of the PES, is a key component of the PES. It aims to “contribute to making Senegal an emerging country in 2035 with a society without discrimination and where women have the same opportunities to participate in its development and enjoy the benefits of its growth.”

In addition, the National Party Observatory, which was established by Decree No 2011-819 of 7 March 2011, monitors and evaluates the implementation of the law on equality between men and women in public policy. The National Party Observatory: (i) undertakes annual gender audits; (ii) collects data on gender equality in public policies; (iii) produces disaggregated data on gender, best practices, constraints and gaps linked to gender; and (iv) makes proposals to accelerate progress towards gender equality in Senegal.

In December 2014, the Government of Senegal launched the National Innovation Fund for Women (FICAW) with a budget of US$50 million or €47,405,000. The fund is managed by the Delegation* Commission for Rapid Enterprise Development (DCERD). The FICAW is a new fund in the Senegalese financial sector. It is an innovative fund for young entrepreneurs in Senegal, as well as across West Africa and other sectors. The principal aim of the fund is to encourage entrepreneurship among Senegalese, targeting women aged 18-40 years old and men above upper age limit.

Under this model, the model for entrepreneurs: financing is structured as follows: (i) Small Financing which focuses on “smaller” projects/businesses; (ii) Incubation funding, for incubation, enterprises with a turnover of less than 5 million CFA francs (€8000); and (iii) Venture capital fund that offers capital in exchange for equity in young companies that have been validated by external CAF partners; and iv) Low-interest loans: at 4% interest rate (with a grace period of six months), the FICAW will finance projects in various sectors such as industries, livestock raising, food processing, agriculture production, digital/ICT, tourism etc.).

In 2015, Senegal’s National Assembly adopted Act No. 38/2015 on entrepreneurship to positively impact the national economy by providing a governance framework and contains recommendations for the development of an entrepreneurial ecosystem in Senegal with start-ups. The Act also provides a suitable legal condition for registering new start-ups in the country.

The implementation of the Act was reinforced by the provisions for amending the general tax code, which were adopted on 20 May 2015. These amendments include: (i) exemption from VAT for the first three years and the reduction in registration fees paid for the creation of companies from CFA francs 48,000 to 36,000 (about €8 or £5.2); Though the implementation Act is still under review by the Constitutional Council, it is expected to boost female-led businesses in the country, by providing tax breaks and other


---
**Page 244**
---

benefits to innovative businesses in fields from agriculture to mobile banking. The tax burden and complexity of the laws were initially seen as reasons why many talented businessmen refrained from creating companies. Given that the reforms are new and implementation has been difficult, it is too early to assess their impact on business creation. However, their potential remains to be seen.

Another initiative of the Senegalese government is the initiative to ease the creation and legalisation of start-ups through creation of a dedicated government bureau: Bureau d'appui à la Création d'Entreprise, or Suresna for the Support and Creation of Businesses (BCE). The Bureau permits entrepreneurs to register their business in one day instead of the usual two days or six days only.

Given that many challenges faced by women entrepreneurs described above, it remains to be seen whether women can effectively exploit these new opportunities. However, at the very least, all the above reforms provide essential conditions for building vibrant and robust women- and youth-friendly economies.

Despite these advancements, and the fact that Senegal has ratified the main international and regional instruments on gender equality, many of those provisions are not enforced in practice. The remaining major gaps at the country level include:

* **Persistent discriminatory legislation,** notably in family law that results in lack of women's legal rights to inherit property, including land and other properties. This is exacerbated by the increasing influence of conservative cultural practices, which often result in women losing their right to inherit, as well as equal access to productive agricultural land and financial capital.
* **Persistent prevalence of harmful traditional practices** that perpetuate SGBV, such as early and forced marriage, female genital mutilation (FGM), and dowry. This is exacerbated by the weak legal protection for women and girls, the practitioners exploit the existing gaps in legal aid services.
* **Limited access to education, employment, decision-making positions, health services, land, and property rights**.
* **Lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, and intersex (LGBTI) identifying groups and lower caste Senegalese women are severely restricted spaces**, especially for the LGBTIs who face physical attacks, police harassment, arrest, and fines or prison sentences.
* **Various forms of forced child labour and sex trafficking** are also of significant concern.
* **Low employment rates for women:** according to a World Economic Forum (WEF) report in 2021**, only 35% of women were employed compared with 64.7% of men, corresponding to a gender gap of 62%.

There are also gaps within national institutions that need to be addressed, such as: (i) lack of resources and staff with appropriate skills, knowledge and capacities to integrate gender in plans, policies, procedures and practices across government line ministries; (ii) stereotypes in the government workforce, which results in women being underrepresented at all levels of government without the necessary technical capacity in gender and/or sectoral expertise. In addition, unit staff are not provided with clear job descriptions, scope of work or specific results to achieve. Additionally, no men are employed in these units, thus diluting and stigmatizing the work of the units.

### 4.2.2. STATUS OF CLIMATE INNOVATION IN EDUCATION, AGRICULTURE AND ENERGY SECTORS

#### 4.2.2.1. GENDER AND CLIMATE INNOVATION IN EDUCATION

Senegal is considered to be one of the sub-Saharan African countries that have made the most significant progress in gender parity in education since 1990, when the rest of the continent had an average enrolment of 30% for girls; Senegal had almost gender parity in primary school enrolment, with 77.3% and 86% enrolment for girls and boys, respectively.

However, the high female enrolment rate is not a reflection of true gender parity in education, given that transition and drop-out issues disproportionately affect girls and women. In fact, only about 50% of girls who are enrolled in primary schools actually complete their primary education and only 31% continue to secondary school. The situation has worsened over the past decade: while 48% of girls were still enrolled in primary schools between 2007 and 2011, this figure dropped to 45% by 2016. Similarly, 54% of young mothers dropped out of school between 2011 and 2014, and only 15% resumed their education. Reasons for dropping out include, among others, child marriage, teenage pregnancy, poverty, lack of access to water and sanitation facilities, and social norms.

In Senegal, the female literacy rate is 63%, compared with 78% for males. The gender gap in secondary school enrolment is even more pronounced: only 40% of girls aged between 15 and 19 years are enrolled in secondary schools, while this figure reaches 62% among boys.

In Côte d’Ivoire, a significant number of women and girls are in the technical and vocational education sector, representing an overwhelming majority of students (71%); far smaller numbers are engaged in agriculture. The lack of access to land, combined with social, cultural, economic, lack of information and domestic work burdens, are significant causes of disparities in educational opportunities for women compared to men.

In Senegal, the gender gap is even more pronounced: 54% of students enrolled in technical education are girls, while only 20% of agricultural students are female. The situation is similar for men, against around seven hours for women. Although efforts are being made to remove the barriers that prevent women from accessing and remaining in school, there are still many women mechanics, agricultural technicians or electricians.

The information and communication technology (ICT) sector is the driving force behind Senegal’s plan to become a knowledge-based economy, with a focus on climate-related issues. Senegalese women constitute 45% of ICT specialized baccalaureate students. Female representation in other fields such as engineering, architecture, medicine, pharmacy and natural science, with 26%, 24% and 16%, respectively.

Despite the progress made by women in the field of education and the global status of Senegal, the education system is not necessarily focused on climate innovation and most universities are largely theory-based; they do not teach practical skills. The lack of practical training for students is a major obstacle to their future employment. Women need to play a more prominent role in the climate value ecosystem “not only as producers of human capital but as capacity builders, innovators and entrepreneurs.”

#### 4.2.2.2. GENDER AND CLIMATE INNOVATION IN AGRICULTURE

The agriculture and livestock sector is Senegal’s main economic activity, representing approximately 30% of the country’s GDP and employing about 65% of its population.

Climate change is projected to lead to rising temperatures, increased droughts and decreased rainfall in Senegal, which would have significant impacts on the agriculture sector and food security. According to national data used to prepare the Third National Communication to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the agricultural sector contributes approximately 49% of the country’s total GHG emissions. The Government


---
**Page 246**
---

**GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ANALYSIS REPORT** | **35**

Senegal recognizes the urgent need to implement adaptation and mitigation measures to increase the resilience of its ecosystems and populations to the impacts of climate change and to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by 2030. Nevertheless, the government is struggling with limited resources and capacity to address these challenges in a coherent manner. The lack of nationally determined contribution (NDC) and national adaptation plan (NAP) priorities, particularly for women’s empowerment and gender equality, further hampers progress in key sectors. Particular challenges include: i) lack of coordination of climate actions and poor capacity in intersectoral planning; ii) limited access to information on climate risks and access to adaptation and mitigation measures for actors, especially at the farmer level; iii) weak institutional frameworks for gender equality and women’s empowerment; and iv) adaptation actions.

These challenges affect women more than men, given the underlying socio-economic and structural inequalities faced in Senegal. Rural women represent almost 70% of Senegal's workforce and produce 80% of the country's food. Although gender equality is guaranteed by law, cultural norms and practices continue to limit women’s access to land and resources. For example, only 13% of agricultural land is owned by women in Senegal, which limits their ability to assert themselves and make livelihood decisions independently. In addition, women are often excluded from evictions or forced displacement due to space farming, thus displacing women to areas of lower quality – most rugged and difficult terrain that has and limited access to water.

Given the above context, rural women in Senegal are mostly identified as the farmers while women are considered to provide support on the farm. In addition, just as in Côte d'Ivoire, women's agricultural activities are often associated with low-value crops such as yams, cassava, and peanuts. However, women are slow but steadily constructing new identities and labour market participation.

Unlike other countries in West Africa, Senegal has a strong policy framework on climate change mitigation. The country’s overarching policy framework, the Plan Sénégal Emergent (PSE), explicitly recognizes climate change as one of its key challenges and sets out strategies for adaptation and mitigation through thematic and sectoral policies in the country – e.g., the country's ongoing effort to update various policy frameworks such as youth employment, women's economic empowerment of rural areas, and food security. The PSE also includes gender equality considerations.

In recent years, innovative loan products, such as obligatory agricultural insurance to mitigate the effects of climate change on agriculture through Banque Agricole, have emerged. Other commercial banks are also offering loans for solar-powered irrigation systems, water pumps and domestic biogas systems for some years. There are a number of ongoing initiatives that provide access to information to farmers that the GCP project could tap into. Examples include a small start-up, "Paysdurya, Mekobon," which is offering paid climate-related


---
**Page 247**
---

data and information to end-users in the agriculture and fisheries sector**. In addition, large companies, many of which are French multinational, such as Orange, Free, Société Générale and BNP Paribas, are engaged in Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) programmes that provide access to credit for women farmers. However, despite these efforts, the impact is limited by digital penetration rate in the country, given that not all end-users, especially women, use digital services.

There is a growing use of drones in the agricultural sector to increase productivity in Senegal. While the use of drones is nothing new, the initiatives led by the Sénégal Programme de Lutte contre la Sécheresse (SPLCS) and the Agro-Producteur d'Afrique du Nord (APAN) project certified to use remote-controlled drones for mapping and data interpretation, is encouraging. When combined with other technologies such as mobile phones, tablets or computers, this technology can help to reduce women's workload and enable them to focus on time and energy on increasing their yields and the contribution of women in improving agricultural practices**

The agricultural technology innovation hub “Késsar Agriboutique” was supported by BMZG in 2017. It is located at the Yoff Centre for Agriculture and acts as a focal point for IT-based technological innovation within the Senegalese agribusiness sector. Yesafr’ Agriboutique offers a range of services to smallholder farmers, including access to credit, training on good agricultural practices, as well as space for project collaboration, training and mentorship to network young ICT entrepreneurs. The hub could provide essential lessons for integrating women and youth in the CATALYS’T initiative.

Senegal is also one of the key players behind the Great Green Wall Project. With cooperation from the African Union, the project entails cooperation among eleven countries in the region and aims to halt desertification by planting 10 billion trees across the continent. The Senegalese National Strategy for Green Jobs was created in November 2015 and covers the period 2015-2030. It includes a number of sectors, including agriculture, livestock, fisheries, forestry, mining as well as: agriculture, forestry, fishing renewable energies, resource-intensive processing industries, recycling and recovery of waste, and construction using sustainable materials.

### 4.2.3. GENDER AND ENERGY IN THE ENERGY SECTOR

A key component of Senegal’s Plan for an Energy Sector (PSE) is the development of the power sector. The government aims to achieve universal access to electricity by 2025 by combining renewable energy with fossil fuels, and to reduce dependence on imported oil by increasing access to electricity, particularly in rural areas; reduction in dependence on imported hydrocarbons, such as petrochemicals, pharmaceuticals and fertilizers, as well as broad utilization of natural resources. The government has also identified Senegal’s potential for developing solar and wind power, and development of its offshore natural gas.

According to the Minister of Petroleum and Energy, Senegal is not looking just to boost the development of renewable energy sources but also to diversify the country's economy by using hydrocarbons, such as petrochemicals, pharmaceuticals and fertilizers, as well as broad utilization of natural resources. The government has identified Senegal’s potential for developing solar and wind power, and development of its offshore natural gas.

Recent changes in policies, driven by concerns over energy security, have improved the financial attractiveness of renewable energy and energy efficiency


---
**Page 248**
---

technologies in the country[17]. Although solar and wind energy, including the Tabia N’Doye wind farm, are currently two of the sub-sectors that comprise the bulk of renewable energy projects in the country, the government is eager to explore other sources of renewables, such as biocultures to capitalize on the large amount available from input agriculture by utilizing more biomass and agricultural waste products.

A recent review audit on energy policies in Senegal identifies a number of gender challenges in the energy sector:

i. Senegal’s Renewable Energy Policy includes women as a target group and the Policy incorporates gender equality principles. However, there is no specific mention of gender desks or focal points within the line ministries. However, these desks/focal points lack focused activities and deliverables and they have not been allocated specific budget etc.

ii. At the macro-level, there is a political will to mainstream gender, as evidenced by the inclusion of gender equality principles in national policies. However, this has yet to be translated into gender actions at the sectoral level because policy-makers lack the knowledge and skills needed for gender analysis. There are no gender desks or focal points for gender analysis as a planning tool and of gender mainstreaming strategies and action plans.

iii. There have been no appropriate consultations on the needs of men and women during the formulation of energy policies. The majority of those who took part in the formulation process were male and therefore proper assessment of women’s energy needs was conducted.

iv. There is an absence of gender-disaggregated data that would inform national energy budgeting, planning, and policy-making processes. As a result, decisions on policy making are not disaggregated by gender. In addition, there is lack of financial resources to support the implementation of gender mainstreaming strategies in the sector.

The most recent data from 2018 indicates that the electricity access rate in Senegal is 67%; 44.2% of rural areas and 92.4% of urban areas have access to electricity[18]. Since women play a significant role in household decision-making, they are disproportionately affected by energy shortages[19]. Women are disproportionally affected by en- ergy poverty because they are more likely than men to be responsible for the provision of high quality, safe and environmentally safe energy services to support economic and human development. In addition, women who live in rural areas (especially poor women) are often hindered in their use due to lack of appliances, information and training or education. In addition, institutional structures are often skewed towards men and as a result, women are less likely than men to have access to energy services such as elec- tricized electricity connection or liquefied petroleum gas (LPG) registration may require a bank account or identification documents, which places women and their enterprises (which are mostly informal) at a disadvantage[20].

Understanding energy use and decisions in household dynamics is key to delivering sustainable energy. According to the findings of a study conducted in Senegal120, socio-economic characteristics (age, household size, land and house ownership, wealth, earnings, religion, region, type of residential structure) and intra-household bargaining power lead to an increase of clean fuel adoption and that households using more than one source of energy are more likely to use clean fuels. Furthermore, it was found that households with more empowered women would be more likely to engage in the uptake of clean fuel and energy efficiency measures.

Overall, this research shows that empowering women is an effective response to climate change, as it would foster the adoption of clean technologies. Furthermore, a study by Energie+ reveals that Senegalese women are less likely than men to use electricity for lighting (50% vs 72%), mobile/smart phones, hand power tools and televisions. These differences are particularly significant in rural areas where women have fewer opportunities to access education and training compared with men, with fewer opportunities, less decision-making power and limited influence. Thus, a 'gender gap' exists between male and female entrepreneurs in Senegal, which is manifesting differential impacts and benefits in relation to energy use and greenhouse gas emissions.

### 4.2.3. KEY BARRIERS AND CHALLENGES FOR CLIMATE VENTURES AND ENTREPRENEUR SUPPORT ORGANIZATIONS (ESOS)

#### A. CLIMATE VENTURES

The following is a list of the key challenges in Senegal gathered from discussions with female climate ventures, Impact Hub and IPEd.

1. Limited access to finance is a key barrier for female-led clean-tech ventures at early, growth and maturity stages.
2. Lack of access to information services and other resources related to climate innovation. The major challenges faced by most female ventures are related to education. A high level of education - often a university degree in a relevant field - is required to be able to effectively participate in the design and implementation of projects that contribute towards the circular economy (focused on recycling), ecological conservation, energy and water conservation, etc.
3. Lack of access to technical services and other climate-related resources is also a key challenge. Women climate entrepreneurs, in particular, lack key skills and know-how or are not aware of how to use these resources effectively. This can be compounded by the fact that women often view themselves as less capable than men when it comes to developing robust business models.
4. The problem is compounded by lack of female role models and a lack of access to networks and information services, which are also barriers for other climate's ventures in Senegal. According to IPEd, the provision of technical assistance to women entrepreneurs is often not prioritized due to the challenges inherent in such innovation initiatives: e.g. finding ways to value waste or reduce waste, improving health and safety at work practices, hygienic aspect of products, etc.

3. Lack of pre-acceleration support at the ideation stage. Female ventures need to be supported in considering their business ideas, assess market viability, weigh what opportunities exist, and even more important for climate innovation, understand that climate innovation is relatively new to them. Yet few pre-acceleration programmes exist in Senegal with no flexible funding sources to entrepreneurs' ideas. The result is a high rate of venture failure. Umoja Dakaar, an initiative by GFA Group has recently started its first pre-acceleration programme to deal with these challenges (see Testimony 3).

4. Youth as the target group for existing pre-acceleration programmes: Most of the existing pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes are targeting other programmes for young people under the age of 35. This locks out women, who have a greater need and focus on serious business engagement at a later stage in life.

5. Lack of support for female entrepreneurs during programme monitoring: Many women entrepreneurs feel they have been "abandoned" after graduating from pre-acceleration pro- grammes. They are not supported by the programmes' monitoring and bridging technical support initiatives: e.g., advice and linkages to public and private sector financial service providers, continuous mentoring and coaching sup- port post programme graduation. The result is that many female entrepreneurs feel that their programmes are not well integrated and derailed such that female ventures that achieve early growth are not necessarily integrated into the programme.

survey sample were more educated, had the same professional experience as the male founders, and experienced similar revenue changes in the previous year.

2. Women entrepreneurs face significant time constraints due to the persistence of traditional gender roles. Many women are unable to participate fully in pre-acceleration services because they have family responsibilities that prevent them from benefiting from pre-acceleration services. For example, Jigen Tech (JTN) an Engineering and Technology Incubator in Senegal, has a program called "Women Empowerment" aimed at integrating women to the tech ecosystem in Senegal with networking, training, entrepreneurship coaching, mentoring and knowledge sharing. Faced challenges emerging from family responsibilities, 2016-2017 edition of the Women Empowerment program had 895 women to participate in its training courses; however, due to absenteeism and cancellations, only 34% (305) attended the training. The main explanation given for the absenteeism and cancellations were mostly related to family responsibilities. In total, 127 women did not attend the training, with 68 non-attendees responding "I could not find someone to look after my children... I did not get permission from my husband... It was too cold, I was already late but did not want to come".

3. Female entrepreneurs shy away from risky business ventures. This is especially true for women in agricultural sectors or tech-related ventures. However, according to Impact Hub Dakar the technology sector has a reputation of being more risk-taking than other sectors.

4. Difficulties in attracting women into pre-acceleration and acceleration programs. According to the same source, the language used when advertising these programs often uses words from the non-gender-respective French words often used in the announcement of the opportunity. For example, "opportunities for young entrepreneurs" which denote mixed gender but could be misunderstood to mean only men are targeted.

5. Difficulties in finding female trainers/coaches. The combination of climate change and technology means there is a smaller number of technical experts in the country and when the gender gap is considered, women are even less represented. This makes it difficult for women to further their training and coaching. Female trainees have more empathy for other women and, by sharing own experiences during the training and coaching, they support women to confront their challenges.

# 4.3. MEXICO

## 4.3.1. INSTITUTIONAL MECHANISMS GOVERNING MEXICAN ENTREPRENEURIAL ECOSYSTEMS

In recent decades, Mexico has made significant progress with regard to gender equality, but it is still far from achieving the goal of full gender equality and women's empowerment[1]. Women hold only 37% of parliamentary seats in Mexico and women in the Executive and Judicial branches are even less represented. In the Mexican workforce, women represent only 20% of employers, and almost 40% of the self-employed, yet they report paying more than double the minimum wage.

In the Mexican workforce, women represent only 20% of employers, and almost 40% of the self-employed, yet they report paying more than double the minimum wage in Mexico[1]. The average Mexican woman spends six hours each day doing unpaid housework, compared with an average of two hours for men. A large proportion of women are concentrated in the lowest-paying jobs; while the proportion of men and women is roughly the same when looking at jobs that pay up to the minimum wage, the proportion of men in jobs that pay double the minimum wages is double that of women[2]. Moreover, almost 30% of women work part-time or on a temporary basis. Women are also underrepresented among the CEO group average of 13%, which compounds the challenge of balancing multiple obligations and caring for children and elderly family members.

Women who decide to participate in the labour market may not have access to a full-time job, because unemployment is higher than it should be (40% compared with 25%), or they are employed on an informal basis as unpaid or self-employed[3]. iii) gender differences regarding employment are also evident when looking at the proportion of women and men who work in agriculture.

The 4th Article of the Constitution of Mexico asserts that women and men are equal before the law. While Article 1 establishes that in Mexico all persons shall enjoy the human rights recognized in the Universal Declaration of Human Rights, Article 2 states that equality between men and women is stressed in the General Law for Equality Between Women and Men, whose main objective is to promote gender equality by eliminating discrimination against women and men and proposes guidelines as well as institutional mechanisms towards the fulfillment of sub-constitutional obligations.

The 2020-2024 PROGULADUAL[4] programme focuses on putting women and girls at the centre of government attention. Key among the objectives of the programme are: priority 1: enhancing the economic empowerment of women; priority 2: promoting gender equality in education, by creating the conditions to recognize, reduce and redistribute domestic and care work people of between families and institutions; and priority 3: strengthening the capacity of women's organizations.

In addition, Mexico has adopted a law for creating the National Institute of Women (INMULERE), a law on the Equality Between Men and Women (2006), a general law on Women's Rights and Gender Equality (2015) and a law on Equal Opportunities in Employment as well as a Norm on Labour Equality and Non-Discrimination (2015). In addition, a National Network for Women's Empowerment was created with the creation of 44 Centres for Indigenous Women and 20 Development Centres for Women, further promotes gender equality and women's protection in marginalized communities and areas.

# 4.3.2 STATUS OF CLIMATE INNOVATION IN EDUCATION, AGRICULTURE AND ENERGY SECTORS

## 4.3.2.1 GENDER AND CLIMATE INNOVATION IN EDUCATION

Although the gender gap in education at all levels of schooling is closing, there are more girls and adolescents who have never attended school: excluding 8% of women 15 years of age or older and literates, compared to less than 5% of men in the same age group[20]. Also, 13.6% of women aged between 15 and 19 are not enrolled in secondary education, while for adolescents from 16 to 19 years old and to 75.3% among young women from 20 to 24 years old.

However, there are important differences across regions and population groups. For instance, in Chiapas the literacy rate is as high as 5.1% for women, compared with 3.65% for men. Indigenous women have a lower level of education than non-indigenous women: only 20% complete a primary education, and only a small share have tertiary education. Although there are substantial differences in the levels of education between indigenous and non-indigenous women across older cohorts, the differences between indigenous and non-indigenous women remains[21].

According to the National Association of Universities and Institutions of Higher Education (ANUIES), 50% of students enrolled at the master's level and 43.7% of those enrolled at the doctoral level. However, women are still under-represented in STEM fields compared with men: only 28.6% of full-time professors are women[22] at the National Autonomous University of Mexico (UNAM), which is considered one of the best universities in Latin America. On the other hand, women are overrepresented in certain professional occupations and under-represented in the high-technology sectors.

Although it is not at the same level as for male students, the number of women choosing science, technology, engineering and mathematics (STEM) studies is increasing. Having STEM knowledge and skills is a key factor to be able to contribute effectively to climate change mitigation efforts; however, having someone on the team who has academic qualifications in these areas can make a positive difference[23]. Women with STEM qualifications are more likely to participate in innovation activities: in addition, academic preparation in these areas could increase women's self-confidence and encourage them to pursue technology-oriented careers, as well as increase their participation in

# Gender and Climate Innovation in Agriculture

According to the UN,[1] the impacts of climate change in Mexico could lead to an increase in rain and tropical phenomena such as droughts across the country—and could consequently aggravate inequalities in health, employment and access to food. Similarly, USAID[2] reported that Mexico’s vulnerability to climate change is high, with a high risk of extreme weather events that would increase the country's exposure to frosts, heat waves, tropical cyclones and floods. Moreover, Mexico has been identified as one of the countries most vulnerable to climate change in terms of tourism industry. Despite being the birthplace of the wheat and maize innovations that led to the Green Revolution,[3] Mexico is now a net importer of foodstuffs. According to the Climate and Development Knowledge Network (CDKN)[4], Mexico is the world’s 13th largest emitter of greenhouse gases according to the UN.

As stated by Tecnológico de Monterrey, the current government has neither the capacity in climate change nor the resources to address it. The country faces a lack of investment and under-funding of many of the major scientific and research initiatives, among others the National


---
**Page 255**
---

# Council for Science and Technology (CONACYT), a federal programme in charge of the promotion of scientific and technological activities, setting government policies, and granting scholarships for postgraduate studies.

Under the new government administration, the National Entrepreneurship Programme was created to promote innovation and competitiveness in SMEs. The programme is aligned with the 2018-2034 strategy that allows for distribution of resources for entrepreneurs through a competitive process. This strategy also includes important pillars that supported the creation of ventures through incubators and accelerators, because many of them were required to be registered as companies.

The Mexican government is nevertheless making some efforts in the light against climate change, which is now common to make climate change a top national priority and integrating climate change mitigation and adaptation priorities into development strategies, as Mexico's Secretary of Environment and Natural Resources (SEMARNAT) has done. At the federal level in Mexico, the Federal Expenditure Budget (PEF) has set aside resources for gender equality and climate change since 2012. Several national development programmes also provide support to women farmers and rural communities to adapt to climate change, such as PROAGROCLIMA, as well as different programmes from the Secretary of Environment and Natural Resources that aim at reducing deforestation.

However, corruption, inefficiency, bureaucracy and inefficiency in the management of such initiatives hamper their implementation. Unlike West African countries, women in the Mexican agricultural sector are not the majority. For example, in 2015, only one out of ten employees in the agricultural sector were men. The sector employed around 6.68 million people, of whom 5.88 million were women and 794 thousand were men. One reason for this is that agriculture in Mexico mainly focuses on large-scale production of corn, wheat and vegetables, which are mostly grown by male farmers who own the land. Women usually must travel for work, this discourages many female labourers. In addition, the female kilos re-visited in 2015 showed that women's wages were lower than men's and do not provide a conducive environment to women workers. Women are said to fear for their safety given their drinking alcohol and drug abuse among male labourers.

Regarding land ownership, according to the World Bank a key challenge faced by women is the lack of access to land. This problem was perpetuated by the 1992 land reform which introduced the concept of communal land possession. For example, in 2015, of the 4.2 million Mexican community members, only 38% were women and they owned just 7.6% (private property rights), only 19.8 percent were men and they owned 82.4%. This means that 23% of the women are property owners but do not have rights over common resources, and

42% are categorized as "wetters," meaning they are inhabitants without private property rights or common use and without voting rights. Furthermore, only 12.5% of the 350,000 representatives, incumbents and alternates in management positions in local assemblies and governing bodies are women. This is despite the fact that women represent a majority (54%) of the population with power[1]. The implementation of this policy looks out women from effective innovation and adaptation.

With regard to climate innovation and agriculture, Agricultural Research for Development (AR4D) has been instrumental in benchmarking agricultural innovations across the globe. According to Badiane et al.[2], on-farm research is a key strategy for increasing productivity and targeting farmers' needs and seeking to address the visible symptoms of inequality. However, implementing this approach requires a change in the way we think about innovation and ways in which social norms, attitudes and distribution of power and resources differently frame women's access to agricultural innovations. Women are often seen as passive actors who are not positioned to take advantage of new opportunities do so; while others fall further behind (see Testimony 1).

In the AgTech start-up ecosystem, Argentina and Brazil account for 74% of the total AgTech ventures in the Latin America region. Reasons include large-scale markets, ecosystems favouring agri-tech innovation, and a strong focus on precision agriculture, etc. The AgTech sector in Mexico generates around US $9 billion (645,457 every year) and is considered to be one of the top 10 food producing and exporting countries. Mexico and Colombia are considered to have a high level of experience in the vegetable sectors, not only in terms of production but also in terms of sales and distribution.

This has provided a conducive environment for ven-tures like Bioniche, which is focused on sustainable agriculture while reducing the use of agri-chemicals and improving soil health. The company's product, BioCricket, restores ecosystem health. The company won the Social Impact Award at the 2019 Global Founda-tion[3] for being a company with the greatest social impact[4]. BIONICHE is a Mexican venture led by women scientists, focuses on production of insect-based protein from crickets – not only complementing animal feed but also providing an alternative to conventional proteins. It reduces emissions and also reduces the high environmental footprint caused by alternative proteins such as soybeans or soya beans. The company has been successful in Mexico and Central America. Nevertheless, being a new venture, the company faces some longer-term challenges related to scalability and commercialization.
