### Gender and Climate Innovation in Energy

Mexico has signed both the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) as well as the Kyoto Protocol, and has submitted a detailed inventory of its greenhouse gas emissions for the period 1990-2015.

The Government of Mexico (GOM) has made substantial efforts at the policy level to reduce GHG emissions by increasing the generation and use of renewable energy and by improving energy efficiency. In 2007, the Government published the National Climate Change Strategy, followed by new legislation in 2008 that established a national climate change program.

The Special Programme for Climate Change (2009-2012) was adopted in 2009 and pledges to reduce Mexico's greenhouse gas emissions by 5% below business as usual levels by the year 2020. However, on 30 December 2010 Mexico submitted its updated NDC: its targets, both conditional and unconditional, are more ambitious than those in the previous projections under business-as-usual (BAU) continue to increase. This reduces the country's carbon intensity by 45% below 2005 levels and moves the rating of this target one category lower to "highly insufficient."

The government has also introduced a number of measures to promote the massive generation of clean power to reach the levels set forth in the Climate Change Act for the electric power industry. The goal is to have 35% of Mexico's electricity generated from renewable energy by 2021 and 50% by 2040.

To achieve environmental sustainability, Mexico has introduced a set of measures for promoting the production of clean energy and the protection of ecosystems. The government has also promoted gender equality in the energy sector through differentiated roles to women and men, which condition their relationship to energy. Women are not only involved as consumers but also as producers of energy.

In addition, they have the power to influence the purchase decisions of their families. A market study conducted by GIZ for Grupo Semarnat revealed that, among the members of the household, the mother is the one who decides what has been said about clean energy, followed by both (father and mother), then the father, daughters, and then "all." Women also have great influence in the decision-making process regarding the purchase of appliances and devices for clean energy resources. However, energy is often perceived as being gender-neutral and women are not considered to be a target group when it comes to energy policies, as it is considered to be a technical and gender-neutral issue.

Since 2001, the Ministry of Environment, SEMARNAT**, has had a Gender Equity Policy which includes the promotion of gender equality in the environmental sector. SEMARNAT also led the National Programme for Equality and Opportunities (Programa Nacional de Igualdad y Oportunidades, PNIIO) and the Regional Gender and Environment Network (Red de Género y Medio Ambiente, RGEN). In order to promote gender equality in the energy sector, SEMARNAT has developed a number of initiatives.

The energy sector in Mexico is undergoing important changes in the context of energy reform. For example, SENER's strategies are anchored in addressing the need to align and coordinate perspectives on gender equality with those of the energy sector. The structure of SENER has the following structure: i) A Regulatory Improvement and Transversal Programmes Unit (Unidad de Mejora Regulatoria y Programas Transversales, UMRPT); ii) A Monitoring and Evaluation Unit that drives and promotes the implementation of PROGUALIDAD in the energy sector; iii) Gender Equality and Non-Discrimination Unit.

**Climate Action Tracker Website: Mexico's updated NDC leaves its climate ambition and transparency, contrary to the Paris Agreement. https://climateactiontracker.org/countries/mexico/ **

### 4.3.3. KEY BARRIERS AND CHALLENGES FOR CLIMATE VENTURES AND ENTREPRENEUR SUPPORT ORGANIZATIONS (ESOS)

#### A. THE CLIMATE VENTURES

The following barriers were identified through literature review, discussions with Tecnolo- gico de Monterrey and some of the female ventures interviewed during the Gender Assess- ment.

Limited access to finance: According to Colombia University, nearly 75% of all women-led SMEs in Latin America lack adequate to adequate financing[1]. Colombia University reports that a high number of women entrepreneurs are unable to obtain credit because they do not have collateral (because of their gender [27%, versus 4% for men]) and 51% of the women surveyed affirm that they have never received any financial advice. Women also report that they lack access to adequate equity training and business advice. In Mexico, only 19% of entrepreneurs are female, and only 1% of women have access to monetary resources necessary to starting their business[2]. Based on interviews with female entrepreneurs in Mexico, the following barriers to accessing financial can be as follows:

1. Limited access to credit: Many of the female entrepreneurs interviewed mentioned how many unmarried female entrepreneurs are seen as a “risky” investment by financial institutions, since they do not have a husband or partner to support them and repay loans. In addition, given the lack of access to collateral assets (as land, cash), women entrepreneurs frequently do not have sufficient collateral to raise large funds[3].

2. Bureaucratic processes in government financial support institutions: Government institutions such as FONACOT and CFE are mentioned in 4.3.2.2. as having bureaucratic procedures which entrepreneurs are expected to attend meetings and other courses before accessing funds. Given the heavy work burden women face, it is challenging for them to fully exploit opportu- nities of accessing funds, particularly if they live in rural areas far from government offices.


---
**Page 259**
---

3. Apart from an equity stake, private investment funds ask for 100% commitment from venture founders. As demonstrated by Testimony 20, female entrepreneurs are discouraged and continuously rely on their friends and family for funding.

4. According to the Inter-American Development Bank, women-owned businesses in Latin America have the second-highest failure rate after those owned by men. Women own only 13% of all businesses in Latin America, yet they employ more than half (52%) of its workforce. The number of women-owned enterprises has closed their companies due to low access to credit and lack of business skills.

5. Lack of confidence: As stated in the section below, female entrepreneurs interviewed at the KII discussions that there is a general lack of business understanding among female entrepreneurs (see Testimony 20).

6. According to the Ministry of Labor, women work an average of 13 hours more per day on household chores and childcare than men.

7. Lack of confidence: According to the Global Entrepreneurship Monitor® 2021, an important barrier for women in Mexico is the lack of confidence when it comes to starting a business, and a significant influence on whether or not they will start their own business may be, and what

### Difficulties in encouraging women entrepreneurs (with specific examples)

According to Tecnológico de Monterrey, among the ventures it supports, it is less likely to see women in Chief Executive Officer (CEO) or Chief Technology Officer (CTO) positions unless they are in a team of only women.

There is still a conservative culture in Mexico (especially in upper-class Monterrey), where women are expected to be homemakers and not work outside the home until a certain age. It is also expected for women not to work as a symbol of economic position.

In Tecnológico de Monterrey University, at least 20% of the students are women. However, there are still more men than women in the high-tech or scientific ventures model. This is because women tend to come from public universities where there are usually fewer resources and less support for full-time researchers. It is, therefore, less common for them to have access to funding or networks to find women developers or CTOS. The few women who do enter these fields often "usually have roles more related to the business side of venture (accounting, finance, marketing, etc.)." (see Testimony 23)

### Lack of role models in climate entrepreneurship

Overall, it has been difficult to find female role models for early-stage women entrepreneurs. Currently, Tecnológico de Monterrey is working with only one local female role model: the president and CEO of Sofía & a member of the University's board.

At the last pitching event, despite aiming and achieving a male-female ratio of three to one, there are still no women mentors or judges.

**Venture Capital (VC) firms**

Many investors in Mexico are aware of the business case for gender diversity18** (see Error Reference source not found.). However, many obstacles have to be cleared before gender equality can take root in the VC world. Implicit biases exist within the venture capital and private equity sectors that make it difficult for women entrepreneurs to secure funding from these sources a priority19**. Overall, the majority of investors in Mexico have a misconception that gender lens investing is not a viable strategy for them20**. However, 75% of investors are open to incorporating gender into their processes and analyses. According to Value for Women21**, some investors believe that they need to be aware of a woman's family or other issues before they future sources of funding.

Not all investors have an explicit strategy for ensuring gender lens investing, given that 71% of the funds do not have diversity and gender inclusion policies; however, 65% are willing to develop them.


---
**Page 263**
---

and implement such policies[17]. A compelling reason for establishing gender diversity and inclusion policies and practices is linked to the need to respond to third-party requirements, specifically those of institutional investors.

According to a recent report by GFA, venture capitalists are not collecting sex-disaggregated data on their investments, which limits gender-related impact analysis as well as the creation of gender-smart products and services. According to Value for Women, only 5% of total venture capital and private equity funding goes to women-led enterprises. A comparison of 2019 and 2020 overall venture performance is shown in Figure 7. Female-only groups attracted $34 million (6%) and mixed groups attracted $86 million, while mixed groups and male-only groups attracted $5 billion and $86%, respectively.

The following challenges have emerged: female-only groups attract less than 1% of total VC investment, while mixed groups and male-only groups attracted 90% and 86%, respectively. The criteria most commonly used by most VCs are not gender-sensitive. The most common investment objective of VC firms in Mexico is realizing an exceptional return for their investments. Some of these criteria often discourage women entrepreneurs, since women entrepreneurs have challenges with committing full-time to the business, as well as lack the business skills and know how to quickly and effectively scale up their business.

## Limited number of women in investment teams and key decision-making positions: Gender bias in VC firms is not limited to their investment decisions and strategies, but also largely present in their own internal structures and administration.

According to Datasus, only a small percentage of the companies have senior women in their investment teams and almost a quarter (22%) do not have any women at all in their investment team. It was found that 61% of the decision-makers in investment teams are men. In fact, the representation of women at the intermediate level and at the entry level was found to be lower than that of the senior level. Women occupy 38% of the positions at the intermediate level and 42% of the positions at the entry level in the studied sample of the market.

Despite the disappointing numbers, the market is recognizing the opportunity to invest in gender diversity and includes Datasus, for example, uses its Gender & Diversity Strategy to help companies achieve their goals. The strategy has three main pillars: increasing women KFs, by 2023 Datasus plans to achieve the following outcomes: retain the women in their investment teams at a rate of 5% to 10%, increase the proportion of women on their boards from 4% to 10%, and increase the proportion of women on their executive boards from 14% to 20%. Despite these plans, there are limited networking opportunities because they lack the appropriate networks to connect to investors. This is a challenge that is not unique to Datasus but also applies to other companies.

Socio-cultural norms cause women-led businesses to be evaluated differently. Although the research was done for STEMtech, the challenges seem to cut across women in founded and non-founded firms.

## Investors cite women's lack of confidence as the cause for low investment: The IBD Lab (V4) study found that women entrepreneurs are more likely than men to overstate their projections and that female entrepreneurs do not ask exactly what they want, and they find hard to internalize their accomplishments. In addition, when female entrepreneurs pitch, they tend to be less confident in their abilities and actually take feedback very personally. While the projection of lack of confidence seems to be a major issue for women entrepreneurs in Latin America, it is also an issue in West Africa, face discrimination during pitching - especially when the investors focus on questions about family life or personal life. This is something that was highlighted by a Mexican founder based in Colombia who noticed that her male co-founders received


---
**Page 265**
---

more direct questions about long-term strategy, yet she attracted questions about her ability to lead (see Testimony 23). These lines of questioning make female entrepreneurs feel as though they must be on the defensive and constantly proving themselves, rather than having the opportunity to expand their ideas.

### Lack of strong personal and professional networks: The IOB Lab / WX Insights report identifies this as the second-greatest challenge. The report states that there is a strong link to whom the entrepreneurs know and that women entrepreneurs are "not being introduced to investors or other key players in the ecosystem." This stems from Latin American culture, which is driven by relationships and essential networks. As one of the investors interviewed for the WX Insights report stated: "The most important thing is someone in my network." Networks are essential for obtaining access to information about opportunities and resources. The WX Insights report reviewed by IOB Lab / WX Insights found that women entrepreneurs approach them through other women, while male entrepreneurs typically women within the investment team who often help other women. The report concludes that the more women there are on these teams, the better the ecosystem will be for future women entrepreneurs.

# INSTITUTIONAL CAPACITY AND GAPS AMONG PROJECT EXECUTING ENTITIES FOR MAINSTREAMING GENDER IN THE CATALI.S'T INITIATIVE

This chapter summarizes the gender capacities within the four Executing Entities.

## A. EXECUTING ENTITIES AT GLOBAL LEVEL

### 1. Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH

GIZ will be responsible for the project's overall management as well as for capacity building measures, including training and mentoring of staff on gender equality, climate change adaptation and gender, climate impact forecast tools and input data measuring & monitoring. GIZ undoubtedly has a strong track record in this area.

GIZ also has a number of other accountability frameworks such as Do No Harm and prevention of sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (PSEA) policies, which guide the project's work on capacity building.

### CAPACITY GAP

GIZ does not have a gender-related gap that needs strengthening by the project.


## 2. Stichting Climate-KIC International Foundation

The Stichting Climate-KIC International Foundation, hereafter named "Climate-KIC", is a non-profit organization with the objective to contribute to the protection of nature and the environment.

GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ANALYSIS REPORT | 55


---
**Page 267**
---

Including the enhancement of sustainability, in aid of the common good, both ecological and human, by way of innovation of climate change mitigation and adaptation, and by stimulation of education and research in the field of climate change mitigation and adaptation. Climate-KIC aims to contribute to this goal through its mission to support the European Commission’s goal of triggering a systematic change in gender across the climate innovation sector. Sticking Climate-KIC’s commitment to equality and diversity is reflected in the composition of boards and systems of Climate-KIC Holding.

Climate-KIC's Equity and Diversity Policy describes its commitment towards the elimination of discrimination, promoting equality and diversity within its stakeholder network, as well as in the areas in which it has influence. The following elements relate to Climate-KIC’s internal and external commitments:

- **Climate-KIC** has overall male to female ratio of 1:1 on its boards.
- In 2020, its management team was 83% female.
- At employment level in 2020, 67% of Climate-KIC's 1,958 staff were female.

Through Diversity and Inclusion (D&I) initiatives, multiple groups are active in ensuring gender mainstreaming; e.g. the management team working group on culture is taking responsibility for integrating diversity and inclusion in leadership and ways of working, the Gender Equality Taskforce is responsible for developing a Gender Equality Action Plan and Education programmes, and the Ethics Group develops, reviews and makes recommendations on policies.

For Climate-KIC’s community of partners, innovators and entrepreneurs, Climate-KIC strives to enable them to better attract and support women-led innovations and initiatives through the full lifecycle of their projects. This is achieved by providing tailored guidance and resources for partners with proactive advice and practical action guidance to actors in the climate entrepreneur-ship ecosystem. These include the Women Entrepreneurs Accelerator (WEA), which integrates gender equity in their interventions and become more gender-smart. Recent developments include the launch of the WEA’s Toolkit for Entrepreneurs, a digital library of resources (WEA), on the development of two “WeRise TooKits”, one Toolkit for Entrepreneurs” and one Toolkit for Accelerators.

Many of the programmes implemented under Climate-KIC's climate entrepreneurship pro- gramme have been exploring the nexus between gender and climate innovation, with the Toolkit for Accelerators being a key example. Both toolkits provide step-by-step guidance, ranging from organisational self-assessment on in- clusive readiness, inclusive channels and criteria for selection of ventures and accelerators, external support to women entrepreneurs, and more. The WEA is one of the GCF project’s efforts in reducing gender biases and increasing gender mainstreaming among climate venture capital funds.

Climate-KIC's current gender policy, practices and tools for mainstreaming gender within the organisation and across the globe through its programmes and partners is substantive. Climate- KIC’s environment and social impact assessment (ESIA) process has a specific scope for leveraging gender-related opportunities, notably in the context of the implementation of the Gender Action Plan and capacity building on gender climate entrepreneurship toolkits.

Capacity Gap

The Toolkit for Entrepreneurs has been created specifically for women entrepreneurs who own an SME in the so-called rising middle-income countries. The Toolkit is designed to provide a step-by-step guide for women entrepreneurs, with a focus on co-created by women entrepreneurs for women entrepreneurs, it directly aimed at women entrepreneurs in this situation, providing them with practical advice and resources to help them grow their business.

The Toolkit for Accelerators has been created specifically for and with accelerators that are focusing on becoming more gender-smart. The Toolkit is designed to provide a step-by-step guide for accelerators, including women entrepreneurs, accelerators and finance experts, and is accessible at https://climate-kic.org/women-on-digital-library-public

B. EXECUTING ENTITIES AT REGIONAL LEVEL
### LATIN AMERICA

#### Tecnológico de Monterrey
Tecnológico de Monterrey's "Entrepreneurship Institute Eugenio Garza Llaguno" is responsible for designing and implementing programs promoting entrepreneurship within the University as well as through external programs. Tecnológico de Monterrey will be the Executing Entity for the pre-accession phase of the project.

Interviews with key management staff indicate that Tecnológico de Monterrey has been at the forefront of progressive internal diversity and inclusion policies. Additionally, within the framework of the UN Women Global Movement HeforShe, a gender equality plan has been created, which, among others, have the objective to reinforce the recognition of human dignity and respect for all people in the university community. The University also has special offices to deal with gender issues, such as the Human Rights Recognition Center. Tecnológico de Monterrey is committed to implementing national policies for ensuring that gender-related misconduct is prevented and addressed. Through the Office of Diversity and Community, different actions towards gender equality have been implemented, as described below:

- **The Zona Shero programme**, which is a collaborative effort between Eugenio Garza Llaguno Entrepreneurship Institute and UN Women, aims to promote women's entrepreneurship by strengthening the progress and development of female students and entrepreneurs in the global entrepreneurship ecosystem.
- For direct support (not exclusively for female entrepreneurs), the Heineken Green Challenge (HGC) is an initiative to bring together entrepreneurs who develop innovations that contribute to sustainable economic growth while also addressing social issues and solving environmental problems in Mexico. Women entrepreneurs are strongly encouraged to participate.
- The University has a gender plan, laying out sets of measures for the equal treatment and opportunities for women and men in the institution's academic and administrative environment. A number of initiatives have been established. Impuls is a committee that has the objective of assuring progress towards gender equality within the university community. It is responsible for executing, following-up and enhancing the development of the gender equality plan.
- Tecnológico de Monterrey is also a member of UN Women's Global Movement HeforShe, with strategies for contributing to gender equality and prevention of gender violence.

The University has also launched a research programme around entrepreneurship in Mexico. By changing entrepreneurs' attitudes towards risk-taking, it aims to increase the number of women who start their own businesses, such as company creation rate, survival, longevity, and job creation, which could form an interesting comparison with the results from this research on gender patterns in climate innovation ventures. It would be interesting to cross-check the data from this research with ESG's ESG Impact metrics (see IPD section below).


---
**Page 269**
---

For Tecnológico de Monterrey’s broader entrepreneurship programmes, such as the HSG and Enlace+, Tecnológico de Monterrey applies a “Conscious Entrepreneurship Strategy,” which incorporates gender considerations. All Tecnológico de Monterrey employees are required to take gender equality courses and are subjected to all the gender policies as an institutional requirement.

### Gender equality gap
Tecnológico de Monterrey’s Conscious Entrepreneurship Strategy has been created to drive gender equality in its initiatives. There has been substantial work undertaken at the institutional level, but there is still a significant gender gap between internal employees and external beneficiaries (such as supported ventures).

However, with the growth of climate-specific entrepreneur events and processes which have not been specifically designed for this environment, the University has been evolving its gender practices. The management team of the Entrepreneur arm of the University feels that there are opportunities to improve gender equality in their programmes.

There is a lack of coordination between the institution’s gender initiatives as they have not always been coordinated or “joined up” and they are evolving independently. There is also a lack of integration with other initiatives, such as their pre-acceleration and acceleration entrepreneurship programmes around more structured gender equality policies. The University has therefore decided to implement an Entrepreneurial Ecosystem (EE) that includes such a structured gender ecosystem approach for all stages of the entrepreneurship programme. The EE is committed to ensuring gender equality within its programmes. The staff are eager to support this initiative, but there may be some resistance from those who have not been part of the project so far. This resistance will need to be managed and addressed in order to mitigate potential gender-related risks.

## II. WEST AFRICA
### A. Investors & Partners: Entrepreneurs & Development (IPED)
IPED will assume the role of the Executing Entity for the acceleration programme in West Africa, and will also manage the disbursement of support grants under the pre-acceleration programme.
IPED is affiliated with the impact investment group Investisseurs & Partenaires (I&P) and builds up on its experience in this field.

IPED has a gender policy, which mainly focuses on: 1) promoting gender equality; and/or 2) integrating a gender lens in funds, programmes and investments. The policy operates at both DIVERSA and IPED levels.
The gender policy is clear and detailed, delineating the different steps that must be taken at all times to ensure gender equality. It also includes specific targets for each of the portfolio levels which are tracked on an annual basis. From an internal perspective, the policy is deployed via recruitment policy and by promoting gender diversity in corporate governance bodies. The policy also covers the implementation of gender equality initiatives within IPED’s funding initiatives. The policy is also pursued at investment portfolio level by factoring gender considerations into all stages of the investment process: from initial screening to exit officer, employee, client and sub-contractor level. All of these elements provide strong evidence of IPED’s commitment to addressing gender equality issues and gender mainstreaming.
The policy is implemented at all levels of the organization:
- Management level: for leadership and overall governance; e.g. 30% of women at management level.

        * A target of 30% of women-owned businesses set up for an equity investment programme (IPEA).

In addition, IPEA uses specific metrics by gender to ascertain the proportion of female entrepreneurs, senior executives, employees and customers they reach. They also track 20% of their investments in women-owned businesses and 15% of their investments in women-friendly investments. For IPEA, gender is now a pillar in itself that must be analyzed in any programme or project to foster opportunities for improvement and reducing gender inequality in action plans.

## Capacity Gap

IPEA does not have a specific person responsible for gender equality among its staff. However, gender equality is comprehensively addressed under its broader Environmental Social and Governance (ESG) umbrella, and there is an ESG & Impact Committee composed of high-level experts who are responsible for this.

IPEA has also built ESG impact tools that are adapted to each fund’s theory of change and impact model in order to better address ESG issues (including gender) in the screening, investment and monitoring process.

Discussions with the ESG Impact Measurement team in IPEA revealed that gender mainstreaming and equality is not a priority for them. They are more focused on financial performance. In some cases, the environment and governance aspects of the ESG have more prominence than the social aspect. The ESG & Impact Committee is currently working on this issue and is currently updating its gender policy, tools, key performance indicators (KPIs) and practices, with the aim of strengthening gender integration across its programmes.

## Inclusion

Impact Hub Abidjan is a small institution of 13 employees founded by four women who, all independently of each other, had previous experience as entrepreneurs. The founders’ explicit objective was to provide support for the development of female entrepreneurs to technical assistance and finance in the entrepreneurship ecosystem. Impact Hub Abidjan has been strong in supporting women ventures are given greater emphasis in the West Africa region, supported by the following:

* 100% of its founders and 80% of its board members are women.
* 62% of its trainers and coaching experts are women.

After attaining lower than expected female-led ventures in its early support projects, Impact Hub Abidjan has been working on this issue, for instance, by designing specific calls that target value chains that have large numbers of female entrepreneurs (e.g. agribusinesses) or by providing training to women.

# GENDER EXPERTISE NECESSARY TO THE PLANNED GCF PROGRAMME

The planned CATALYST Initiative should employ a Gender Manager with in-depth knowledge and implementation experience in gender equality and mainstreaming in entrepreneur support programmes. The vacancy should explicitly require such expertise.

# GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ANALYSIS REPORT

## Countries: India and South Africa

### Project Overview

The project builds on Climate-KIC's newly developed gender and climate tool for incubators and accelerators (previously piloted in Kenya) to support women entrepreneurs in Nigeria, with the aim of crowding-in gender equality into the business model of these partners. The project will work with partners to:

- Strengthen local capacity-building structures related to gender equality and entrepreneurship.
- Increase the number of women participants in programmes.
- Incentivize partners to apply a gender lens to their programmes by actively embedding new gender approaches.

### Project Activities

The project focuses on providing training to the partner organisations, including workshops for staff and mentors. It will also provide support to partners to develop gender-focused pilot projects within their local ecosystem. The project aims to:

- Transform partners' own organisation to better support women.
- Develop a model of next stage enabling action or co-investment material (e.g., a business plan template).
- Provide direct support to women-led start-ups (e.g., create a mentorship network, provide access to funding opportunities).

### Expected Outcomes

The project aims to:

- Support more women entrepreneurs to develop an innovative idea and become entrepreneurially active through innovative concept-building on existing gender-to-market solutions.
- Improve access to finance for women-led start-ups. While the fund will not be directly involved in providing financial support, it is expected that partners will use this as a catalyst to run similar programmes in the future.

### Gender Tool

The gender tool will be an integral component of the CATALYST™ project gender mainstreaming efforts. Lessons learnt from the above activities will inform the development and refining of the tool and ultimately, the CATALYST™ initiative gender outcomes.

Since 2019, G4A initiatives have been conducting evidence-based impact evaluations to assess the effectiveness of projects and presenting findings in working papers, reports, policy briefs, and other publications. The initiative provides the following:

- A Gender Data Portal for rural Mexico: e.g., Mexico (https://genderdata.cofundmx.org/programme/mexico).
- Evidence-based impact data by LAC Governments.
- Dissemination of evidence and partnering across socio-economic groups.
- Capacity-building to build knowledge on gender-inclusive evaluations.

3. Publications, e.g., Women Entrepreneurs in Mexico: Building Sectoral Resilience and Reskilling Profits (2021).

7. GENERAL CONCLUSIONS

In summary, the gender assessment has brought to light the following:

- A strong business case for gender in entrepreneurship within the SME sector exists and is supported by a growing body of evidence. Due to the nascent status of "cleantech", engagement with gender issues in the 'climate venture' space specifically is less developed than in other sectors, but the lessons learned from the KIC's pool of expertise is expected to immeasurably benefit the project. IPED in West Africa also has a strong track record of working on gender equality within the gender-entrepreneur nexus, though not specifically in a climate change context.

The governments in Latin America and West Africa have ratified international and regional gender equality conventions and signed numerous national-level agreements with numerous measures to improve gender equality. However, concrete actions – backed, crucially by political will – are still lacking, with the following gender gaps in three key sectors that have direct links to climate change (agriculture, education, agriculture and energy) being prominent and persistent:

- Women's access to land is a critical issue for women farmers, particularly in Latin America. This is due to a combination of structural and rigid social-cultural barriers and lack of broader enabling gender ecosystem. Although there are some important exceptions, the challenges faced by women from that in Latin America, lack of status equality, lack of business understanding and role-models, and lack of access to finance are common challenges across the two regions.

- For the Executing Entities, difficulties in recruiting large numbers of women into their pre-existing gender-balanced teams, as well as a limited number of female role models and mentors, are common features across the two regions.

The Gender Assessment also identifies some of the ways that the Executing Entities, local implementation partners and other ecosystem actors can tackle gender-related challenges and create innovative strategies for women's inclusion.

# Recommendations

## A. General Recommendations for Project Design

1. Ensure EEs and local implementation partners have the capacities and necessary tools for gender mainstreaming (see Section 5 on institutional capacity assessment). It is clear that there is strong gender commitment from different EEIs, however, gender mainstreaming is not always fully embedded in their work plans and does not seem to be the same across all individual EEs. In this regard, the following recommendations are seen as crucial for the project:

   i. Develop a unified gender-mainstreaming document for the project that provides essential tools for gender and climate innovation addressing the regional context.
   
   ii. Ensure EEs and local implementation partners are adequately trained on gender mainstreaming in climate innovation. The training could, among other benefits, provide them with the necessary skills to integrate gender considerations into their innovation generation and acceleration programmes and be able to advise and train climate ventures on gender issues.

   iii. Rather than appointing and training one focal point person from each EE, ensure the staff of the two regional initiatives CATALYST (EEI) and CATALIST (EE) are trained. This will also ensure that all EEs and local implementation partners are trained. It will also create more buy-in and momentum for gender mainstreaming in climate innovation across the project and its implementation activities and end, to effective realisation of the EEIs' commitments on gender equality.

2. Encourage support and document cross-learning on climate gender mainstreaming between EEs and regions. There is currently little documented evidence into what strategies were used by different EEs and regions to integrate gender considerations in their work plans. Gathered from interviews with the EEs reveals some promising strategies, such as focusing on research and development of gender-sensitive technologies, creating gender-inclusive women platforms and networks, and anchoring the pre-acceleration/acceleration programmes in gender equality. It is recommended that such promising strategies are further tested during the project and the best pathways identified for scaling and learning. There is an opportunity for more deliberate and systematic cross-learning between EEs and regions, particularly between country-level ventures where (particularly) funding country- and community-level voices and insights into gender and climate innovation can be better integrated in the work of EEs and local implementation partners through, for example workshops and reporting of best practices.

3. Review and update the project's gender indicators. These indicators should be integrated in the project's learning framework.
4. Include gender diversity in the project's governance structure in both regions.

## B. General Recommendations for the EEIs Running the Pre-Acceleration and Acceleration Programmes

1. Ensure calls for applications and scouting are inclusive and encourage applicants of all genders:
   - Use different channels and networks to reach all genders.
   - Hold information events and consider holding separate women-only events.
   - Create a gender-sensitive application form that integrates gender-sensitive language to avoid confusion in the French and Spanish interpretation of some gender-sensitive words.

## GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ANALYSIS REPORT

### EXCLUSION CRITERIA

**Ventures will be excluded if:**

a. They have a business model with obvious negative implications for women (e.g., exorbitant hours, long commutes, or working hours without paid compensation).

b. At the mid-stage of development but none of their founders and employees are women.

### SELECTION CRITERIA

**Pre-acceleration:**

a. The selection process should be open to ventures that have:

- A least two of its founders identify themselves as female or minority group members (e.g., a woman of color, person with positions opposed by gender e.g. an applicant preferential treatment for women).
- Explicit plans for increasing the number of women in top and middle level management, in line with their business growth plan.

b. A prioritization score should be given to ventures that:

- Are 30%-50% or one or more women and/or have at least 40% of its founders identify themselves as female or minority group members.
- In Latin America, this could be differentiated with a preference for women in managerial positions.

c. Have explicit high performance indicators for increasing the number of women in top and middle level management, in line with their business growth plan.

d. Have products or services that address a gender-related socio-economic issue (e.g., increase women's wages; reduce women unpaid work burden; unlock the potential of women entrepreneurs; promote female participation in male-dominated sectors - e.g. climate-related technology off-take).

e. Have basic policies or procedures for facilitating a safe and conducive working environment for all employees, including a code of conduct against sexual harassment, abuse and discrimination and equal recruitment and wage policies.

f. Have capacity to mentor and network with women entrepreneurs and provide best practices for successful women entrepreneurs at the pre-acceleration stage.


---
**Page 279**
---

### Establishing gender-diverse selection panels

A study by GALI found that "having more than 40% women on a selection committee is associated with significantly more women-led ventures in applicable posts" (2018).

#### Adaptation of the programme curriculum language to be inclusive of all genders, including showcasing successful women entrepreneurs to provide role models and examples.

#### During programme delivery, identify and enhance the capacity of the venturers to maximize gender benefits.

- Integrate gender mainstreaming issues early on in the ventures, integrate gender in the venturers' capacity assessments and make available mechanisms for strengthening diversity (gender) awareness.
- Create a safe space where women can share their experiences, increase awareness of gender disparities and power dynamics that impact their own entrepreneurial journey and the venture's success. This will also help to reduce conscious and unconscious bias arising from their own actions. Additionally, include training on mechanisms for identifying and eliminating SEAH.

#### i. Ideation process should also capture women and minority voices. In West Africa, given the cultural norms around female participation in business, ideation sessions are conducted separately, or at least have some sort of facilitation support, for example, a woman facilitator to ensure that women participate fully. This is because research has shown that male dominance can be an issue in mixed groups and that women may not feel comfortable speaking up when men turns talking, and use disproportionate amounts of time when talking" (2018). It is, therefore, important that potential male dominance in mixed groups is prevented so that female voices are heard.

#### ii. Ensure training, mentoring and coaching venues and timings are suitable for female entrepreneurs to participate fully without feeling excluded.

#### Aim for gender diversity to deliver the programme. This includes building a gender-diverse pool of mentors and coaches so that all participants, especially female entrepreneurs, need not feel excluded.

#### Help to create and maintain network support communities by:

- Inviting successful women climate entrepreneurs as keynote speakers and role models to community-building and promotion events.
- Ensuring that networking opportunities are available for female entrepreneurs. Where events are undertaken online, video recordings (data protection mechanisms allowing access only to those who have registered) can be made available to attend these.
- Create a strong and supportive community of practice for women entrepreneurs. The strength of this community of practice lies in the collaboration and support that women give to each other, which often goes beyond the project lifespan. In addition, create an environment where women can connect with other women from different sectors, experts, funders and other like-minded individuals.
- Consider creating a gender-balanced network of mentors and coaches including public institutions supporting entrepreneurs in the respective regions to community building events.

#### References

### Recommendations for the Climate Ventures

#### Pre-acceleration stage:

i. Ventures should gain awareness of gender issues: e.g., disparities and power dynamics that impact their own entrepreneurial journey and how to apply a gender lens to product/service development, marketing, and sales. This includes understanding the specific needs and capacities required by women entrepreneurs and how they can meet these needs in their own actions. Additionally, include training on mechanisms for identifying and eliminating SEAH.

ii. Ventures should recognize the value of including sex-disaggregated data in their market research on their customers (different ways that men and women use a product or a service) and in their business models (e.g., how they can identify new opportunities, enable validation of specific products or services, and help refine and strengthen the business model).

iii. Ventures should be willing to identify priority areas and the type of support needed to have their own capacities built in gender mainstreaming during their participation in the program.

#### Acceleration stage:

For many ventures at this stage, especially those that have not gone through the project pre-acceleration phase, there is a growing awareness about gender issues. These ventures are interested in learning how to incorporate gender into their business. For many, the lack of awareness and understanding of gender issues has been a barrier to growth. Ventures that were focused only on trying to scale their business, become sustainable or, in some cases, just keep the business alive have not had time to consider gender issues as part of their strategy. Gender should be prioritized by the founders at this stage. To avoid inclusion issues being seen as additional burdens, the EUs can support the ventures to integrate gender considerations as early as possible within their own project design and implementation. For many ventures at an earlier stage, ventures would gain awareness of gender issues: e.g., disparities and power dynamics that impact women entrepreneurs in their entrepreneurial journey and how they can apply a gender lens to product development as well as how to reduce conscious and unconscious gender bias arising from their own business models.

i. Should commit to ensuring equal opportunity and equal pay for the same jobs among their employees / labourers: e.g., they should create a salary scale that does not discriminate based on gender.

ii. Should continually and deliberately analyze their team's composition by keeping an eye on gender ratios in the top and middle management teams and ensure venture growth plans include a strategy to increase female employee numbers and the gender composition of all part-time vs full-time employees.

iii. Should ensure gender inclusion in products and services: The ventures should be able to objectively identify how they can design their product or service to meet women's needs, their ultimate target is, and how they intend to get there. This is helpful in a number of ways:

- It helps to design more tailor-made products and services: e.g., as AgTech businesses must consider the different needs of male and female farmers and farm users and should, therefore, commit to ensuring its solution is designed with the digital gender gap in mind. This step will give the venture clarity on the product and market opportunities it can and will pursue.


---
**Page 281**
---

- It helps to recognize the value of gender-disaggregated data - understanding who the end user of its product is could be very useful for the venture’s business. Sometimes products can be purchased by a man but used by a woman; knowing this is helpful for targeting marketing and sales efforts, as well as for planning sales and product upsale efforts. In some cases, if the sales representative's gender and customer's gender are different, it could help to close deals.
- It also helps to attract investment from global financiers, who are becoming more interested in responsible and gender-inclusive investments.

iv. The venture should not promote or reinforce negative gender stereotypes and create market opportunities for their products. The ventures should celebrate women senior leaders and other women role models as well as provide opportunities to represent women publicly through marketing and other channels. The advertisements of their products and services should also refrain from negative gender stereotypes and present women and marginalized groups in a dignified way.

1. **INTRODUCTION**
   This Gender Action Plan (GAP) forms the basis for operationalizing the recommendations of the Gender Climate Change Alliance (GCCA). In this sense, the GAP provides a tangible and explicit accountability framework for gender mainstreaming through all the CATALYST Initiative activities and is intended to be reviewed and updated as necessary. At any given time, the GAP ensures the programme is compliant with the GCC’s gender policy (GCF/B.24).
   The GAP proposed below is indicative and may be further amended and adjusted by the AE during programme inception and implementation to reflect new knowledge gathered.

2. **OVERVIEW RECOMMENDATIONS FROM THE GENDER ASSESSMENT**
   A. **GENERAL RECOMMENDATIONS FOR PROJECT DESIGN AND IMPLEMENTATION**

1. Ensure the Executing Entities (EEs) and local implementation partners have the capacities and necessary tools for gender-climate mainstreaming. Based on the institutional capacity assessment, it is recommended that the EEs provide training to their staff and other beneficiaries in gender mainstreaming in climate innovation. However, gender expertise and capacities for gender mainstreaming in climate innovation is limited among most of the EEs. In this regard, the following recommendations are seen as be crucial for the project:
   i. Develop a unified gender-mainstreaming document for the project that provides essential tools for gender and climate innovation addressing the two regional contexts.
   ii. Ensure EEs and local implementation partners are adequately trained on gender mainstreaming in climate innovation. Training could include other beneficial practices such as providing practical guidance for conducting gender analysis, including acceleration programmes and be able to advise and train climate ventures on gender mainstreaming.

2. Encourage collaboration between the two regional initiatives CATALYST Initiative Latin America and CATALYST Initiative Africa. There is currently little documented evidence into what strategies work best in securing a healthy pipeline of female ventures, but anecdotal evidence gathered from the EEs suggests that such promising strategies are further tested during the project and the best pathways for scaling up gender mainstreaming in climate innovation are identified. It is recommended that systematic learning based on what gender and climate innovation strategies are working and where they can be scaled up (e.g., through replication of successful models, including climate change innovation at the global level) and then replicating the lessons among the EEs and local implementation partners through, for example, workshops and reporting of best practices.

3. Review and update the project’s GAP as applicable during implementation.


---
**Page 287**
---

4. Include gender diversity in the project’s governance structure in both regions.

B. GENERAL RECOMMENDATIONS FOR THE EEI-ACCELERATING THE PRE-ACCELERATION AND ACCELERATION PROGRAMMES

1. Ensure calls for applications and scouting are inclusive and encourage applicants of all genders:
   i. Use different channels and networks to reach all genders.
      a. Hold information events and consider holding separate women-only events.
      b. Create gender-sensitive language in the call for applications, using pregnant gender-sensitive language to avoid confusion in the French and Spanish interpretation of some gender-sensitive words.
      c. In materials, include messages to counteract negative stereotypes to encourage and attract female entrepreneurs.

2. Apply a gender lens when selecting ventures for the pre-acceleration and acceleration programmes so that all genders have an equal opportunity to be selected; this can be achieved through:
   i. Setting clear exclusion and selection criteria, including its weighting. The following criteria are examples of what could be considered and should be reviewed, implemented and refined on an ongoing basis during project implementation.

| b. At the initial due diligence interview, it was found to have none of their founders and employees being female and with no intentions of diversifying their teams in order to include more women. | | |

# Establishing gender-diverse selection panels

A study by GFA found that "having more than 45% women on a selection committee is associated with significantly more women-led ventures in applicant pools." (1)

## Adopting an inclusive and gender-balanced accelerator programme curriculum language to be inclusive of all genders, including showcasing successful women entrepreneurs to provide role models and examples women participants can relate to.

### During programme delivery, identify and enhance the capacity of the venturers to maximize impact

Introduce gender mainstreaming issues early on to the venture. Integrate gender in training sessions, particularly for those who are not aware or have limited understanding of identified capacity gaps. Specific training should focus on raising ventures' awareness of gender norms and power dynamics that impact their own enterprises, including how to avoid them and how to apply gender lenses when making decisions as well as how to reduce conscious and unconscious gender bias arising from their own actions. Additionally, include training on mechanisms for identifying and eliminating SEAH.

### Ideation should also capture women and minority views

In West Africa, give women and minorities the opportunity to participate in ideation sessions or conduct them separately, or at least have some sort of facilitation support for these groups. This is because research has shown that male dominance can be an issue in mixed groups during ideation, where "men are more likely to interrupt women, take turns talking, and use disproportionate amounts of time when talking." It is, therefore,

important that potential male dominance in mixed groups is prevented so that female voices and ideas are heard during ideation processes.

iii. Ensure training, mentoring and coaching venues and timings are suitable for female entrepreneurs to avoid exacerbating their time constraints.


5. Aim for gender diversity on the board of directors. This includes building a gender-diverse pool of mentors and coaches so that all participants, especially female entrepre- neurs', perspectives and experiences are understood and accommodated.

6. Help to create and maintain network support communities by:

i. Inviting successful women entrepreneurs as keynote speakers and role models in community-building and professional development events.

ii. Ensuring times for networking events are suitable for female entrepreneurs. Where events are undertaken online, video recordings (data protection mechanisms allowing access only to those who have registered to attend) should be made available so that they can attend these.

iii. Creating a strong and supportive community of practice for women entrepreneurs. The strength of this community of practice lies in the collaboration and support that women provide each other. This includes creating opportunities for women entrepreneurs to create exposure and link venture founders with networks of individuals or groups con- taining sector experts, funders and other like-minded individuals.

iv. Conducting key stakeholder interviews on gender mainstream- ing including public institutions supporting entrepreneurs in the respective regions to community building events.


C. RECOMMENDATIONS FOR THE CLIMATE VENTURES

I. pre-acceleration stage:

Venture leadership and management at this stage tends to be amorphous, which provides an opportunity for the project to have an early start gender mainstreaming into the management and operations of the venture. The ability to identify and address gender issues (and their root cause and cap- ture capacity) is essential. For the ventures to be gender-smart, the following topics for specific training are recommended:

i. Ventures should gain awareness of gender issues e.g., disparities and power dynamics that impact their own entrepreneurial journey and how to apply a gender lens to product/service development, marketing and sales, and operations. This will help them to better understand their own actions. Additionally, include training on mechanisms for identifying and eliminating SEAH.

ii. Ventures should recognize the value of including sex-disaggregated data in their market re- search on their customers (different ways that men and women use a product or a service) and in their employee recruitment practices. Clarifying gendered differences will reveal op- portunities to develop specific products or services, and help refine and strengthen the business value proposition.

ii. Ventures should be willing to identify priority areas and the type of support needed to have their venture succeed built in gender mainstreaming during their participation in the pro- gramme.


Acceleration stage:

For ventures at this stage, especially those that have not gone through the project pre-acceleration programme, they may already have rigid structures in place and may not initially be


---
**Page 290**
---

Interested in learning how to incorporate gender into their business. For many, the lack of awareness of the connection between gender inclusion and business performance will keep them focused only on trying to scale their business, become sustainable or, in some cases, just keep the business afloat. However, it is important for entrepreneurs to understand that gender inclusion should be prioritized by the founders at this stage. To avoid inclusion issues being seen as additional burden, they need to ensure that gender inclusion is embedded within their venture products and structures. Just like the ventures at pre-acceleration stage, ventures at seed stage need to consider how gender inclusion can impact their own entrepreneurial journey and how to apply a gender lens to product/service development as well as how to reduce conscious and unconscious bias arising from their own cultural background.

I. Should commit to ensuring equal opportunity and equal pay for the same jobs among their employees/ labourers: e.g. they should create a salary scale that does not discriminate against women.

II. Should continually and deliberately analyze their team's composition by keeping an eye on gender ratios in the top and middle management teams and ensure venture growth plans are inclusive of both genders. They must keep track of employee numbers and the gender composition of all part-time and full-time employees.

III. Should ensure gender inclusion in products and services. The ventures should be able to clearly specify what gender inclusion means for their products and services, what their ultimate goal is with regards to gender inclusion and how they will measure success.
- It helps to design more tailor-made products and services: e.g. an AgTech business may want to ensure that the yield of its female users is equal to that of its male users and therefore it should keep this in mind when designing its product and taking a gender gap into mind. This step will give the venture clarity on the product and market opportunities.
- It helps to recognize the value of gender-disaggregated data - understanding who the end-user of its product is could be very useful for the venture's business. Some times people are not aware that their customers are women, but this information can be helpful for designing marketing tactics, strategic messaging and sales efforts around sales and customer acquisition.
- It also helps to attract investment from global financiers, who are becoming more interested in responsible gender-inclusive investments.

IV. The ventures should make women visible in order to challenge stereotypes and create market opportunities for their products. The ventures should celebrate women senior managers in their teams and provide them with opportunities to represent the business publicly. Women entrepreneurs and leaders need to be celebrated as role models, and businesses and services should also refrain from negative gender stereotypes and present women and marginalized groups in a dignified way.

3. GENDER ACTION PLAN (GAP)

Table 2 CATALYST Initiative: Gender Action Plan below summarizes the activities identified to address the gender gap for female entrepreneurs and microenterprises. It is organized according to the overall programme’s logical framework up to the level of sub-activities.

The GAP indicators and activities are to be adjusted and reviewed during inception and implementation.

The budget allocations are included in the corresponding sub-activities of the programme and are subject to further review and update by the AE.

A. GENDER STAFFING AND RESPONSIBILITIES

The staffing and implementing arrangements for the gender management are to be integrated in the overall project management structure:

At overall project management level:
• One full-time Gender and Environmental and Social Safeguards Manager will oversee implementation of the GAP and will be responsible for overall GAP monitoring at project management level. This person will be a staff member of GIZ.

At regional level
There will be two regional Gender Specialists who will assist the Gender Manager. In West Africa it will be an in-PED staff member, and in Latin America a GIZ staff member.

Monitoring

Monitoring arrangements will follow the standard GIZ and GCF policies and procedures:
• EEs act as the first line of consolidation of reporting;
• PMU to act as the second line of consolidation across regions and ensure distribution of results;
• GIZ as the AE to ensure supervision and accuracy/timeliness of reporting.

Updating the GAP

The Gender Manager will be responsible for conducting periodic updates of the GAP, as required, in coordination with the regional Gender Specialists.

# ANNEX 1

The proposed checklist covers general gender aspects for climate ventures applying to any of the CATALIST® Initiative regional programmes. It could be included as part of the application form and intends to provide overall guidance only; the checklist will need to be reviewed, complemented, and refined on an ongoing basis during project implementation.
